# of Saint Caillín <note>original: Naoṁ Caillín</note>
### of
## Fenagh <note>original: Fiḋnaċa</note>
# of the Plain of Réin <note>original: Muiġe Réin</note>

## A.D.
## 560 <note>original Roman numerals: DLX</note>

August 9, 1875.
*The Book of Fenagh*, originally compiled by Saint Caillin <note>Saint Caillin was a 6th-century Irish saint and the founder of the monastery at Fenagh, County Leitrim.</note> in the time of Saint Patrick <note>original: *Tempore St Patricii*</note>, revised by W. M. Hennessy <note>William Maunsell Hennessy (1829–1889) was a renowned Irish scholar and translator of ancient manuscripts.</note>, and published by D. H. Kelly in Dublin, 1875.

---

->Wrightly Grange<- <note>Likely referring to the estate of the author in Fuerty; D.H. Kelly was a prominent landowner and scholar in Roscommon.</note>
->Fuerty<-
->Roscommon<-

Dear Sir,

I am greatly pleased to find that *The Book of Fenagh* meets with your approval. To be praised by a man who is himself highly praised <note>original: *Laudari a laudato viro*</note> is no small compliment.

Thank you for your *Leabhar na Féinne* <note>original: *Lebar na Feine*. This likely refers to J. F. Campbell’s 1872 collection of Gaelic heroic ballads, *Leabhar na Féinne*.</note>. I recognize many old friends <note>Kelly is referring to the legendary heroes and familiar myths found within the collection of ballads.</note> in the quick glance I have had at it, and I promise myself both pleasure and instruction as I become more thoroughly acquainted with it.

Is it not strange that we are having such glorious weather while London, Wales, and other places are suffering so much? I hope our sunshine is not the precursor to a storm, but even if it is, it at least secures our hay harvest.

->Very faithfully yours,<-
->D. H. Kelly<-

<summary>A letter from publisher D. H. Kelly to a fellow scholar (likely J. F. Campbell) expressing gratitude for the positive reception of 'The Book of Fenagh' and acknowledging receipt of the recipient's own work on Gaelic ballads.</summary>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page contains a handwritten note dated August 9, 1875, describing the "Book of Fenagh." It serves as a bibliographic and historical summary of an ancient Irish manuscript that had been recently revised and translated at the time of writing.</meta>

August 9, 1875.

The *Book of Fenagh*, originally compiled by Saint Caillin in the time of Saint Patrick <note>original: "Tempore St Patricii"</note>, was revised by W. M. Hennessy. <gloss>Translated into</gloss> English by D. H. Kelly. Dublin, 1875 —

---

At page 9 <note>original: "IX"</note> of the Introduction, the Editor ~~fairly~~ expresses the opinion that the book is a rental <note>A record of the rents, lands, and rights belonging to a person or institution.</note> of Saint Caillin's royally endowed Abbey of Fenagh. It consists of poems, epic recitations <term>rhapsodies</term>, and legendary historical accounts of the origin and extent of these endowments. These pieces were subsequently put together with a prose narrative, and in that form, its remains have descended to our times. The book is said to be an unquestionable specimen of very ancient Gaelic literature. The date on the cover is 560 AD.

London W

<summary>This handwritten note provides a historical overview of the "Book of Fenagh," identifying it as a 6th-century record of the Abbey of Fenagh's property and legendary history.</summary>
<keywords>Book of Fenagh, Saint Caillin, Saint Patrick, W. M. Hennessy, D. H. Kelly, Abbey of Fenagh, Gaelic literature, Irish manuscripts, 19th-century scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page follows a previously illegible section of the manuscript. The author has now transitioned into a clear discussion regarding the dating and scholarly analysis of ancient Irish manuscripts, specifically citing editorial work from 1875.</meta>

Looking at the very fairly stated introduction, it appears that Mr. Hennessy <note>William Maunsell Hennessy (1829–1889), a noted Irish scholar and editor of ancient texts.</note> and the Editor did their best in 1875 to expand the abbreviations <note>original: "contractions"; referring to the shorthand used by medieval scribes</note> in manuscripts which they suppose to be as old as 1516. The compilation of the subjects contained in these writings was attributed by O'Curry <note>Eugene O'Curry (1794–1862), a foundational figure in the study of Irish manuscript materials.</note> to the year 1430. Judging by internal evidence, the current Editor dates the compilation to about 1300.

However, Saint Patrick's mission to Ireland is dated to the year 432. The material printed is therefore the best that skilled Irish scholars can now determine <insert>out</insert> of writings roughly 350 years old. These do not claim to be direct copies of the book which the Irish saint is supposed to have compiled 1,315 years ago, but are instead believed to be copies of later compilations.

->London W<-

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues a critical evaluation of the "Book of Fenagh," specifically discussing its chronological origins and its function within the community as a ritual object.</meta>

...made nine hundred or <insert>a thousand</insert> years after the date of Saint Patrick's mission (432 AD). 

The antiquity of the written contents must be judged by the language and its meaning—by the story and the way it is told. The English translation <insert>now</insert> enables any reader to judge the *Book of Fenagh*.

My opinion is that it was a rent roll <term>a record of rents or dues owed to a church or landlord</term>, <insert>intended to be learned by heart</insert>, and that it is a rigmarole <note>original: "rigmarole"; here implying a rambling or incoherent collection of stories</note>. The <insert>Priest</insert> used to lend it out to be sworn upon, as a relic of sanctity. It served as a charm and a mystery to those who could not read <insert>old vellum <term>prepared animal skin used for writing</term> with its archaic letters and contractions</insert>. To those who can [read it]...

---

->London W<-

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the validity of ancient manuscripts, specifically comparing the "Book of Fenagh" to a fraudulent magical charm. The previous page was noted as illegible, but this page picks up with a clear anecdotal argument.</meta>

Once read, it is now like that famous charm against weak eyes of which we have read. A witch was brought before a judge with this "charm" kept inside a ball of wool <term>original: worsted; a type of fine, smooth wool yarn</term>. She was guilty of curing many <gloss>weak eyes</gloss> with that charm. 

But the judge had once been a student. By his order, the ball of wool was unwound, and at the heart of it was found a scrap of paper with these words written in the judge's own hand:

> "The Devil scratch out both your eyes
> "And shit into the holes likewise.

The antiquity and the sanctity of this "Book of Fenagh" <note>The *Book of Fenagh* is a 16th-century Irish manuscript regarding Saint Caillín of Fenagh; the author is implying that the book's reputation for holiness is as hollow as the witch's crude charm</note> are about equal to the power of that charm once the mystery of the wool was unwound.

---

->London W<-

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the previously illegible section to a clear appraisal of the scholarly work performed by William Maunsell Hennessy and Denis Henry Kelly in their 1875 translation of the *Book of Fenagh*.</meta>

All lovers of truth and all who admire honest work well done must admire the work performed by Mr. Hennessy and Mr. Kelly. They have untangled the knot <note>original: "unravelled the worsted"; a metaphor using wool thread to describe solving a complex problem</note>, and here is an old Irish manuscript unraveled and translated into English. It is the Rig Veda <note>The oldest of the sacred Hindu scriptures, used here as a comparison for the antiquity and complexity of the Irish text.</note> all over again. It is another old rigmarole <term>rigmarole: a long, rambling, and often confused story or procedure</term> unraveled—another "sacred mystery" laid bare to the eyes of common sense. It is a very curious and very interesting study.

->London W<-

<term>Arabic script</term>: The writing system used for the Arabic language.
<term>bleed-through</term>: Ink from the opposite side of a page that is visible through the paper, often making the text on both sides difficult to read.

# DEDICATION

### TO THE

## LADY LOUISA TENISON <note>original: "Tennisson"</note>.

---

MY DEAR LADY LOUISA,

I consider myself especially fortunate to be allowed to publish my volume under the patronage of that rare person: a fashionable English woman who does not look down on taking an interest in the history and antiquities of the land of her adoption <note>Lady Louisa Tenison (1819–1882) was an English traveler and artist who moved to Ireland after her marriage to Edward King-Tenison</note>; and who—an even rarer circumstance—has made herself familiar with our ancient Celtic language.

This language, as a spoken tongue, is now rapidly dying out. However, it is fortunate that in recent years, especially in Germany, it has captured the attention of the most capable philologists <term>scholars who study the history and structure of languages</term> of our day.

Much has been done to rescue its ancient records (perhaps the oldest written records in Europe) from "manuscript oblivion" <note>the state of being forgotten or lost within unpublished handwritten documents</note>; but very much still remains to be done. It is highly desirable to complete this work while the spoken tongue still exists to correct the theories of those who only study it as a dead language. Therefore, I have taken it upon myself to rescue one such ancient volume from the obscurity of manuscripts and present it here in print. This edition includes a faithful translation into English, with the ancient and often inconsistent shorthand <term>original: "arbitrary contractions"</term> carefully decoded, and with a text diligently compared <term>original: "sedulously collated"</term> with all surviving copies <term>original: "exemplars"</term> to ensure it is as pure and perfect as possible.

<vocab>Lady Louisa Tenison, Celtic language, philologists, manuscript oblivion, translation, copies</vocab>

<summary>In this dedication, the author thanks Lady Louisa Tenison for her patronage and her rare interest in the Irish language. The author explains the urgent need to preserve and translate ancient Celtic manuscripts while native speakers are still available to assist in the process.</summary>

<keywords>Lady Louisa Tenison, Celtic language, Irish antiquities, philology, manuscript preservation, translation, historical linguistics</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Lady Louisa Tenison, moving from the preservation of the Irish language to the specific historical content and geographical focus of the volume.</meta>

<page-num>iv</page-num>

This ancient book contains the history and traditions of the Two Breifneys <note>original: "Breifneys"; refers to the historical territories of the O'Rourke and O'Reilly clans, roughly corresponding to modern counties Leitrim and Cavan</note> dating back to the fifth century. These records are accompanied by plentiful explanatory notes by my friend Mr. W. M. Hennessy <note>William Maunsell Hennessy (1829–1889), a prominent Irish scholar and translator of ancient texts</note>, who has successfully identified many locations that were previously unknown. I hope to present this work to your Ladyship in a form that—considering it is the voluntary effort of a simple country gentleman—might be accepted without too much harsh criticism. 

It is true that some of its legends are wild and imaginative, but they are not at all more absurd than those of ancient Greece or Rome. Mixed in with these stories is a great deal of genuine history, as well as much that may provide valuable information for the topographer <term>a person who describes and maps the physical features of a specific region</term> and the philologist <term>a scholar who studies the history of languages and written records</term>. 

Finally, I thank you for your interest in our national history and for your kind patronage. I am also grateful for the photographs which were used to create the illustrations in this volume.

->I remain,<-
->Your Ladyship’s very grateful and humble servant,<-
->D. H. KELLY.<-

<summary>In this conclusion to the dedication, D. H. Kelly describes the book's contents regarding the history of Leitrim and Cavan and acknowledges the scholarly contributions of W. M. Hennessy.</summary>
<keywords>Breifney, Leitrim, Cavan, W. M. Hennessy, D. H. Kelly, Irish history, topography, philology, Lady Louisa Tenison</keywords>

# INTRODUCTION.

The text of the following edition of the *Old Book of Caillin* <note>original: "Old Book of Caillin"; also known as the Book of Fenagh, a medieval Irish manuscript detailing the life and miracles of Saint Caillin, the patron of Fenagh, County Leitrim</note>, as the *Book of Fenagh* was anciently called, has been taken, as far as possible, from a very fine transcript (¹) most accurately made by the—

->*Illustrations.*<-

1. THE OLD CHURCH OF FENAGH . . . . *Opposite the Title page.*
2. THE CROMLECH <term>a prehistoric monument consisting of a large flat stone resting on upright ones; often a burial site</term> AT FENAGH (supposed to be the burial place of Conall Gulban <note>Conall Gulban (died c. 464) was an Irish king and the ancestor of the Cenél Conaill dynasty, from which the O'Donnells descend</note>) . . . . *Opposite p. 91.*

---

See below <note>original: "infra"</note>, p. 291, where he says that it was “through meter and ecstasy—

(²) This manuscript, Cottonian Vespasian E. VI. <note>original: "Cott. Vesp. E. VI."; a reference to the Cotton collection held in the British Library</note>, is indicated as MS. B. in the following pages.
(³) The late Dr. Todd <note>James Henthorn Todd (1805–1869), a notable Irish scholar and historian</note> thought so. “The manuscript of the *Book of Fenagh*,” he wrote,

<column-break/>

“by Maurice O’Mulconry <note>Muirgheas Ó Maolconaire, a member of a famous family of hereditary Irish historians and scribes</note>, written in 1517, from which Mr. O’Donovan made his transcript, is in private hands. But the more ancient manuscript from which Maurice O’Mulconry’s copy was taken, is in the British Museum.”—*Irish Archaeological Miscellany*, Vol. I., p. 113, note f.

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the manuscript's history, specifically focusing on the transition from poetry to prose and the reliability of O’Mulconry’s 1517 transcript compared to fragments in the British Museum.</meta>

Caillin spoke the things mentioned above to Manchan, *even though we have written them in prose*.” Further on we are told, “that it was this Tadhg (O’Roddy) <note>Tadhg O'Roddy (fl. 1700) was an Irish scholar and the last traditional head of the O'Roddy family, who were hereditary coarbs (guardians) of Fenagh</note> who directed Maurice O’Mulconry to put this book here into a narrative form . . . because there was only poetry in the original 'Old Book' (p. 311);” and again the copyist adds, “that the book was written only in meter until now; and from this point forward, it consists of both stories and poems (p. 393).”

Therefore, if O’Mulconry’s statements are trustworthy (and there seems to be no reason to doubt their accuracy), it is clear that the British Museum fragment cannot *be part of the original Old Book of Saint Caillin*. Instead, it seems to be a transcript made from O’Mulconry’s copy.

<margin>
Internal evidence
1300
1430
</margin>
The content of some of the leaves <term>original: "folios"; the individual sheets of a manuscript</term> missing from O’Mulconry's transcript are also missing in the British Museum fragment. However, that fragment does provide(¹) the text of the lost leaves 11 and 29 of O’Mulconry’s copy; but unfortunately, even by comparing both, the full text of the original Old Book cannot be recovered. As for the Old Book itself, it is now impossible to determine either the actual date it was written or what finally happened to it. But judging by its content, it must have been compiled around or before A.D. 1300. Its transcriber, O’Mulconry, explains that one of the reasons he copied it was because “the vellum <term>high-quality parchment made from animal skin</term> on which Caillin’s Old Book was previously written had become old and decayed (p. 393).” Professor O’Curry <note>Eugene O'Curry (1794–1862), a foundational scholar of Irish history and medieval manuscripts</note> believed that a portion of the work was composed around the year 1430(²); but he was led into this error by misidentifying(³) the identity of—

(³) Professor O’Curry’s error is more understandable given that Tadhg O’Roddy,

<column-break/>

who should have known the contents of the manuscript, says of this William Gorm, “whoever he was, I do not know” <note>original: "ce be he nescio"; a blend of Middle Irish and Latin</note>. See p. 72, note 5.

<summary>This page discusses the textual history of the Book of Fenagh, explaining that Maurice O’Mulconry converted the original verse-only "Old Book" into a mix of prose and poetry in 1517. It also addresses chronological errors made by earlier scholars regarding the manuscript's origins.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Caillin, Saint Manchan, Tadhg O'Roddy, Maurice O'Mulconry, British Museum, Book of Fenagh, manuscript tradition, vellum, Professor O'Curry, William Gorm</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the authorship of the Old Book of Fenagh, examining evidence that suggests it was a collaborative work rather than written by St. Caillin himself, and addressing later additions by the O'Roddy family.</meta>

the person called “William Gorm,” or William de Lacy <note>original: "Lasci"</note>, referred to on pages 73 and 77 below <note>original: "infra"</note>.

It could not, however, have been written by St. Caillin; for if it had been, O’Mulconry would surely have said so. It seems instead to have been a work composed by more than one person. In one place, the copyist complains “that *the Old Book of Fenagh had tired him, for it was a cleric who wrote the original model <note>original: "exemplar"</note> of this part of it.*” ($^1$) If the writer in question had been St. Caillin, the complaint would scarcely have been uttered in the presence of Tadhg O’Roddy, St. Caillin’s successor. It was O'Roddy who, through his great devotion to Caillin ($^2$), had caused the Book to be put “in a narrative form.”

We cannot say what other liberties O’Roddy and his scribe may have taken with the original Book, besides inserting the “prose summaries.” He likely had these written to enhance the value of the work, or perhaps to explicitly state the extent and nature of the taxes and dues to which he was entitled as St. Caillin’s successor at Fenagh. However, his learned descendant, Tadhg O’Roddy ($^3$), who added several marginal notes to the O’Mulconry copy, innocently <note>original: "naively"</note> reproves “the ignorant people” who were constantly saying “that Tadhg O’Roddy put many lies into this Book of Caillin, which was written in the year <margin>1516</margin> of Christ’s age, 1516.” ($^4$)

Much could be said of the O’Roddy family, the hereditary <term>comharbs</term> <note>Successors to the founder of a monastery; guardians of church lands and relics</note> of Fenagh and successors of St. Caillin, who preserved the Old Book of Fenagh along with the shrine and the bell of the saint.

---

($^1$) See <note>original: "Vide"</note> p. 330, n. 8.
($^2$) See p. 311.
($^3$) For an account of this remarkable man, see Dr. Todd’s paper in the

<column-break/>

*Irish Archaeological Miscellany*, Vol. I.,
p. 112, and following <note>original: "et seq."</note>.
($^4$) See p. 170, n. 2, below.

<summary>This page argues that the Old Book of Fenagh was not written by St. Caillin himself but was a multi-author work later adapted into prose by the O'Roddy family to record their hereditary rights and dues. It also addresses historical accusations of O'Roddy "falsifying" parts of the text in the 16th century.</summary>

<keywords>St. Caillin, Book of Fenagh, Tadhg O’Roddy, O’Mulconry, William de Lacy, comharb, Irish Church history, hagiography</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of the O’Roddy family's role as the hereditary successors of St. Caillin and the official guardians of the Old Book of Fenagh, focusing on their lineage and the eventual dispersal of their famous manuscript collection.</meta>

written. It is stated in the present work that the “Abbotship or Herenachship <term>herenach</term> <note>A hereditary steward of church lands in the Irish tradition</note> of Fenagh” were always filled from one branch of the family called “the sons of the cleric” (¹) <note>original: "clerech"</note>. This cleric, whose name was Alexander, was the grandson of Rodachæ, from whom the name of O’Rodachæ or O’Roddy has been derived, and must have lived about A.D. 800, since Tadhg O’Roddy, who commissioned the transcription of the work in 1516, was the 25th in descent from him. There is no reason to doubt the statement quoted above, that the succession to the Abbacy of Fenagh was hereditary (²) in his family. The O’Roddys seem to have been distinguished for their great zeal in the promotion of Irish literature. Their generous hospitality has often been the subject of praise by Irish poets.

The last learned representative of the family, Tadhg O’Roddy of Crossfield, near Fenagh (whose annotations on the O’Mulconry manuscript are reproduced in the present work), states in his letter to Edward Llwyd, written about A.D. 1690, that he had “as many Irish books of philosophy, medicine <note>original: "physic"</note>, poetry, genealogies, mathematics, invasions <note>A reference to the *Lebor Gabála Érenn*, or Book of Invasions, a collection of mythical and historical narratives of Ireland</note>, law, romances, etc., and as ancient as any in Ireland.” (³) Where are they all now? It is only surprising that the O’Mulconry copy has not shared their fate. Its preservation is doubtless owing to the veneration attached to it as a relic of the founder of Fenagh.

<margin>1516</margin>
The last of the O’Roddys to whom the custody of this ancient Book descended was the parish priest of Kilronan, a man of sadly intemperate habits. He used to supplement <note>original: "eke out"</note> his slender income by sending out the Holy Book for a small fee <note>original: "gratuity"</note> to be used by any who—

(³) *Irish Archaeological Miscellany* <note>original: "Ir. Arch. Miscel."</note>, p. 122.

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of the "Old Book of Fenagh," describing its use as a sacred object for oaths and its movement through various custodians in the 19th century before being formally transcribed.</meta>

wished, by swearing upon it, to clear themselves from any accusation <note>original: "imputation"</note>, or to make any agreement especially binding by having it witnessed upon the book. The local peasantry looked upon such an oath or attestation as something incredibly serious, believing that its violation would surely bring terrible retribution to those who broke it. Unfortunately, the manuscript was kept very carelessly by its reverend custodian; and because it consisted of loose pages <term>folios</term> without a binding, several of them have been lost.

At his death, the manuscript passed—along with his other small possessions—to his successor, the Rev. Mr. Fitzgerald, who saw that he was respectably buried. I, the translator and editor, am indebted to Rev. Fitzgerald’s courtesy for being permitted to see and examine the book. It was brought to his residence at Kilronan for that purpose by my excellent friend Thomas M'Dermott Roe. I was visiting him at Alderford in 1844, the year before the terrible Great Famine (1845), when that excellent specimen of a true Irish chief died of a fever. He caught the illness while making benevolent efforts to relieve his suffering countrymen.

The Rev. Mr. Fitzgerald also kept these precious pages loose in a drawer along with his clothes; however, I believe that none of them were lost while in his care. From him, they passed into the possession of the late Rev. Mr. Slevin, the Parish Priest of Gortlitteragh, County Leitrim. He kindly permitted my friend Mr. W. M. Hennessy to compare <term>collate</term> the original with the translator’s transcript of Dr. John O'Donovan's copy (which is held in the Royal Irish Academy). This allowed him to certify the transcript's exact correctness.

<margin>1516</margin>
In fact, this undeniably ancient volume of Irish literature is, in the editor's opinion, a **RENTAL**—a record of the rents, tributes, privileges, and legal protections <note>original: "immunities"</note> of St. Caillin's royally-endowed Abbey of Fenagh in County Leitrim. It consists of poems and rhapsodies...

and legendary historical accounts of their origin and extent; these were subsequently combined with a prose narrative, the surviving portions <note>original: "remains"</note> of which have descended to our times. It is, however, an unquestionable <note>original: "indubitable"</note> specimen of very ancient Gaelic <note>original: "Gælic"</note> literature, and neither trouble nor expense has been spared to present it to the public in as complete a state as possible. 

In this effort, the Editor has had the cordial assistance of Mr. W. M. Hennessy, who accompanied him to Fenagh to personally inspect the locality. Mr. Hennessy also kindly traveled to London to search the Irish manuscripts in the British Museum, and then went to Oxford to examine the Irish collections there. Here, the Editor must express his great gratitude <note>original: "obligations"</note> to Mr. Hennessy for revising his manuscript and correcting its errors, for his valuable annotations, and for his inestimable aid in guiding the volume through the printing process. Indeed, if any credit is given to this work, it is due more to him than to the Editor—who is a mere country gentleman who, out of love for the ancient literature of his native land, has devoted himself to its production for nearly thirty years.

As to what portion of the *Old Book of St. Caillin* <note>St. Caillin (6th century) was the founder of the monastery at Fenagh, and the book is traditionally attributed to him</note>, if any, might be held in the inaccessible library of Lord Ashburnham, we can only refer to Dr. O'Conor's *Catalogue of the Stowe Library* <note>The Stowe manuscripts were a famous collection of Irish documents; Lord Ashburnham’s refusal to allow scholars access to them was a major frustration for 19th-century historians</note>.

The Editor must also express his gratitude to the Very Reverend the Dean of Armagh, Messrs. O'Loony and O'Longan, and other valued friends who have aided him in identifying historical locations <note>original: "fixing localities"</note>; and to Miss Stokes for kindly designing the initial letter.

Jesus Christ, Son of the living God, Lord of all creation, second person of the Trinity <note>original Irish: "diadachtu" — divinity</note>, mediator between the people of heaven and earth, Savior of the human race. It is he who spoke these words to show the great goodness which God bestows on his saints and on his righteous ones, and also to the company who give great love to him in the church here below.

John, moreover, son of Zebedee, <term>heir of virginity</term> <note>original: "comarba na hoği" — a title for John the Apostle, referring to his traditional role as a successor to Christ in caring for the Virgin Mary, or to his own celibacy</note>, the twelfth apostle whom Jesus chose, the man who wrote the divine Gospel, he who drew the fountain of true wisdom from the breast of the Savior; it is he who wrote these words and left them in memory with the church until the end of the world; wherefore he says here: "I will give to him who thirsts of the fountain of the water of life freely" <note>original Latin: "Ego sitienti dabo de fonte aquae uivae gratis"</note>. To him who desires the truth, there shall be given freely from the fountain of living water. The connection of these words, however, is with the passage where Jesus said before: "I am Alpha and Omega, the beginning and the end" <note>original Latin: "Ego sum alpha et omega, PRINCIPIUM ET FINIS"</note>; that is, I am the beginning of all creation, and I am its completion. So it is following those words—

I will give to him who thirsts from the fountain of the water of life <note>original Latin: "EGO sitienti dabo de fonte aquæ vivæ"</note> freely; that is, he who thirsts for the Truth, to him will I give from the Well of the Water of Life, freely.^1

He who overcomes shall possess <note>original Latin: "possidebit"</note> these things;^2 that is, he that conquers, to him shall these things be given.

And I will be his God <note>original Latin: "Et ero illi Deus"</note>.

And he shall be a son to me <note>original Latin: "Et erit ille mihi filius"</note>.

Jesus Christ, Son of the Living God, Lord of all created things, the Second Person of the Godhead, the mediator for the people of Heaven and Earth, the Savior of the human race—it is he who spoke these words to proclaim the great benefits which he bestows upon his saints and righteous ones, and upon those who show him great love in the Church on Earth.

John, moreover, the son of Zebedee, the <term>Comarb</term>^3 of the Virgin, the twelfth Apostle whom Jesus chose; the man who wrote the Divine Gospel; the person who established the well of true knowledge^4 from the bosom of the Savior—it is he who wrote these words and left them as a memorial with the church until the end of the world. He said here, “I will give to him who thirsts from the fountain of the water of life freely” <note>original Latin: "EGO sitienti dabo de fonte aquæ vivæ gratis"</note>; that is, “Whosoever thirsts for the truth, to him will I give from the Well of the Water of Life freely.” These words, as used by John, are a completion of what Jesus said before: “I AM THE ALPHA AND THE OMEGA, THE BEGINNING^5 AND THE END” <note>original Latin: "EGO SUM ALPHA ET OMEGA, PRINCIPIUM ET FINIS"</note>, meaning: I am the beginning of all things; I am their end. And it is in following those words that John weaves together—

<meta>This page continues the genealogy of Saint Caillín, tracing his lineage back to Rudraige. It then begins the narrative of his early life, education under the legendary Finntan, and his eventual journey to Rome before the arrival of Saint Patrick.</meta>

| | |
|----------|----------|
| Son of Cethguine Chalupach | Son of Conmac |
| Son of Mochta | Son of Fergus |
| Son of Meroman | Son of Ros |
| Son of Moghad Taet | Son of Rudraige. |

It is then, moreover, that the festival and celebration of Saint Caillín are held, in the month of November; on the second day <note>Saint Martin’s Day is November 11th; Saint Caillín’s festival is observed on the 13th.</note> after the feast of Saint Martin.

Finntan, the son of Labraid, son of Bith, son of Lamech, who is called the "son of Bochna" <note>original Latin: "qui dicitur mac Bochna"</note> (for Bochna was his mother’s name <note>original Latin: "ar Bochna nomen matris eius"</note>). This Finntan was the chief elder of Ireland. It was by him that Saint Caillín was reared and educated until he was fully one hundred years old. For this reason, Finntan commanded him to go to Rome to study wisdom and knowledge, so that his wisdom and knowledge might later be a precious gem and a key to unlock the ignorance and lack of knowledge of the men of Ireland.

Caillín lived for two hundred years more, until Patrick, son of Calpurnius, came to sow the seeds of the Gospel and the faith, and to banish idols and sorcery <note>original: "appacht"</note> from Ireland.

It was in the time of Laoghaire, son of Niall, that Patrick came into Ireland. Twelve years after Patrick’s arrival, Caillín returned to Ireland. Many other saints also followed the path of learning and the course of study that Saint Caillín undertook. Truly, there was a long duration between the various <term>holy orders</term> <note>religious ranks or degrees within the church</note> and the steps that Saint Caillín took. During the time of Cormac mac Airt, he received his clerical tonsure <note>original: "grada coronta," literally "crowned orders," referring to the shaving of the head as a sign of religious devotion</note>...

---

...Breac (p. 16), but it does not carry the line beyond the fifth generation, Cecht, or MacCecht.

¹ **Second day.** — The 11th of November is Saint Martin’s day; and Saint Caillín’s festival is commemorated on the 13th of the same month.

² **Finntan.** — This is the legendary character who is said to have survived the Deluge in Ireland, and to have shared the history of Ireland’s settlements from the Flood until the time of Saint Finnian of Moville (around 560 AD), under the name Tuan Mac Cairill. In ancient Irish legends, four people are represented as having been saved from the Flood, besides the inhabitants of the Ark: namely, Fors in the East, Farran in the North, Finntan in the West, and Annoid in the South. The historian Keating thought this fable (which he states he could not find in “any chief book of authority”) was worthy of refutation. However, it is strange that he was not acquainted with the account of these four individuals contained in the *Lebor na hUidri* <note>The Book of the Dun Cow, one of the oldest and most important Irish manuscripts.</note>.

It also establishes the timeline of his life, claiming he lived for hundreds of years and returned to Ireland shortly after Saint Patrick's arrival.</summary>

Moreover, the time when the festival and solemnity of Saint Caillín is celebrated is specifically in the month of November, on the second day¹ after the festival of Saint Martin <note>Saint Martin’s Day is November 11th; Saint Caillín’s festival is observed on the 13th.</note>.

Finntan²—son of Labraid, son of Bith, son of Lamech, who is called "Son of Bochna" (for Bochna was his mother's name)—this Finntan was, indeed, the chief elder of Ireland. It was by him that Saint Caillín was nursed and fostered until his hundredth year was completed.

It was then that Finntan commanded him to go to Rome to learn wisdom and knowledge, so that his wisdom and knowledge might afterwards be a precious gem to the men of Ireland, and a key for unlocking their ignorance and lack of knowledge.

Caillín remained in Rome for two hundred years, until Patrick, son of Calpurnius, came to sow piety and faith, and to banish idols and images from Ireland.

Patrick came to Ireland during the time of Laoghaire Mac Neill. Twelve years after Patrick's arrival, Caillín returned to Ireland. Few other saints performed the study³ and labor that Saint Caillín performed. For much time passed between the various ecclesiastical ranks and degrees that Saint Caillín received. During the time of Cormac Mac Airt⁴, he received the order of Tonsure <note>The ceremonial clipping of hair to signify admission into the clergy</note>.

This chronology of Saint Caillín’s life is a clear example of the exaggeration of Irish scribes, who sought to exalt the virtues and merits of their favorite saints by attributing to them superhuman lifespans. ...at the altar of Peter in Rome, Italy <note>original: *i Roim leṫai*. Letha was the Irish name for Latium or Italy.</note>. Fourteen kings ruled Ireland during that period, including their successions:

**Cormac ua Cuinn** <note>Cormac mac Airt, a legendary High King</note>: ruled forty years until he died in the house of Cletty.

**Eochaidh Gunnat**: ruled one year until he was slain by Cormac <margin>2</margin>.

**Cairbre Lifeachair**, son of Cormac: ruled seventeen years (or perhaps twenty-seven), until he fell in the battle of Gabhair-Liffe <margin>3</margin>. During his reign, Caillín received the rank of **archdeacon** <note>original: *ruibdechain*</note>.

**The Fothads**: ruled one year, until Fothad the Charioteer <margin>4</margin> was slain by Fothad the Plunderer <margin>5</margin>. Fothad the Plunderer died in Moylinny <margin>6</margin>, at the battle of Ollarba, at the hands of the Fenians of Finn, grandson of Baiscne <note>Finn Mac Cumhaill's legendary band of warriors</note>.

**Muiredach Tirech**: ruled thirty years, until he was slain by Caelbad, King of Ulster and son of Crunn badrui, over the river Dabhall.

**Caelbad**: ruled one year until he was slain by Eochaidh Muighmheadhoin.

**Eochaidh Muighmheadhoin**: ruled eight years until he died of a disease at Tara.

**Crimthann mac Fidaig**: ruled seventeen years, until he died from a poisoned drink administered by—

---

These extravagant statements <note>referring to the saint's improbable lifespan</note> found no place in the original *Life of Saint Caillín*.

<margin>1</margin> *Letha.*—Latium. The name *Letha* was also applied by Irish writers to Armorica, or Brittany. However, the Latinized form of the name in this case is *Letavia*. Dr. O’Donovan (*Tribes and Customs of Hy Fiachrach*, p. 412) seems to support the statements of Patrick Lynch and Lanigan that the name *Letha* was never applied to Latium. But he was certainly wrong, as the evidence which he himself presents (in the place cited) conclusively shows. See also O’Curry’s *Lectures*, appendix, p. 502.

<margin>2</margin> *Cormac.*—This is an error. Eochaidh Gunnat, who is not counted as a king of Ireland by Tigernach—although he is

<column-break/>

called so in other Annals—was slain by “Lugaidh Menn, son of Aengus, of the men of Ulster.” (*Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 267.)

<margin>3</margin> *Gabhair-Lifè.*—“Gabhair of the Liffey.” There were many places in Ireland called Gabhair (genitive: *Gabhra*). The place referred to here seems to have been in *Lifè*, a plain in County Kildare through which the River Liffey flows. It was probably the old name of the hilly country near Ballymore-Eustace, from Bishop Hill to Brittas.

<margin>4</margin> *Fothad Cairpthech.*—“Fothad the Charioteer.”

<margin>5</margin> *Airgthech.*—that is, “the Plunderer.”

<margin>6</margin> *Magh-Linè*, or Moylinny, was the—

...at the altar of Peter in Rome, Italy <note>original: *Letha*. An ancient Irish name for Latium or Italy.</note>. During the reign of Laoghaire mac Neill <note>The High King contemporary with Saint Patrick</note>, he received the rank of bishop. Fourteen kings governed Ireland during that time, including those two <note>referring to the spanning period</note>, namely:

**Cormac, grandson of Conn** <note>Cormac mac Airt</note>: ruled 40 years, until he died in the house of Cletty.

**Eochaidh Gunnat**: ruled one year, until he was slain by Cormac <margin>2</margin>.

**Cairbre Lifeachair**, son of Cormac: ruled 17 years, or 27, until he was slain in the battle of Gabhair-Liffe <margin>3</margin>. During his time, Caillín received the rank of sub-deacon.

**The Fothads**: ruled one year, until Fothad the Charioteer <margin>4</margin> fell by the hand of Fothad the Plunderer. Fothad the Plunderer <margin>5</margin>, moreover, fell in Moylinny <margin>6</margin>, in the battle of Ollarba, at the hands of the soldiers of Finn, grandson of Baiscne <margin>7</margin>.

**Fiacha Sroiptine**: ruled 31, or 30 years, until he was slain by the three Collas, specifically in the battle of Dubh-Comar <margin>8</margin>. In the time of this Fiacha, Caillín took upon himself the rank of deacon.

**Muiredach Tirech**: ruled 30 years, until he was slain by Caelbad, King of Ulster and son of Crunn Badrai, over the river Dabhall.

**Caelbad** reigned one year, until he fell by the hand of Eochaidh Muighmheadhoin <margin>9</margin>.

**Eochaidh Muighmheadhoin** <margin>10</margin> ruled 7 years, until he died of illness in Tara.

**Crimthann**, son of Fidach: ruled 16 years, until he died of the poisoned drink given to him by Mongfind <margin>11</margin>, daughter of Fidach. In the year after the death of—

---

<margin>6</margin> Moylinny: The ancient name of a plain nearly equal in size to the present barony of Upper Antrim, County Antrim. See Reeves’ *Down and Connor*, p. 62.

<margin>7</margin> Finn, grandson of Baiscne: Also known as Finn Mac Cumhaill.

<margin>8</margin> Dubh-Comar: Literally the "black confluence." This is the junction of the Blackwater and Boyne rivers, near Navan, County Meath.

<margin>9</margin> Muighmheadhoin: Spelled *Munbeðan* in Manuscript A.

<margin>10</margin> Muighmheadhoin: Spelled *Munbeðh* in Manuscript A.

<margin>11</margin> Mongfind: Literally "Fair-hair." 

<column-break/>

This woman, who is a famous character in Irish legend, was the wife of Eochaidh Muighmheadhoin and the sister of Crimthann, son of Fidach, King of Ireland (who died <term>378 AD</term> according to the *Annals of the Four Masters*). She is said to have died from tasting—to encourage her victim—the poisoned drink which she gave to her brother Crimthann, whom she wished to replace in the kingship with her own son Brian, the ancestor of the Ui Briuin families of Connacht. She was regarded as a great sorceress; and in a text in the *Book of Ballymote*, All Hallows' Eve is stated to have been called *Feil Moing*, or "Mong’s Festival," by the common people.

<margin>fol. 2, a.</margin> Niall of the Nine Hostages, son of Eochaidh Muighmheadhoin, ruled for twenty-seven years until he died from a wound inflicted by Eochaidh, son of Enna Cennselach, at the Sea of Icht <note>original: *Muir Icht*. The English Channel, named after the *Portus Iccius* used by Julius Caesar</note>, while he was invading the kingdom of Armorica <note>original: *Leatha*. Laoghaire, son of Niall, was King of Ireland when Caillín received the rank of bishop. After arriving in Ireland, he received the rank of archbishop from the hand of Saint Patrick, son of Calpurnius—the high apostle of Western Europe.

Patrick granted Caillín a "noble tribute" <note>original: *soerchuairt*. A traditional right to visit and receive hospitality or taxes</note> from every man of art and craft in Ireland. Afterward, Patrick granted him the high legateship of the island of Ireland over all the other saints of Ireland, because he was the oldest of them; and he remained in the legateship of Ireland for a hundred years after that.

Finntan gave three hundred ounces of solid red gold to Saint Caillín when he first sent him to Rome to study. Caillín, in turn, brought many remains and relics with him from that journey when returning from Rome, to increase the honor, reverence, and protection of his city and his beloved church, Fenagh of Moy-Rein <note>original: *Fidnacha Moighe Rein*. <note>original: *Letha*. See previous note regarding Latium and Armorica.</note>

<note>original: *Muir-Icht*. The "Sea of Icht," supposed to have taken its name from the *Portus Iccius* of Caesar. Irish writers use the term to refer to the British Channel. <note>original: *Letha*. This refers to Letavia, or Armorica, as distinguished from the other *Letha* (Latium/Italy). If this genealogy is correct, Caillín likely lived in the latter half of the 6th century. [During the reign of] Eochaidh Muighmheadhoin, Caillín was ministering at the altar of the priests in Rome of Letha <note>original: *Letha*. A term in Old Irish that could refer to Latium (Italy) or Armorica (Brittany). In the context of Rome, it refers to Italy.</note>.<sup>1</sup>

Niall of the Nine Hostages <note>original: *Niall Noígiallach*</note>, son of Eochaidh Muighmheadhoin, ruled for 26 years, until he died from a wound inflicted by Eochaidh, son of Enna Cennselach, at the Sea of Icht <note>original: *Muir-Icht*. It was during his time that Caillín, son of Niata, became a monk.

Laoghaire Mac Neill was King of Ireland when Caillín received the rank of bishop. After he came to Ireland, it was there he received the rank of archbishop from the hand of Saint Patrick, son of Calpurnius—that is, from the chief apostle of Western Europe.

Patrick gave the tribute of every man of learning in Ireland to Caillín; and Patrick cursed every one of them who would not obey Caillín's successors and his beautiful church, namely Fenagh <note>original: *Fidhnacha*</note>. Patrick afterward gave him the high legateship of the island of Ireland in the presence of all the saints of Ireland, for he was the oldest<sup>5</sup> of them; and he remained in the legateship of Ireland for 100 years after that.

Finntan gave three hundred ounces of solid red gold to Saint Caillín when he first sent him to Rome to study.<sup>6</sup> Caillín also brought many remains and relics<sup>7</sup> with him on that occasion when returning from Rome; this was to increase, moreover, the honor, respect, and right of protection of his See and beautiful church, namely Fenagh of Magh-Rein.

<meta>This page continues the account of Saint Caillín’s holy relics and his deathbed instructions to Saint Manchán regarding his burial and the status of his church at Fenagh.</meta>

[The relics of the] twelve apostles, the relics of Martin, the relics of Lawrence, and Stephen the martyr. The object that served as a protection and a treasury for those relics was a cloth <note>original: *bret*. This word now signifies frieze, or coarse woolen material; but formerly it meant a veil or linen cloth. In the lists of relics mentioned in the Lives of Irish Saints, there is none more curious than this "dribble-cloth."</note> which Mary the Virgin made with her own hands. It was this cloth that was around Jesus while she was nursing him in his infancy.

These are the relics that he ordered <note>original: *fulair*. To command or order.</note> to be enshrined afterward, and a shrine to be closed around them. And he (Caillín) left a final instruction <note>original: *comairle*. Literally "advice," but here meaning a deathbed command.</note> with Manchán <note>Saint Manchán of Moethail (Mohill), County Leitrim. His festival is kept on February 14th, on which day a fair is held called "Monahan fair." The local folk etymology suggests it was named because buyers from County Monaghan frequented it, but the name actually derives from Saint Manchán. "Monahan's" (or St. Manchán's) Well is still shown there.</note> at the end of his life: namely, that he should be buried in the Cemetery of Mochaemhóg <note>original: *Reilig Mochaemhóg*.</note>. When twelve full years had passed after the death of the holy Caillín, his remains were to be raised and placed in the same shrine as those other relics.

Manchán did as Caillín himself had said; he raised his remains and opened the shrine. So, in the same shrine as the remains of the eleven apostles, and the remains of Stephen and Lawrence, and the cloth that was around Jesus Christ, are the remains of the holy Caillín, son of Niata.

And he [Caillín] declared for his city and his establishment <note>The monastery at Fenagh.</note> that it should be a high sanctuary and a chief church over all Ireland; and he decreed that this shrine should be a sanctuary for both laypeople and clergy until the end of the world.

It is clearly evident to everyone the honor and reverence God shows to that high elder and noble, faithful, truly pious saint: namely, the holy and devout Caillín. For God sent his own messenger and angel to him, to inform and tell him about every king and every prince who ruled Ireland <margin>folio 2, b. o</margin> from the time Cessair <note>In Irish mythology, Cessair was the leader of the first settlers to reach Ireland before the Flood.</note> entered it until the time of Patrick and Laoghaire Mac Néill; at which time Germanus was Abbot of Rome and Amatho was King of the Romans...

<meta>This page continues the account of Saint Caillín’s holy relics and his deathbed instructions to Saint Manchán regarding his burial and the status of his church at Fenagh.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>

In Rome were the relics of the eleven Apostles, along with the relics of Saint Martin, Saint Lawrence, and Stephen the Martyr. The protective covering he kept around those relics was a cloth which the Virgin Mary made with her own hands. It was this very cloth that was wrapped around Jesus while He was being nursed in His infancy.

Those are the relics which he subsequently ordered to be covered and enclosed in a shrine. He (Caillín) then left instructions with Manchán, toward the close of his life, to bury him in the Cemetery of Mochaemhóg <note>original: *Relig-Mochaemhog*. Saint Mochaemhóg was the founder of Liath-mór-Mochaemhóg, now known as Leighmockevoge in County Tipperary. He was a relative of Saint Caillín through the Conmaicne lineage. Records suggest he died around the year 646.</note>. He specified that twelve years after his death, they should take up his remains and place them in the same shrine as the other relics.

Manchán did exactly as Caillín had commanded. He disinterred Caillín's remains and opened the shrine. Thus, in the same shrine as the relics of the eleven Apostles, the relics of Stephen and Lawrence, and the cloth that was around Jesus Christ, are the relics of Saint Caillín, son of Niata.

And he granted a privilege to his city and settlement <note>This refers to Fenagh.</note>: that it should be a primary sanctuary and a high church over all Ireland. He also ordained that the shrine should be a protection for both laypeople and clergy until the end of the world.

The honor and reverence God granted to that illustrious, pious, and truly devout arch-senior and saint, the chaste Caillín, is plain and manifest to everyone. For it was to him that God sent His own messenger and angel to recount the history of every king and every lord who ruled Ireland from the time Cesair <note>A legendary figure in Irish mythology, often cited as the leader of the first inhabitants of Ireland before the Great Flood.</note> first arrived until the time of Saint Patrick and King Laeghaire Mac Neill. (At that time, Germanus <note>original: *Germanus*. The text incorrectly identifies him as the "Abbot of Rome." This is likely a mistake for Pope Celestine I, who sent Saint Germanus of Auxerre to Britain to combat the Pelagian heresy.</note> was the Abbot of Rome, and Amatho <note>A further historical error in the original text regarding Roman leadership.</note> was the King of the Romans.)

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the angel’s revelation to Saint Caillín regarding the history of the kings of Ireland, transitioning into a legendary history of the island’s earliest settlements.</meta>

...during his time. No less, however, did the angel reveal to him, by the command of Christ, every king who would come after him until the Day of Judgment in Ireland.

### The First Occupation: Cessair <note>original: *Gaḃail Cesra hic prima*. Cessair is a figure from the Lebor Gabála Érenn (The Book of Invasions), traditionally the leader of the first settlers of Ireland before the Biblical Flood.</note>

The angel first told Caillín that Cessair, daughter of Bith, son of Noah, took possession of this noble and angelic island, namely Ireland. Fifty women arrived along with her, and three men came with her: Fintan son of Labraid, Bith son of Noah, and Ladra. From Bith, son of Noah, son of Lamech, is named Slieve Beagh <note>original: *Sliaḃ Beṫa*, a mountain on the border of Counties Monaghan and Fermanagh.</note>. From Ladra the pilot is named Ard Ladrand <note>original: *Ard Ladrand*, traditionally identified as Ardamine, County Wexford.</note>. He was the first person to die in Ireland before the Flood; he died from an excess of women <note>original: *atḃaṫ do furail banaiċ*. According to legend, because there were only three men for fifty women, Ladra died from exhaustion.</note>.

They arrived forty days before the Flood. All of them died during the Flood except for Fintan alone, who remained in a deep sleep throughout the duration of the deluge. Fintan lived in the habitation of Ireland for three hundred and eleven years until Partholón arrived.

### The Second Occupation: Partholón <note>original: *Gaḃail Parṫaloin secunda*. Partholón is the leader of the second mythical group to settle Ireland.</note>

Ireland was empty for three hundred years after the Flood, the angel told Caillín, until Partholón, son of Sera, son of Sru, son of Esru, arrived from the Greeks. He was in Ireland for three hundred years until he died of a plague afterward, along with nine thousand of his people...

---

...of course, as there was no such “King of the Romans.” The writer was probably thinking of Amator, the predecessor of Saint Germanus in the See of Auxerre, who is also called “King of the Romans” in the *Tripartite Life of Saint Patrick*. *See Todd’s St. Patrick, 317, note 2.*

The following note, the first three lines of which represent a quatrain in Tadhg O’Rody’s <note>Tadhg O’Rody (fl. 1680–1706) was a prominent Irish scholar and antiquarian from County Leitrim who maintained the manuscripts at Fenagh.</note> handwriting, is added in the lower margin of the original manuscript, column 2, side a:

“Eight score cakes <note>original: *sruḃan*, a type of bread or cake.</note>, nine times over, was Caillín’s feast—a fact without falsehood. There were three persons to each of these cakes from the group chanting the Divine Office. That is: 1,440 cakes; and the usual number of church people with Caillín was 4,320 men, besides all other guests who might arrive, along with servants and others. I am Tadhg O’Rodaighe, son of Garrett junior, son of Tadhg, son of Garrett, son of Tadhg, son of Tadhg, son of William, etc. In the year of our Lord, 1688.”

“Eight score cakes, nine times, was Caillin’s feast, fact without falsehood. Three persons to each cake of these, of the band of Celebration-chanting, viz.: — 1,440 cakes; and the usual number of Church people with Caillin was 4,320 men, together with all other guests who might come besides, and servants and others. ## The First Settlement: Cessair

“Cessair, then,” said the angel to Caillín, “the daughter of Bith, son of Noah, was the first to occupy this holy, angelic island—that is, Ireland. Fifty women, moreover, came with her. Three men also came with her: namely, Fintan, son of Labraid, son of Bith, son of Lamech; Bith, son of Noah, son of Lamech, after whom Slieve Beagh<margin>Slieve Beagh: Now a mountain on the borders of Monaghan and Tyrone. The burial mound (cairn) where Bith is said to be buried still exists. See the Annals of the Four Masters, Year of the World 2242.</margin> is named; and Ladra the pilot, after whom Ard Ladrann<margin>Ard Ladrann: Likely Ardamine, County Wexford, though historical evidence for the identification is still being debated.</margin> is named. He [Ladra] was the first to die in Ireland before the Flood. He died from an excess of women.<note>original: *female persecution*. Legend says Ladra died from exhaustion because there were only three men to satisfy fifty women.</note>

They arrived forty days before the Flood. They all died before the Flood except for Fintan alone, who remained asleep during the deluge. Fintan lived in Ireland for three hundred and eleven years until Partholón arrived.”

### The Settlement of Nemed

Then Nemed arrived, the son of Agnomán, son of Paim, son of Tat, son of Sera, son of Sru, son of Esru<note>original: Tat, or Thoth, is said to have been the brother of Partholón and the son of Sera... son of Magog, son of Japheth. Some accounts make Fathacht the son of Riphat, son of Gomer, son of Magog.</note>, thirty years after the time of Partholón. Starn, Fergus, Iartan<note>original: Iartan. This name is more correctly written "Iarbhanél" in other authorities.</note>, and Aindind were his four sons. They spent two hundred and eleven years in Ireland until the destruction of Conaing’s Tower.

Conaing, the son of Faebar, of the Fomorians<note>original: Fomorians. The ancient popular idea as to the origin of the Fomorians is thus given by O'Flaherty: "Our historians call these people 'Fomorians'; by this word, our people denote all overseas enemies who infested the first colonies of Ireland. They claim all were descended from Ham from Africa, except for these first Fomorians, to whom they assign no other home or origin." (original Latin: *Hos Historici nostri Fomhoraigh nuncupant...*) — *Ogygia*, p. 5.</note>, built that tower to impose a harsh tax on the children of Nemed.

Because of this, the children of Nemed went to destroy that tower to rid themselves of their oppression. Sixty thousand of the men of Ireland<note>original: men of Ireland. By "men of Ireland," Nemed's descendants are meant.</note> went to that siege. The Fomorians and the men of Ireland fought near Conaing’s Tower<note>original: Conaing's Tower. This tower is alleged to have stood on *Tor-inis*, "Tower-Island" (otherwise Tory Island), off the northern coast of Donegal. It is supposed to have been a glass tower (original: *vitreous tower*). See Todd's *Irish Nennius*, p. 47, n. a.</note>. Because of the fury of the fighting, neither side noticed until the high tide of the sea swept over them both. None of them escaped except for one ship containing thirty strong men of the men of Ireland.

### Concerning the Settlement of the Fir Bolg

9,000 died in a single week—specifically, men, women, sons, and daughters. Ireland remained a wasteland for thirty years afterward.

### The Settlement of Nemed

After that period, Nemed arrived. He was the son of Agnomán, son of Pamp, son of Tat<note>original: Tat¹ — a genealogical link often connecting these figures to the biblical Magog.</note>, son of Sera, son of Sru, son of Esru. He arrived thirty years after the time of Partholón. Starn, Fergus, Iartan<note>original: Iartan² — often called Iarbhanél in other sources.</note>, and Aindind were his four sons. They lived in Ireland for 211 years, until the destruction of Conaing’s Tower<note>original: Conang's Tower³ — a fortress associated with the Fomorians.</note>.

Conaing, the son of Faebar, belonged to the Fomorians. He built that tower to impose a harsh tax on Nemed’s descendants.

To escape this oppression, the descendants of Nemed went to destroy the tower. Sixty thousand of the men of Ireland marched to its destruction. The Fomorians<note>original: Fomorians⁴</note> and the men of Ireland<note>original: men of Ireland⁵ — refers to the descendants of Nemed.</note> met near the tower. They fought so stubbornly that neither side noticed the tide rising until the water washed over them both. Only one boat escaped, carrying thirty Irish warriors.

### The Settlement of the Fir Bolg

Ireland remained uninhabited for two hundred years, just as the Angel had revealed to the noble, pious, and faithful Saint Caillín, the son of Niata. Finally, the descendants of Starn (Nemed’s son), known as the Fir Bolg, arrived from Greece. They were fleeing the harsh demands imposed on them by the Greeks<note>original: Greeks⁶ — Gregaig.</note>, who forced them to carry clay onto bare rocks until they became flowering plains. These men headed for—

<margin>
Greeks<note>original: Gregaig</note>. This is the last word of the text of folio 2, b, in Manuscript A. In the lower margin, these lines occur:
*Nocha mianachad aoibe neach da fiadachad feine. Is ferr foidid tre aille ná gairbe ocur geire. Cleith an uilc do gni co taoi maith do breith cuirp o dál Dé ; Cen co hatma in ti dogni, cuici ni na hasna e.*

<meta>This page continues the history of the Fir Bolg, explaining how they escaped their servitude in Greece and divided Ireland among their five leaders. It also mentions an even earlier, fleeting visit to Ireland by three fishermen just before the Great Flood.</meta>

...bags. These men made hide-covered boats for themselves out of the leather bags they had used to carry the earth. They traveled toward Ireland, arriving in three groups. One third of them, led by Sláinge, son of Dela, son of Lot, landed at **Inbher Slainge**<note>original: Inbher-Slainge — The Slaney estuary.</note> with one thousand people. Another third, numbering two thousand people, landed at **Inbher Dubhglaisi**<note>original: Inbher-Dubhglaisi</note> under the leadership of Gann and Sengann. The final third of the host, led by Genann and Rudraige, landed at **Inbher Domhnann**<note>original: Inbher-Domhnann</note>. Because of this location, they are called the **Fir Domhnann**. These three groups are known as the **Fir Bolg**, the **Fir Domhnann**, and the **Gaileoin**.

### The Five Kings and Their Provinces

Sláinge, the king of the **Gaileoin**, ruled the province stretching from **Inbher Colptha**<note>original: Inbher-Colptha — The Boyne estuary.</note> to the **Meeting of the Three Waters**<note>original: Comar-tri-nusce</note>. Gann ruled from that meeting point to **Belach Conglais**<note>original: Belach-Conglais</note>. Sengann ruled from Belach Conglais to Limerick, covering the two provinces of Munster. Genann ruled the province belonging to Medb and Ailill <note>Connacht</note>, and Rudraige ruled the province of Conchobar <note>Ulster</note>. This group also numbered two thousand people. This royal assembly then granted the high kingship of all Ireland to Sláinge.

### The Story of the Three Fishermen

Afterward, Saint Caillín related what the angel had told him regarding the very first people to reach Ireland: three fishermen named Capa, Laigne, and Luadat. They arrived one year before the Great Flood. However, they soon left to go back for their wives and were ultimately drowned at **Tuaig Inbher**. 

Caillín explained why these men were not counted in the earlier lists of settlements: it was because they brought nothing with them in their ship except for three handfuls of green grass. "I mention them now," Caillín said, "so that no one can accuse me of leaving anything out of my account."

<columns>2</columns>
¹ <margin>**Inbher-Slainge:** The estuary of the river Slaney at Wexford.</margin>

² <margin>**Inbher-Dubhglaisi:** There are several theories about this location. The name means "Estuary of the Black Stream" and could be translated as Inver-Douglas. However, the account of the first battle of **Magh-Tuiredh** (found in a Trinity College Dublin manuscript) states that Gann and Sengann landed at a place called Ard-na-Caerach on Inbher-Dubhglaisi, which served as the boundary between the ancient territories of Corcomroe and Corco-Bhaiscind in County Clare. This likely refers to the mouth of the river Enny, which flows into Liscannor Bay.</margin>

³ <margin>**Inbher-Domhnann:** Some sources identify this as Malahide Bay. However, it was almost certainly the ancient name for Broadhaven in the barony of Erris, County Mayo, which was once called **Irrus-Domhnann**, or the "Erris of the Damnonii."</margin>

⁴ <margin>**Inbher-Colptha:** The estuary of the River Boyne.</margin>

⁵ <margin>**Comar-tri-nusce:** The "confluence of three waters"—the meeting point of the rivers Suir, Nore, and Barrow near Waterford.</margin>

⁶ <margin>**Belach-Conglais:** "The pass of Glas’s hound." This was a location near Cork city.</margin>

### The Division of the Provinces

Sláinge, king of the **Gaileoin**, ruled the province stretching from **Inbher Colptha**<note>original: Inbher-Colptha — The Boyne estuary.</note> to the **Meeting of the Three Waters**<note>original: Comar-tri-nusce — Where the Suir, Nore, and Barrow rivers meet.</note>. Sengand's province reached from **Belach Conglais** to **Limerick**<note>original: Luimnech</note>, covering the two provinces of Munster. Genand ruled over the province of **Medb** and **Ailill** <note>This is the province of Connacht, where Medb and Ailill were Queen and King Consort.</note>. Rudraige, however, ruled the province of **Conchobar** <note>This is Ulster, frequently called *Cuiged Conchobhair* (original: Conor's Province, literally "Conor's Fifth"), as Conor Mac Nessa was king of Ulster at the same time Medb and Ailill governed Connacht.</note>. Two thousand people also made up his host. All these chieftains then granted the high kingship of Ireland to Sláinge.

### The First Three Fishermen

Saint Caillín also related afterward, as the Angel told him, how three fishermen named Capa, Laighne, and Luasad were actually the first to arrive in Ireland. They arrived one year before the Great Flood. They eventually set out to return for their wives, but they were drowned at **Tuagh-Inbher**<note>original: Tuagh-Inbher — The mouth of the River Bann. According to the *Dinnsenchus* (Lore of Places), the name comes from Tuag, daughter of King Conall Collamhrach, who was drowned there.</note>.

Regarding this story, Caillín said: "The reason why I did not give them the first place in the list before the other colonizations is because they took no provisions with them in their ship, but only three handfuls of green grass. The reason I have included them now," said Caillín, "is so that no one can find fault with my account."

"If you desire the truth of the matter," said Caillín, "concerning the number of years from the beginning of the world until the time of Sláinge, son of Dela, I will give you the truth as I received it from the angel:

## Regarding the Kings of the Tuatha Dé Danann, as Caillín recounted

**Nuada Airgetlám** <term>Airgetlám: "Silver-hand."</term> ruled for four years before coming to Ireland, until his hand was struck off at the first **Battle of Magh Tuired**. <note>original: Maige Tuired — A legendary battle where the Tuatha Dé Danann defeated the Fir Bolg. Nuada lost his hand there, making him temporarily unfit for kingship as a king had to be physically "whole."</note>

**Bres**, son of Elathan, ruled for seven years, until Nuada’s hand was healed. <note>Bres was chosen as a regent but proved to be a tyrant.</note>

**Nuada Airgetlám** ruled again for twenty years.

**Delbaeth** ruled for ten years.

<sup>2</sup> *Argatros.*—"Silver Wood." The ancient name of a wood on the River Nore, in the parish of Rathbeagh, barony of Galmoy, County Kilkenny. The fort from which the parish derives its name (Rath-Beothaigh, now Rathbeagh) is said to have been constructed by Heremon. Some antiquaries, however (including the Four Masters, at the Year of the World <term>A.M. 3501</term>), represent the battle as having been fought in the neighborhood of Geashill, in King's County <note>now County Offaly</note>.

<sup>3</sup> *one hundred.*—The reign of Tigernmas...

<meta>This page continues the chronological list of the Milesian kings (the sons of Míl) as recorded by Saint Caillín. It transitions from the earliest joint rule of the brothers Heremon and Eber into a lineage-by-lineage account of their successors, detailing their reign lengths and significant battles.</meta>

### Regarding the kings of the sons of Míl <note>original: Miled; refers to the Milesians, the legendary ancestors of the Gaels</note>, as Caillín reckoned them.

**Heremon** and **Eber** reigned for one year in joint sovereignty, until Eber was killed in the Battle of Airgetros <note>original: Argartros</note> by Heremon.
**Heremon**, son of Míl, reigned for fifteen years.
**Muimne**, **Luigne**, and **Laigne** reigned until all three died.
The four sons of **Eber**—namely **Er**, **Orba**, **Feron**, and **Fergna**—reigned for half a year.
**Irial Fáith** <note>original: Irial the Prophet</note>, son of Heremon, reigned for 10 years.
**Ethriel**, son of Irial, reigned for thirty-one years <note>original: a month and 30 years</note>.
**Conmael**, son of Eber (the first king of Ireland from Munster), reigned for 50 years, or according to some, 27 years.
**Tigernmas**, son of Follach, grandson of Ethriel; he was in sovereignty for one hundred years.
**Eochaid Étghothach** <note>original: Eochaidh (i.e. Ethgothach)</note>, son of Conmael, reigned for 30 years.
**Cermna** and **Sobairce**, 40 years. They were the first kings of Ireland from the Ulaid <note>original: Ultonians; the people of Ulster</note>.
**Eochaid Faebarglas**, twenty years.
**Eber**, son of Conmael.
**Fiacha Labrainne**, son of Smirgoll, 24 years.
**Eocho Mumu**, 20 years, or 21 years.
**Oengus Olmucaid**, son of Fiacha Labrainne, eighteen years.
**Enna Airgtech**, son of Eochaid Mumu, 40 years.

---

<sup>3</sup> **Tigernmas** <note>the text "mas" in the OCR is the end of his name</note> is variously fixed at 100, 70, and 50 years.
<sup>4</sup> *Cermna and Sobairce.*—These are described as having been killed in the battle of Dun Cermna (or Dún Mhic Phádraig, as it was called in Keating’s time), a fort on the Old Head of Kinsale, County Cork. From Sobairce was named Dun Sobairce, now Dunseverick, in the barony of Cary, County Antrim. The date of the battle is given as <term>A.M.</term> <note>original: Anno Mundi; Year of the World</note> 3707.
<sup>5</sup> *Eber.*—Some critic, probably Thady

<column-break/>

O'Rody, has added the marginal note: <margin>“I do not find this Eber in the Book of Occupations.” <note>original: “ni faġbuim int Eber ſo iſin żabaltus”; referring to the *Lebor Gabála Érenn*, or Book of Invasions</note></margin> His name does not occur in the standard lists of the Kings of Ireland.
<sup>6</sup> *Fiacha Labrainne.*—Killed in the battle of Belgadan (now Bulgadan), near Kilmallock, County Limerick, in the year <term>A.M.</term> 3751.
<sup>7</sup> *Eochaid Mumu* (from whom Mumha, that is, Momonia or Munster, takes its name).—Killed in the battle of Cliu (or Cliach), a territory lying around Knockany, County Limerick, in the year <term>A.M.</term> 3772.

**Rothechtaid**, son of Main, son of Aengus Olmucaid.

Regarding the silver shields which **Enna Airgtech** <note>The king from the previous page</note> is stated to have given away: his name *Airgtech* means silver-like <note>original: argenteus</note>, derived from *airget*, meaning silver <note>original: argentum</note>. Enna Airgtech was killed in the battle of Raighne (or Magh-Raighne) in Ossory, in the year of the world <term>A.M.</term> 3842. <note>Anno Mundi, or "Year of the World," is a calendar based on the biblical creation of the world.</note>

^1^ <term>Finnscothach</term>.—Irish etymologists have explained this title as being derived from *finn* (wine) and *scotha* (flowers). They add that during the reign of this Fiacha Finnscothach, there were flowers that produced wine, which was then pressed into cups. See Keating (Haliday's edition), page 329.

<column-break/>

^2^ <term>Aildergdoit</term>.—In some ancient sources, the name is written *Faildergdoit* and explained as meaning "red hand-ring"; for it is said that gold rings were first worn around the hand during his reign. *Book of Leinster*, folio 9, b 2.
^3^ *Son of Rothechtach.*—More accurately, "son of Demal, son of Rothechtach." See *Book of Leinster*, folio 10, a 1.
^4^ <term>Ollfinachta</term>.—He was the son of Rothechtach, the king who preceded him, and was killed by his successor in the year of the world <term>A.M.</term> 4177.

According to the chronology of the **Four Masters** <note>The Annals of the Four Masters is a major chronicle of medieval Irish history compiled in the 1630s.</note>, or <term>A.M.</term> 3389, according to the calculations of **O'Flaherty** <note>Roderic O'Flaherty (1629–1718), author of *Ogygia*, a major work on Irish chronology.</note>. *Ogygia*, part iii. chapter xxxii.

<sup>5</sup> **Apthach**. — The etymologists say that **Eochu** was so called from the great number of persons who died during his reign (<term>A.M.</term> 3301, according to Keating; 3432, according to O'Flaherty). *Apthach* is an adjective derived from *aptu*, meaning destruction or ruin <note>original: exitium</note>; the accusative singular is *apthin*, meaning destruction or death <note>original: perniciem</note>, as noted in Ebel’s edition of Zeuss's *Grammatica Celtica*, page 266.

<sup>6</sup> **Corcu Loígde**; or the "clan of Lugaid." — This tribe, which derived its name from

<column-break/>

**Lugaid Laighde**, who was eighth in descent from Lugaid, the son of Ith (the nephew of Milesius), was settled in the southwest of the present County Cork. See the historical tract on this clan published by **Dr. O'Donovan** from the *Book of Lecan* <note>A significant medieval Irish manuscript compiled around 1400.</note> in the *Celtic Society's Miscellany*, 1849, pages 1–144. The **O'Driscolls** were the traditional chiefs of this tribe.

<sup>7</sup> **Lugaid Cal**. — The brother of Lugaid Laighde, mentioned in the previous note.

<sup>8</sup> **Innarraid**. — So called because he was the first person who gave pay <note>The term "Innarraid" refers to "wages" or "stipend."</note> to soldiers.

**Simon Brec**, son of Aedan Glas.

**Eochu Uarches**, son of Lughaidh.
**Eochu** (known as *Fiadhmuine* <note>original: "Venator," meaning "the hunter"</note>), and **Conaing** (known as *Bec-iacla* <note>meaning "of little fear"</note>), the two sons of Conmael, son of Lughaidh Cal (they ruled for five years in joint sovereignty). Alternatively, they are described as the two sons of Conghal, son of Lughaidh of the **Corcu Loígde** <note>A major kingdom in ancient Munster</note>. Others say Eochu was the son of Conghal, and Conaing Bec-iacla was the son of Duach, son of Muiredhach, son of Simon; they shared the same mother as Eochu Uarches, son of Conghal. **Lughaidh**, son of Eochu Uarches; it was by his hand that Eochaidh Fiadhmuine fell.
**Lughaidh** reigned for seven years after that, until he fell by the hand of Conaing Bec-iacla (whose name means "his fear is small" <note>original: bec a egla</note>).
**Conaing Bec-iacla** ruled after that.
**Art**, son of Lughaidh, son of Eochu Uarches.
**Eochaid**, son of Art; but I do not count this reign unless it refers to **Oilill Find**, son of Art.

**Eocha**, son of Oilill Find.
**Argatmar**.

This refers to the pay of soldiers in Ireland. The epithet *innarraidh* seems to be a compound of *inn*, or *ind*, the Irish definite article, and *araidh*, a word related to the Latin *aes, aeris* <note>meaning "money," "bronze," or "pay"</note>.

<note>1 *Fiadhmuine*.—Translated into Latin as "Venator," or "the hunter," by the historian O'Flaherty, who likely considered the word to be a compound of *fiadh* (a deer) and *muine* (a brake or thicket).</note>

<note>3 *Carco-Luighde*, or *Corco-Luigdech*.—See note 6 on page 25.</note>

<note>5 *bec a ecla*; literally "little his fear";</note>

<column-break/>

*iacla=ecla* ("fear").—In the *Book of Leinster* <note>A significant 12th-century Irish manuscript containing myths, genealogies, and lore</note> (folio 10, b. 1), Conaing is called *bec fhiaclach* (or "little-toothed"); and also *bec-eclach*, or "little fearing," because "he never felt fear or terror."

<note>6 *reckon him*.—The name "Eochaidh" is undoubtedly a mistake for Oilill Finn, or Oilill the "Fair," who reigned from <term>A.M.</term> 3542 to 3551 <note>A.M. stands for Anno Mundi, "Year of the World," a calendar starting from the biblical creation</note>, according to O'Flaherty's chronology. In the writings of Keating and the Four Masters, the name of Fiacha Tolgrach is given as the predecessor of this Oilill Finn; however, it does not appear in O'Flaherty’s list, nor in the more ancient list found in the *Book of Leinster*.</note>

**Duach Ladhrach** <note>original: "ladhrach," meaning "the swift" or "the clawed"</note>, son of **Fiacha Tolgrach**.

**Fergus Fortamail** ("Fergus the Strong").
**Oengus Tuirmech** <note>original: "tuirmech," meaning "the Reckoner" or "the Prolific"</note>, son of **Eochaid**.
**Conall Collamrach**.
**Nia Segamain**, son of **Adamair Foltchain** ("Adamair of the Beautiful Hair" <note>original: "foltchain"</note>).

<meta>This page continues the chronological succession of the High Kings of Ireland, following the transition from the period of the sons of Argatmar to the establishment of the line of Ugaine the Great. It focuses on the legendary lineages and the duration of their reigns.</meta>

**Duach Ladhrach** <note>original: "ladhrach," meaning "the swift" or "the clawed"</note>, son of **Fiacha Tolgrach**.

**Lugaid Laidech**.

**Aed Ruad** <note>original: "Aedh Ruadh," meaning "Aed the Red"</note>, son of **Badurn**, son of **Argatmar**; **Dithorba**, son of **Diman**, from **Uisneach** in Meath; and **Cimbaeth**, son of **Fintan**, son of **Argatmar**, from **Finnabair** in the plain of **Magh Inis**. <note>Magh Inis is the modern-day Barony of Lecale, County Down.</note>

**Macha**, daughter of **Aed Ruad**.

**Rechtaid Rigderg** <note>original: "Rig-derg," meaning "Red-arm"</note>, son of **Lugaid**, son of **Eochaid**, son of **Oilill Find**, son of **Art**, son of **Lugaid Laimderg** ("Lugaid of the Red-hand"), son of **Eochaid Uarches**.

**Ugaine the Great** (**Ugaine Mór**), son of **Eochaid Buadach** ("Eochaid the Victorious"), obtained the kingship of Ireland and **Scotland** <note>original: "Alba"</note>, extending his rule as far as the **English Channel** <note>original: "Muir-Icht," the Sea of Icht</note>.

**Cobthach Cael Breg** <note>meaning "the Slender One of Bregia"</note>.

**Fercorp**, son of **Mogcorp**.

**Conlaed the Handsome** (**Conlaed Caem**), son of **Irereo**.

**Adamair** <note>original: "Amadair," a common scribal variation</note>, son of **Fercorp**.

**Eochaid Altlethan** <note>original: "Altlethan," meaning "of the Wide Joints"</note>, son of **Ailill**.

**Fergus Fortamhail** <note>original: "Fortamhail," meaning "the Victorious" or "the Brave"</note>.

**Oengus Tuirmech**, son of **Eochaid**.

**Conall Collamrach**.

**Nia Segamain**, son of **Adamair Foltchain** <note>original: "Foltchain," meaning "Fair-haired"</note>.

**Enna Airgthech** <note>original: "Airgthech," meaning "the Plunderer" or "the Wealthy"</note>, son of **Oengus Tuirmech**.

**Crimthand Coscrach**, who ruled for 4 years, until he fell by the hand of **Rudraighe**.
