<column-break/>

<sup>7</sup> 4 **years**. The numerals *viii* <note>meaning 8</note> of Manuscript A probably represent *vii* (7), which is the number of years **Crimthand** is generally understood to have reigned; for it is not always easy to distinguish between the numbers *v* (5) and *ii* (2) in Irish manuscripts.

<summary>This page catalogs a sequence of legendary Irish High Kings, from the era of Macha and Ugaine Mór through to Crimthand Coscrach, detailing their lineages and titles. It includes scholarly annotations on the translation of their epithets and chronological discrepancies in medieval manuscripts.</summary>
<keywords>High Kings of Ireland, Macha, Ugaine Mór, Irish Genealogies, Alba, Muir-Icht, Irish Manuscripts, Crimthand Coscrach, Oengus Tuirmech</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the genealogy of the Irish High Kings, focusing on Rudraige mac Sithride, the ancestor of the Dál nAraidi and the legendary heroes of the Ulster Cycle. It details his reign, his famous descendants, and the traditional divisions of Irish nobility.</meta>

**Rudraige**, son of **Sithride**, moreover, is the ancestor from whom the **Dál nAraidi** <note>The Dál nAraidi were a major kingdom of the Cruthin in north-eastern Ireland.</note> descend. These are the men of **Ulster** from **Emain Macha** <note>original: "Ulaiḋ Emna"</note>—the descendants of **Ollom Fodla**, son of **Fiachu Findscothach**. Twenty-five of them took the kingship of Ireland; for these are the three noble lines of Ireland: **Conn**, **Araidhe**, and **Eoghan**, as **Eochaid** sang <note>original: "ut Eochaid cecinit"</note>:

->The three nobles of Ireland who are celebrated,<-
->The hosts of the **Branch** <note>The "Craobh Ruadh" or Red Branch of Ulster.</note> with the splendor of Ulster,<-
->**Conn**, for whom music and feasts were a covenant,<-
->And the **Eoghanacht** of **Munster**.<-

**Rudraige**, son of **Sithride**, was the grandfather of **Conall Cernach** <note>original: "Conaill cernaiġ," meaning "Conall of the Victories"</note>, son of **Amargin**, and of **Fergus mac Róich** <note>original: "Ferguḋa mic Roiġ"</note>. Every territory that **Rudraige** extended his rule over throughout Ireland, **Fergus** later settled his own descendants upon by the strength of battle—namely the **Corca Duibhne**, the **Ciarraige**, and the **Conmaicne** <note>These are tribal groups that traditionally claimed descent from Fergus mac Róich.</note>. He won twenty battles while defending the rights of **Rudraige** over Ireland, as the *Book of Invasions* <note>original: "lebar gaḃala"</note> states.

**Rudraige** was seventy years in the kingship of Ireland until he died of a plague in **Silver-glen** <note>original: "Argatglinḋ"</note>.

***

<margin>1 **Dál nAraidi**—The Dál nAraidi, who derived their tribe name from **Fiacha Araidhe**, king of Ulster in the third century, occupied the greater part of the present County **Antrim**. See *Ogygia*, part 3, chapter 18; and Reeves's *Ecclesiastical Antiquities*, page 334, following.</margin>

<margin>2 **Emain**—The royal residence (near **Armagh**) of the kings of Ulster until AD 332, when the place was demolished by the **Brothers Colla**, ancestors of the **Airgíalla**, and the Rudrician tribes were driven into the district of **Ulidia** proper, or the present counties of **Down** and **Antrim**.</margin>

<margin>3 **Eochaidh**, that is, **Eochaidh Ua Floinn**, a famous Irish poet who died about AD 984; for an account of whom see O'Reilly's *Irish Writers*, page 64. O'Clery's copy of the *Book of Invasions* (Manuscript in the Royal Irish Academy) contains a poem ascribed to **Eochaidh**, in which the descendants of the three chieftains, **Conn**, **Araidhe**, and **Eoghan**, are thus referred to:</margin>

> **Conn**, **Eoghan**, noble **Araidhe**—
> The descendants of the three chieftains are:
> The **Araidhe** in **Emain** without stain,
> **Conn** "of the Hundred Battles" <note>original: "ced chathach"</note> in **Tara** <note>original: "Temair"</note>;
> **Eoghan** in **Cashel** of the kings—
> There his seed resided.

<summary>This page records the reign of Rudraige mac Sithride, a pivotal ancestor in Ulster's history, and identifies him as the progenitor of the Dál nAraidi and the grandfather of heroes Conall Cernach and Fergus mac Róich. It highlights the traditional Irish division of nobility into the lines of Conn, Araidhe, and Eoghan.</summary>

<keywords>Rudraige mac Sithride, Dál nAraidi, Emain Macha, Ulster Cycle, Fergus mac Róich, Conall Cernach, Eochaidh Ua Floinn, Book of Invasions, High Kings of Ireland, Irish genealogy</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the genealogy of the Irish High Kings, focusing on Rudraige mac Sithride and the expansion of his descendants, including the legendary Fergus Mac Róich, across Ireland.</meta>

**Rudraige**, as well, was the son of **Sithride**. From him descend the **Dál nAraidi**; <margin>1 **Dál nAraidi**—A major kingdom of the Cruthin people in North-East Ireland.</margin> for they are the true **Ulidians** <note>The ancient people of Ulster.</note> of **Emain Macha**, namely the descendants of **Ollom Fodla**, son of **Fiacha Finnscothach**. Twenty-five of them held the kingship of Ireland, for the three noble clans <note>original: "septs"</note> of Ireland were **Conn**, **Araide**, and **Eoghan**, as **Eochaid** sang:

->The three noble clans of Ireland, it is sung,<-
->Are the hosts of the **Araide** <margin>4 **Araide**—The Dál nAraidi; see note 1.</margin> with the pride of **Ulster**;<-
->**Conn**, <margin>5 **Conn**—The race of Conn of the Hundred Battles, a legendary High King.</margin> to whom assemblies were dear,<-
->And the **Eoghanacht** <margin>6 **Eoghanacht**—The dynasty descending from Eoghan Mór, son of Oilill Olum, who ruled Munster in the second century.</margin> of **Munster**.<-

**Rudraige**, son of **Sithride**, was also the ancestor of **Conall Cernach**, son of **Amergin**, and of **Fergus Mac Róich**. Wherever **Rudraige** ruled throughout Ireland, **Fergus** later established his own descendants through the force of battle—namely the **Corca**, <margin>7 **Corca**—This likely refers to the **Corca-Modhruadh**, the ancient inhabitants of Corcomroe in County Clare (represented by the O’Loghlens and O’Conors). They are said to descend from Corc Ferdoid, a son of Fergus Mac Róich and **Medb** (Mab), the Queen of Connacht often called the "Cleopatra of Irish history."</margin> the **Ciarraige**, <margin>8 **Ciarraige**—Descendants of Ciar, another son of Fergus Mac Róich and Queen Medb.</margin> and the **Conmaicne**. <margin>9 **Conmaicne**—Various clans descended from Conmac, son of Fergus Mac Róich and Queen Medb. For more, see O’Flaherty’s *Ogygia*, part 3, chapter 46.</margin> He won twenty battles defending **Rudraige’s** right to Ireland, as the *Book of Invasions* <note>original: "Lebar Gabhala"</note> records. **Rudraige** held the kingship of Ireland for seventy years until he died of a plague in **Silver-glen** <note>original: "Argat-glend"</note>. <margin>10 **Silver-glen**—The scholar John O’Donovan identifies this as a glen in the barony of Farney, County Monaghan, though his authority for this is questioned.</margin>

The primary branch of the **Ciarraige** family was settled in **Ciarraighe-Luachra**, the ancient name for **County Kerry** (though the name *Ciarraighe* originally only represented the northern part of the present county). Other branches of the tribe gave their names to districts in **County Roscommon** and **County Mayo**. See O’Flaherty’s *Ogygia*, part 3, chapter 46.

It describes how Fergus secured territories for his descendants across Ireland, establishing the roots of several major clans.</summary>

This is the year in which **Christ**, Son of the living God, was born in Bethlehem of Judah to save the human race.
The Pentarchs <note>original: "coicedaig" — the five provincial kings of Ireland.</note> after that: namely, **Conchobar**, son of Fachtna Fathach; **Cairbre Nia Fer**; **Tigernach Tetbannach**; **Cú Roí**, son of Dáire; and **Ailill**, son of Máta.
**Nuada Necht** of the Leinstermen; after him,
**Conaire the Great**. It may be that after Conaire the Great there were provincial kings, as others say <note>original: "ut alii aiunt"</note>.
**Lugaid Riab nDerg**. This is the Lugaid whom the three triplets <note>original: "findemna" — the Three Finns of Emain.</note> fathered upon their sister, **Clothru**, daughter of Eochaid Fedlech. Then Lugaid fathered a son upon his own mother: namely, **Crimthann**, son of Lugaid. This Crimthann was King of Ireland.

**Findatmar**, son of Nia Segamain,¹ reigned.

**Bresal Bodibaid**,² son of Rudraige.

**Eochaid Fedlech**, son of Find.

**Eochaid Oirem**; brother to Eochaid Fedlech.

**Etirscél the Great**, great-grandson of Iar, of the Erna.⁵

This is the year in which **Christ**, son of the Living God, was born⁶ in Bethlehem of Judea to save the human race.

The **Pentarchs**⁷ <note>original: "Pentarchs" — the five provincial kings of Ireland who ruled simultaneously.</note> followed after, namely: **Conchobar**, son of Fachtna Fathach; **Cairbre Nia Fer**; **Tigernach Tetbannach**; **Cú Roí**, son of Dáire; and **Ailill**, son of Máta.

**Nuada Necht** of the Leinstermen; after him

**Conaire the Great**. Perhaps the Pentarchs should be placed after Conaire the Great, as others say. <note>original: "ut alii aiunt"</note>

**Lugaid Riab nDerg**.⁸ This is the Lugaid whom the three **Findemnas** <note>original: "Findemnas" — the "White Triplets" of Emain Macha.</note> fathered upon their sister, **Clothru**, daughter of Eochaid Fedlech. This same Lugaid fathered a son upon his own mother: namely, **Crimthann**, son of Lugaid. This Crimthann was King of Ireland.

The following may be translated—

<note>original Irish: "Mairg cuingir ni ar a charaid..."</note>
Woe to those who force a thing from a friend,
Unless he is pleased to give it;
For the two things that will result from it
Are malice and bitter reproach.

<note>original Irish: "Is saoith lem int aos leigind..."</note>
It is a grief to me that the learned class
Should go to the fierce pains of hell,
While those who never studied wisdom
Should enter a sunny paradise!

<column-break/>

Of all such asking, two things are the end:
Bitter reproaches and a hateful mind.

"Is it not sad that learned men should go
To Hell's dire pains and everlasting woe!
And simple, unlearned dullards should rejoice
In all the joys of sunny Paradise!"

⁸ **Riab nDerg**.—Properly **Sriab nDerg**, "of the red stripe." He was so called because of two red stripes with which he is alleged to have been born (around his neck and waist), as a sign of his incestuous origin.

⁵ ...Irish kings. He died in 266 AD according to the *Annals of the Four Masters*; and is said to have been buried in Rosnaree, on the southern bank of the River Boyne, almost directly across from Newgrange. ¹ ***Colla Uais***, or "Colla the Noble."—One of three brothers called "the Collas" (sons of Eochaidh Doimhlen, son of Cairpre Lifechair), by whom the ancient residence of the Rudrician kings at Emain Macha <note>original: "Emania"</note>, near Armagh, was destroyed in the year 332 AD. The Rudrician tribes, the ancient Ulstermen, or Clan Rury, were at the same time driven to the east of the River Bann and Lough Neagh. The territory to which they were then confined, comprising the present counties of Antrim and Down, was afterwards known as Ulidia proper.

<column-break/>

These Collas were the ancestors of the principal families of Oriel <note>original: "Oirghiall"</note>, and several other districts not only in Ireland, but also in Scotland. From Colla Uais, the eldest of the three, are descended the MacDonnells, MacAlisters, and MacDougalds of Scotland; and from Colla Da Chrich, the O'Kellys of Ui Maine <note>original: "Hy-Maine"</note> derive their well-documented descent.

...in the part of the following poem which speaks of the regal succession.
⁵ *Dathi*.—**Nathi**, A.; a change produced by the assimilation of the **d** of Dathi to the final letter of some preposition or governing word preceding it in a sentence. Thus *ren Dathi* (before Dathi) would be pronounced nearly as *re Nathi*.
⁶ *Lugaid*.—The letters **c. n.** are prefixed to signify that this king was of the *Clann-Neill*, or the descendants of Niall of the Nine Hostages.
⁷ *Son of Erc and Muiredach*.—Erc, usually a man’s name, was the name of Muirchertach’s mother. She was the daughter of Loarn, king of Scotland <note>original: "Alba"</note>.

<column-break/>

His father was Muiredach, son of Eoghan, son of Niall of the Nine Hostages. (See the account of Muirchertach and his mother, printed from *Mac Firbis’s Genealogies*, in Todd’s *Irish Nennius*, Appendix pp. 101–104). A remarkable story <note>original: "tract"</note> describing the life and death of Muirchertach Mac Erca is contained in the *Yellow Book of Lecan*. According to this story, through the tricks of a fairy or sorceress named Sin (pronounced *Sheen*), for whom he had abandoned his own wife, he was drowned in a vat of wine and subsequently burned in the “house of Cleitech over the River Boyne.” The *Annals of the Four Masters* record this catastrophe in the year 527 AD. The letters **c. n.** in the margin...

<summary>This page lists the High Kings of Ireland from Colla Uais to Diarmait Mac Cerbaill, noting the arrival of Saint Patrick and Saint Caillin. It also provides genealogical details and a legendary account of the death of Muirchertach Mac Erca.</summary>
<keywords>High Kings of Ireland, Saint Patrick, Saint Caillin, Conmaicne, Muirchertach Mac Erca, Clann-Neill, Yellow Book of Lecan, Irish mythology, genealogy</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of the High Kings of Ireland. Having reached the reign of Diarmait mac Cerbaill in the mid-6th century, the text now transitions from historical record to the prophecies of Saint Caillin regarding future kings.</meta>

Saint Caillin lived during the time of Diarmait mac Cerbaill, and he promised that he would name every king who ruled Ireland from the time of Sláine <note>Sláine mac Dela, the legendary first High King of Ireland</note> down to Diarmait. There were <margin>folio 5a, 1.</margin> seven score <note>140</note> kings between them <note>original: *eturra*</note>, as Saint Caillin himself revealed when he numbered the kings in the poem that follows, "Great Ireland, Island of Angels," as he said:

->One king and seven score kings,<-
->I tell you without dispute,<-
->From Sláine to pleasant Diarmait,<-
->Is the number of kings who ruled Ireland.<-

After counting those kings, as he had received their names through a revelation from an angel, Caillin spoke: "Since I have now counted," he said, "every king who ruled Ireland from Sláine until now—that is, until Diarmait son of Cerball—I will now perform a task more difficult <term>doirdge: more difficult or obscure</term> than that. I will declare the names of every king who shall rule Ireland from today until the Doom <term>brath: the Day of Judgment</term>—that is, from Diarmait's time until the end of the world," said the holy person, Saint Caillin.

Though it is a secret and obscure thing to the people of the world to know what they cannot hear or see—specifically those certain things destined and fated for them—the kings who came before and during his own lifetime were no more obvious or manifest to Caillin than the kings who would come after him. This was because they were revealed to him by the angel, to whom every thing is clear and known.

He then named the kings who would rule Ireland from the time of Diarmait until the Doom:

1.  **Diarmait Mac Cerbaill**, first.
2.  <margin>Clann-Neill <note>The letters 'c. n.' in the margin indicate the king belonged to the Clann-Neill dynasty.</note></margin> **Domnall** and **Fergus**, the two sons of Muirchertach Mac Erca; they ruled for either one <margin>folio 5a, 2.</margin> or three years <note>The Annals of the Four Masters record their joint reign as lasting three years.</note>. They died a natural death.
3.  <margin>Clann-Neill</margin> **Baetan**, son of Muirchertach, and **Eochaid**, son of Domnall; they ruled for three years.

<summary>Saint Caillin confirms the number of Irish kings from the legendary Sláine to his contemporary, Diarmait mac Cerbaill, and begins a prophetic list of all future kings of Ireland revealed to him by an angel.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Caillin, Diarmait mac Cerbaill, High Kings of Ireland, prophecy, Sláine mac Dela, Clann-Neill, Irish history, medieval genealogy</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of the High Kings of Ireland. Having reached the reign of Diarmait mac Cerbaill in the mid-6th century, the text now transitions from historical record to the prophecies of Saint Caillin regarding future kings.</meta>

Caillin lived, then, during the time of Diarmait; and he himself promised that he would name every king who ruled Ireland from Sláine <note>original: Slainge; Sláine mac Dela, the legendary first High King of Ireland</note> to Diarmait. There were one hundred and forty kings <note>original: "One king and seven score"</note> between them,¹ as Saint Caillin himself explains after listing the previous kings in the poem which follows—namely, "Noble Ireland, Island of Angels," <note>original: *ut dixit*</note> as he said:

> "One king and seven score kings,
> I tell you without difficulty,
> From Sláine to jovial Diarmait,
> Is the number of kings who possessed Ireland."

It was after this that Caillin spoke (that is, after naming those kings according to the gift of prophecy <note>original: "explanation"</note> he had received from the Angel): "Since I have therefore reckoned," he said, "every king who ruled Ireland from Sláine to this time—that is, to Diarmait Mac Cerbhaill—I will now do something more difficult² and incomprehensible than that. Specifically, I will relate the name of every king who will rule Ireland from this day until the Doom <term>the Doom: the Day of Judgment; original: *bráth*</term>—that is, from Diarmait until the end of time," said Saint Caillin.³ However obscure and incomprehensible things they neither see nor hear may be to the people of the world—even those certain things fated and in store for them—it was no less easy or plain for Caillin to list the kings who ruled Ireland during and before his own time than the kings who would come after him, because they had been explained to him by the Angel, to whom everything is possible and easy.

He then spoke of the kings who would rule Ireland from the time of Diarmait until the Doom,⁴ namely:

**Diarmait Mac Cerbhaill**, first.

**Domhnall and Fergus**, two sons of Muirchertach Mac Erca, who ruled for one year (or three years).⁵ They died.⁶

**Baetán**, son of Muirchertach, and **Eochaidh**, son of Domhnall, who ruled for three years.⁷

***

<margin>Note 5</margin> Fergus and Domhnall, the two sons of Muirchertach (son of Muiredach, son of Eoghan, son of Niall of the Nine Hostages), reigned for three years. Other authorities allow them only one year. See note ⁷.

<column-break/>

⁶ <note>died: original: *ecc atbathatar*; literally "they died a death."</note>

⁷ <note>years: The letters "c. n." prefixed in the original manuscript indicate that the kings mentioned were of the *Clann Néill*, the descendants of Niall of the Nine Hostages.</note>

$^1$ *Domhnall.*—A marginal note adds that he was the <term>first king of the Clann Cholmáin</term> <note>original: cēd riġ cloinne Colmain</note>, a sept <note>term: a division of a family or tribe</note> descended from Colman (slain A.D. 558, according to the *Chronicle of the Scots* <note>original: Chron. Scotorum</note>), son of Diarmait, son of Fergus Cerrbheoil, son of Conall Cremthainn, son of Niall of the Nine Hostages. "Clann Cholmáin" was the tribe name of the powerful family of the O'Melaghlins of Meath and Westmeath. The chronicles mention 17 Irish monarchs from the Clann Cholmáin sept.

$^2$ *Frosach:* "The Showery."—He was called this because three remarkable showers (of silver, blood, and honey) are said to have fallen in Ireland in the year of his birth, A.D. 716; and three more (of silver, wheat, and honey) are recorded as having

<column-break/>

fallen in Inishowen, County Donegal, in the first year of his sovereignty (A.D. 763, according to the *Annals of the Four Masters*). He was of the Clann Néill, as the letters "c. n." attest.

$^3$ *Donnchad.*—Of the Clann Cholmáin (c. col.). See note $^1$.

$^4$ *Caille.*—This should probably be "Caillne," as the title is derived from the name of a river (the Callann) in which Niall is recorded to have drowned in A.D. 844. See the *Annals of the Four Masters* for that year. O'Donovan (cited above) wrongly supposes it to be the King's River in County Kilkenny.

This entry concludes folio 4, a. 2, on the lower margin of which is a stanza addressed to a person named Guaire, a specimen of pedantry not worth reproducing here.

$^5$ *Flann.* — Called Flann of the Shannon <note>original: Sinna</note>. He belonged to the <term>Clann Cholmáin</term> <note>original: c. c.</note>, who were the descendants of Colman the Great.

$^6$ *Congalach.* — The letters <note>original: r. a. r.</note> indicate that he was of the *Slicht Aedha Sláine* (the lineage or <term>sept</term> of Aed Sláine), as the genealogy provided above confirms.

$^7$ *Domnall.* — Of the <term>Clann Néill</term> <note>original: c. n.</note>.

$^8$ *Maelsechlainn.* — This name is composed of *Mael* (meaning "bald" or "tonsured servant" <note>original: calvus</note>) and *Sechlainn*, the possessive form of *Sechlann*. This is a rearrangement of the name *Sechnall*, or Saint Sechnall, the founder of the Church of Dunshaughlin in County Meath. The name means "Servant of Saint Sechnall." The name was pronounced "Melaghlin" and is now 

<column-break/>

Anglicized as the first name Malachy, or the surnames McLoughlin or Loughlin. The McLoughlins, or O'Melaghlins, were the senior branch of the <term>Southern Uí Néill</term> <note>original: Hy-Neill</note>, who lived in the modern counties of Meath and Westmeath, along with part of County Dublin.

$^9$ *Brian:* that is — Brian Boru <note>original: Borumha</note>, who deposed his predecessor, Maelsechlainn the Great, in the year 1001. He appears to have attempted to depose him in the previous year, for the *Chronicle of the Scots* <note>original: Chronicon Scotorum</note> records, under the year 998 (actually 1000), the "first turning of Brian and the men of Connacht against Maelsechlainn." See Todd's *The War of the Gaels with the Foreigners* <note>original: Cogadh Gaedhel re Gallaibh</note>, introduction, pages 154–155.

<meta>This page continues the list of the High Kings of Ireland, moving from the reign of Brian Boru and the restoration of Maelsechlainn II into the period of the "Kings with Opposition" before the Anglo-Norman invasion.</meta>

Maelsechlainn again <note>original: iterum; this refers to Maelsechlainn II reclaiming the throne after the death of Brian Boru at the Battle of Clontarf in 1014.</note>.
Turlough O'Conor (king with opposition <note>original: rí con fhressabra</note>)
Muirchertach, son of Niall.
Rory the Great O'Conor, king without opposition.

Caillin then recounted things that had not yet happened, which neither historians nor poets knew—specifically, the kings who would rule Ireland from Rory O'Conor until the end of time. And it was not only a vision and a prophecy by Caillin regarding the kings who had not yet come; rather, it was through the truth and the prophecy of the angel that Caillin foretold every king who would hold Ireland from Dermot, son of Cerball <margin>folio 5, b. 2.</margin> until the end of time. For it was during his contemporary lifetime with Dermot that Caillin composed the poem:

->Ireland, great island of angels.<- <note>original: heri oll oilen aingeal</note>

Caillin recounted fifty-nine kings who would take the kingship of Ireland from the time of Dermot until the end of time; all those kings were foretold through prophecy and vision. For Caillin also recounted seven score <note>140</note> kings from the time of Flainge to Dermot.

***

<note>Note 1</note> *Again*; that is—after Brian's death at the Battle of Clontarf in 1014.

<note>Note 2</note> *With opposition.* — *Fressabra* (from *fris*, "against him," and *abrad*, "to speak"), meaning a king whose authority was not generally acknowledged by all provinces. The order of succession to the throne of Ireland, from the death of Maelsechlainn II (the Great) to the accession of Rory O'Conor, is somewhat uncertain. The historians of Munster and Connacht maintain that Turlough O'Conor was undoubtedly king of Ireland, but the northern writers deny him that dignity. The historian Keating states that Donnchad, son of Brian; Turlough, son of Tadhg; and Muirchertach, son of Turlough, reigned in turn after Maelsechlainn II, but he adds "only in the kingship of the Southern Half <note>original: Leth-Mogha</note> and the greater part of Ireland." Muirchertach is indeed styled the "glorious king of Ireland" by Anselm; and Dr. Lynch <note>referencing John Lynch’s *Cambrensis Eversus* (Cambrensis Overthrown), 1662</note> contends that at least Turlough and Muirchertach are entitled to rank among the supreme monarchs of Ireland. See *Cambrensis Eversus* vol. II. Dublin, 1850; pp. 45-9.

The historians of Leinster assert that Dermot Mac Maelnambo, the great-grandfather of Dermot Mac Murrough, was king of Ireland after Donnchad, son of Brian. His claims are put forward in the *Book of Leinster*, a 12th-century manuscript in Trinity College, Dublin. Speaking of "kings with opposition," the writer says: "Thus are 'kings with opposition' reckoned in the regal list: if the king be of the Northern Half <note>original: Leth-Chuind</note>, and he has *all* of the Northern Half plus one province of the Southern Half <note>original: Leth-Mogha</note>, that man is king of Tara and of Ireland 'with opposition.' If he is of the Southern Half, however, he is not called king of Ireland unless he have all...

<summary>This page discusses the later High Kings of Ireland and the prophetic list attributed to Saint Caillin, while explaining the historical concept of a "King with Opposition" (rí con fhressabra) during periods of contested rule.</summary>
<keywords>Maelsechlainn II, Turlough O'Conor, Rory O'Conor, Saint Caillin, Kings with Opposition, Leth-Chuind, Leth-Mogha, Irish Prophecy</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the High Kings of Ireland, moving from the restoration of the throne to the historical-mythological prophecies attributed to St. Caillin of Fenagh.</meta>

Maelsechlainn again <note>original: Maelsechlainn II; this refers to his second reign after the death of Brian Boru in 1014</note>.¹
Turlough O'Conor, (king with opposition) <note>original: rí con fhressabra; a king whose authority was not recognized by all the provinces of Ireland</note>.²
Muirchertach, son of Niall.³
Rory the Great O'Conor, (king without opposition).

St. Caillin afterwards listed what had not yet come to pass, and what neither historians nor poets knew—namely, the kings who would rule Ireland from the time of Rory O'Conor until the end of time. And it was not only⁴ a prophecy and prediction on the part of Caillin to identify these future kings, but it was also through the knowledge and prediction of the Angel that Caillin foretold every king who would rule Ireland from Dermot, son of Cerball, until the end of time. For it was during the lifetime of Dermot, son of Cerball, that Caillin composed the poem:

->“Noble Ireland, Isle of Angels”<-

Caillin calculated that fifty-nine kings would reign in Ireland from the time of Dermot until the end of time. He listed all those kings through prophecy and spiritual vision. Caillin counted one hundred and forty-one kings <note>original: one king over seven score</note> from Slainge <note>the mythical first king of the Fir Bolg</note> to Dermot.

***

*Mogha's Half <note>original: Leth-Mogha, the southern half of Ireland</note>, and Tara with its territories, and the second province of Conn’s Half <note>original: Leth-Chuind, the northern half of Ireland</note> added to it. Mac Maelnambo <note>Dermot Mac Mail na mBo, King of Leinster</note> was thus king of Ireland, for he held all of Mogha's Half, and Connacht, and the men of Meath, and the people of Ulster and Oriel. It was by him that Donnchad, son of Brian Boru, was driven across the seas.* <margin>Folio 13, a. 2. See *Cambrensis Eversus*, Vol. II., p. 39.</margin>

³ *of Niall*; that is—of Niall Mac Lochlainn, one of the northern Uí Néill.

⁴ *alone*. The original Irish word is *namá*. More correctly it is *nam-má*, meaning "only" or "alone" <note>original Latin: tantum, solum</note>, which the scholar Ebel would interpret as *na-n-má*, "that it be no more" <note>original Latin: ut non sit magis</note>. It is now obsolete as an adverb, its place being taken by *amain*, which O’Donovan considers its actual equivalent. He probably regarded *amain* (though he does not explicitly say so) as formed from *nama* by the process which the scholar Cormac characterizes as *delidin*, or "inversion of letters"—a process by which the word *fer* (man) is converted into *ref*. But the ancient form *namma* (in which the 'm' is doubled) is opposed to this theory; and Herr Ebel’s suggestion seems more likely to be correct, especially as he gives an instance in the place cited where the form is *nanmá*.

<column-break/>

⁵ *one king over seven score* (141). This matches the calculation of the historian O'Flaherty. Of the entire list, 136 were Pagan. See O'Flaherty’s curious classification of the various ways they lost their lives in his book *Ogygia*, page 420. Only 17 of them died a natural death.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of St. Caillin’s prophetic poem, specifically calculating the total number of Irish kings and explaining the religious motives behind his writing.</meta>

...in his poem <note>original: ina duain</note>. These are nine score and nineteen kings <note>a total of 199 kings</note> in all, without excluding Slainge <note>original: can athgabail Slainge; Slainge was the first king of the Fir Bolg</note>, as Caillin said in his poem, namely:

> Nine score kings plus nineteen,
> From Slainge until the Judgment—it is no lie,
> This is what I have accurately counted:
> The number of kings who ruled Ireland.

If you wish to know the truth of why Caillin numbered these kings, this is the reason: to increase the honor, dignity, and protection of his city and his religious establishment <term>conghbail</term>, his church, and his high sanctuary <term>nemed</term> until the end of the world—namely, Fenagh of Moy-Rein <note>the monastery of Fenagh in County Leitrim</note>. For there is no king, nor chieftain of noble rank, nor landowner <term>brughaidh</term>, nor provider <note>original: biatach; a person responsible for hospitality</note>, nor master-scholar <note>original: ollam</note>, nor great poet in Ireland who is not descended from one of those kings; thus, they would all continue to honor his city after his time—namely, Fenagh of Moy-Rein.

There are also other matters spoken of in this poem composed by Caillin, son of Niata, the subjects of which are the matters mentioned above: the invasions previously described, and the succession of kings from Slainge, son of Dela, son of Loth, to Dermot, son of Cerball. He also prophesied every king who would rule Ireland from then until the end of time.

a fol. 6, a. 1.
</margin>

->Great Ireland, island of angels,<-
->A place of dignity for the primary affairs;<-

<column-break/>

¹ *without including. can athgabail.* *Ath-gabhail*, as a law term, means "distraint," "reprisal," or "withernam" <note>the legal act of seizing goods to settle a debt</note>. But in this text, it is certainly used in the sense of "including."

² *habitation. conghbail* = *con-gabhail*, "co-occupation" or "co-possession;" from *con*, "together," and *gabhail*, "taking." Wrongly explained by some commentators <note>original: glossarists</note> as meaning *Convallis* <note>original Latin: a valley</note>. With the adjective *nua* <note>new</note> prefixed, it forms *Nuachongbhail* (Anglicized as Nohoval or Noghovail), which is the name of several places in Ireland.

<column-break/>

³ *sanctuary.*—The word *nemed* is explained as *sacellum* <note>original Latin: a small shrine or chapel</note> in the 8th-century manuscript cited by Zeuss (where he gives the Gaulish forms *nemeton* and *vernemetis*, meaning "a great temple" <note>original Latin: fanum ingens</note>). Dr. O'Donovan gives several traditional meanings from Irish manuscripts in which the word signifies "musician," "carpenter," "smith," "cow," etc.; but in these cases, the idea of protection or privilege seems to be the core meaning.

⁴ *brughaidh*; i.e. "landholder," or "farmer."

<meta>This page continues the discussion of St. Caillin’s prophetic poem regarding the 199 kings of Ireland, explaining that his purpose for listing them was to secure the future prestige and protection of his monastery at Fenagh by linking it to the ancestry of all Irish nobility.</meta>

...announced in his poem <note>original: Lay</note>. There are 199 kings <note>nine score and nineteen</note> in total, including¹ Slainge, as Caillin said in his poem, specifically:
> Nine score kings and nineteen,
> From Slainge until the Last Judgment <note>original: doom</note>—this is no lie;
> According to my diligent calculations,
> This is the number of kings who shall rule Ireland.

Therefore, if one wishes to know why Caillin listed those kings, this is the true reason: to increase the honor, respect, and protection for his city and dwelling,² and for his church and high sanctuary,³ until the end of the world—namely, Fenagh of Moy-Rein <note>original: Fidhnacha of Magh-Rein</note>. This is because there is no king, chief, nor person of noble rank—no large landowner <term>brughaidh</term>,⁴ no food-provider <term>biatach</term>,⁵ no master scholar <term>ollamh</term>,⁶ nor eminent poet in Ireland—who is not descended from one of those kings. [He intended] for them all to honor his city after his death, specifically Fenagh of Moy-Rein.

This poem composed by Caillin, son of Niata, also discusses other matters regarding the previous⁷ events mentioned: the history of colonization <term>gabhaltus</term>⁸ up to this point, and the list of kings from Slainge (son of Dela, son of Loth) to Diarmait Mac Cerbhaill. Furthermore, he prophesied every king who would rule Ireland until the end of time.

However, we think it would be tedious to dwell here on everything the poem mentions; we will relate those details elsewhere in this book, further on <note>original: <italic>infra</italic></note>.⁹ This is the poem:

⁶ <term>ollamh</term> (pronounced *ollave*). The chief professor of any discipline was called an <term>ollamh</term> of that field.

⁷ *foregoing*. <term>anuas</term>; literally "from above."

⁸ <term>gabhaltus</term>. Meaning "occupation," "invasion," or "colonization." The word is derived from <term>gabhail</term>, meaning the act of taking or assuming.

...was called Mac Cecht, "son of the plough," because he had deified the plough; and the name "Mac Greine" was given to Cethor, from the sun (*grian*), which was his god. During their reign, around <term>A.M.</term> <note>original: "Anno Mundi," Latin for "Year of the World," a dating system starting from the biblical Creation.</note> 2934, the Milesian colony arrived in Ireland, according to Irish legendary history.

<margin>1 *Without conflict* [cin traitt]. *Cin* is for *cen*, meaning "without"; and *traitt* (or *traid*) means a quarrel or conflict. Conall is usually called Conall Cael, or "the Slender." He reigned together with his brother Cellach from A.D. 642 to 654, and alone from 654 to 658.</margin>

<margin>2 *Also* [and]; literally "there." [A.]</margin>

<margin>3 *Fedhlim*. A marginal note, most likely added by the 17th-century scholar Thady O'Rody, states: "That man is not in the successions <note>original: "atgabalaib"</note> of the kings, except according to Caillin alone." The Fedhlim in</margin>

question was Fedhlim Mac Crimthainn, King of Munster (died A.D. 847), whom the Munster historians claim was the monarch of Ireland. However, the Northern writers deny him this honor. <margin>4 *Maelsechnaill*. See note 6.</margin>

<margin>5 *Brian of Banba*. This refers to Brian Boru. He is called Brian of Banba, or "Brian of Ireland," as Banba is a poetic name for Ireland.</margin>

<margin>6 *Maelsechnaill*. This is the same person called "Maelsechlann" (through a swap of letters <term>metathesis</term>) in the previous line. Deposed by Brian Boru in A.D. 1002, he later returned to the throne.</margin>

->The same mighty Maelsechnaill;$^6$<-
->Turlough, <note>original: "Toirdelbhach"</note> stout Muirchertach,<-
->And Ruaidhrí, lord of Ireland.<-

->The brown-faced Osgamuin of Dabhall.$^{12}$<-

***

He returned to the throne in 1014, after Brian's death at the Battle of Clontarf, and held it until his own death in the year 1022.

$^7$ *Derg-donn*. This and the ten names that follow are legendary. A marginal note describes them as <note>original Irish: "do na riġaiḃ na feta-tur caċ cenmoṫa toimsiu na ruaḋ nama"</note> "of the kings whom nobody knows, except for the conjecture of the sages only." *Derg-donn* means "Red-brown." There is a prophetic poem in the *Yellow Book of Lecan* (a famous medieval Irish manuscript), column 908, attributed to Finnachta, a king of Connacht in the 9th century, in which *Donn-derg* ("Brown-red") is mentioned in a list of future kings of Connacht.

$^8$ *Long Hand*. <note>original Irish: "lamh fada"</note> The indi-

<column-break/>

vidual indicated by this title has not been identified. It may be a nickname <note>original: "epithet"</note> for the Aedh mentioned in the preceding line.

$^9$ *Gray-chest*. <note>original Irish: "cliabh glas"</note> This may also be a nickname for Aedh.

$^{10}$ *Crissalach*. This name means "dirty girdle." It is undoubtedly legendary <note>original: "apocryphal"</note>.

$^{11}$ *Another Sraiptine*. The son of Cairbre Lifechar, son of Cormac Mac Airt, was called Fiacha Sraiptine. See note 3 on page 35, previously <note>original: "ante"</note>.

$^{12}$ *Dabhall*. This was the ancient name of the River Blackwater, which flows between the counties Armagh and Tyrone and empties into Lough Neagh.

From this lineage is derived the family name of O'Kennedy of Ormond, a clan <note>original: "sept"</note> thus entitled to be considered as senior to the family of O'Brien.

<note>1</note> *Tighernan's wife.* The famous Derbforgaill <note>also known as Dervorgilla</note>, daughter of O'Melaghlin, king of Meath. Her alleged abduction by Diarmait Mac Murchadha, king of Leinster, is asserted to have led to his expulsion from his kingdom of Leinster, and to the subsequent invasion of Ireland by the Anglo-Normans on his behalf. O'Melaghlin: The ruling family of Meath. Leinster: One of the four provinces of Ireland. Meath: An ancient kingdom and province in central Ireland. Anglo-Normans: The medieval English invaders. ->The homes of the Foreigners, after that,<-

->Tighernan, though his companions are numerous;<-

O’Rody was correct in describing Tighernan O'Rourke as *Caech*, or “one-eyed.” Giraldus Cambrensis also calls him “One-eyed” <note>original Latin: *Monoculus*</note> (*The Conquest of Ireland* <note>original Latin: *Hibernia Expugnata*</note>, book 1, chapter 1). Queen Dervorgilla died in Mellifont Abbey in <term>A.D.</term> 1193, in the 85th year of her age; so that at the date of her alleged elopement with Dermot MacMurrough in 1152, she was 44 years old, while the immoral <note>original: "profligate"</note> Dermot was 62!

² *Conn.* Conn of the Hundred Battles, killed by Tibraide Tirech in <term>A.D.</term> 212.

<column-break/>

³ *Uisnech.* See note ², page 62, above <note>original Latin: *ante*</note>. Dervorgilla is here called the Queen of Uisnech, in accordance with a well-known practice observed by Irish writers of identifying princes by the names of famous places within their territories.

⁴ *The King of Leinster.* Diarmait Mac Murchadha <note>Dermot MacMurrough</note>.

⁵ *Tlachtga.* This was the ancient name for the Hill of Ward, near Athboy, county Meath, on which there is a remarkable earthen fort, said to have been built by King—

¹ <margin>Ualgarg</margin> *Ualgarg.* Ualgarg O'Rourke, son of Cathal, who was brother to Tighernan. From this name Ualgarg (<term>Anglicized as Ulrick</term>), now obsolete as a Christian name, is derived the surname Magolrick (meaning "Son of Ualgarg"), borne by a collateral branch of the O'Rourkes, whose descendants are now very numerous in Leitrim and Cavan.
² *Win.* <note>original: brisfid</note>; literally "will break."
³ <margin>Sliabh-Cairbre</margin> *Sliabh-Cairbre.* The ancient name of the wild, mountainous district on the

<column-break/>

northern boundary of the present county of Longford.
⁴ <margin>Crannagh</margin> *Crannagh.* See notes 6 and 7 on page 77.
⁵ *Thirty years.* <note>original: 10. mbliaona 20</note> (Ten years and twenty).
⁶ *Fruitful.* The ancient Irish believed that the abundance of the land and sea, as well as the weather, were directly governed by the moral character and legitimacy of their princes. A "just" king brought prosperity, while a "false" king brought famine. This same belief was common among many ancient Eastern nations. See O'Donovan's edition of the *Battle of Magh-Ratha*, page 100.
⁷ *Foes.* <note>original: namdib</note>. A marginal note reprehends... <unclear>text cuts off</unclear>

<meta>This page concludes the prophecy regarding Ualgarg O'Rourke, detailing his military successes, his thirty-year reign, and his eventual peaceful death as a pilgrim. It also includes a critical note from a later commentator, Thady O'Rody, regarding the poetic meter.</meta>

<language>English, Irish</language>
<page-num>69</page-num>

->He who avenges the fierce hero is<-
->His brother's son, Ualgarg.$^{1}$<-
->This Ualgarg will nobly win$^{2}$<-
->Two victories on the same day:<-
->The defeat at Sliabh-Cairbre,$^{3}$ fiercely;<-
->The defeat at Crannagh$^{4}$ of the true contests.<-
->From that time forward, many will be the victories<-
->Which the active Ualgarg shall gain,<-
->In assuming power, West and East,<-
->Over the Foreigners <note>original: Galls; specifically the Anglo-Normans</note>, and over the Irish <note>original: Gaedhil</note>.<-
->For thirty years,$^{5}$ without sorrow,<-
->Ualgarg will hold strong sovereignty.<-
->It will be a firm, spirited reign;<-
->It will be fruitful$^{6}$ and profitable.<-
->Against his foes$^{7}$ he will be a plunderer,<-
->He will be illustrious and generous;<-
->He will be joyful and fond of feasting;<-
->He will be rich in cavalry$^{8}$ and fond of chess.$^{9}$<-
->It is a blessing for the strong Ualgarg,<-
->That he will become a famous pilgrim;$^{10}$<-
->Though his offenses were many, West and East,<-
->By the sword <note>original: with iron</note>, still, he will not be killed.<-

---

<columns>2</columns>

The poet Caillin was criticized for a fault in his meter. <term>“That is a flawed stanza, Caillin. Is it not a great shame that Caillin should be guilty of a flawed stanza? By the hand of my god-parent <note>original: gossip; from the Old English 'god-sib' meaning a spiritual relative</note>, I do not like it. However, there is no remedy for it.” <note>original Irish: Is camrann sin a Ċaillin. Naċ mor an naire Ċaillin abeiṫ cionntaċ a gcamrann marso. Laṁ mo ċairdes Crisṫ ni maiṫ lem e, giḋ naċ bfuil araċ air.</note></term> The critic was Thady O'Rody, who probably could not decipher the first word of the stanza, 

<column-break/>

owing to the shape of the initial letter, and therefore accused Caillin unjustly.

$^{8}$ *rich in cavalry*. <term>marc-shluaghach <note>original: marcṡluaġaċ</note></term>; literally, "possessing a multitude of horses."

$^{9}$ *fond of chess*. <term>fidhchellach <note>original: fiḋċellaċ</note></term>. This is properly an adjective derived from <term>fidhchell <note>original: fiḋċell</note></term>, "chess"; but it is not easy to translate with one word, unless one could say "chess-like" or "chess-playing."

$^{10}$ *pilgrim*. The *Annals of the Four Masters* record that Ualgarg O'Rourke, lord of Breifne, died in 1231 while on a pilgrimage to the River Jordan.

¹ **Occupying.** <note>original: ic gaḃail</note>. The preposition "after" <note>original: iarn</note> is written over "occupying" <note>original: ic</note> as a variation, meaning "after occupying." At the end of this stanza, the scholar Thady O’Rody adds the note, "that stanza was not very clear to me" <note>original: ni maiṫ ra bu leir dam in rann roin</note>. ¹ Extravagance. <term>forbond</term> <note>original: "fbond" or "forbann"</note> is explained as "bad or false law" by O’Donovan in the Supplement to O’Reilly’s dictionary. However, it seems to mean "excess" or "extravagance," being composed of the words *for* ("over") and *band* ("a leap" or "step"). <note>original: "super" and "saltus"</note>

² Lough Allen. <note>original: "Loch-Aillinne"</note> A lake in County Leitrim. The murder of Aedh, son of Domhnall, son of Ferghal O'Rourke, is recorded in the *Annals of the Four Masters* under the year 1226.

⁵ Blue William. <note>original: "Uilliam Gorm"</note> "Whoever he was, I do not know," <note>original: "Ce be he nescio"</note> adds the scribe Thady O’Rody in the margin. William Gorm was the son of Hugh de Lacy by his second wife, the daughter of Ruaidhri O'Conor, the last High King of Ireland. For marrying her without the license of King Henry II, De Lacy was dismissed from the office of Viceroy in 1181. He is called "the king's representative" <note>original: "fer inad an rig"</note> or Viceroy below <note>original: "infra"</note>, but he never actually held that title.

Brown-haired Cathal will not be deposed—
I certify this to you, without exaggeration. <note>original: "extravagance"</note>
The fate of that Aedh <note>Aedh is a common Irish royal name, often anglicized as Hugh.</note> afterwards,
I shall certainly tell you:
The wealthy <note>original: "opulent"</note> king will be slain
By treachery, on Loch Allen. <note>original: "Loch-Aillinne," a lake on the River Shannon.</note>
In that time, truly, <note>original: "without falsehood"</note>
The Foreigners <note>likely referring to the Vikings or Anglo-Normans.</note> will ruin my fair church,
After the fair, famous Cathal
Who will slay Aedh in treachery.
Stoutly will I avenge
My church upon the Foreigners;
For it is a burning grief to me this day
That William Gorm <note>William "the Blue" or "the Swarthy." Likely a reference to William de Burgh or another Norman invader.</note> should profane me.
I will grant, without deceit,
And the noble saints of Ireland also,
That William Gorm shall not obtain, thereafter,
Power over the Gaels <note>original: "Gaedhil," the native Irish people.</note> until the Day of Judgment. <note>original: "until doom."</note>

<margin>Written here is a note giving the writer's view of an enemy's love:

"An enemy's love is here for you,
If you would listen to me a little:
We were not born with you,
Nor shall we be buried with you." <note>original: "Sepe mbioba innra duit / dianertithea limra beg; / rec ni rogenainn let / ir ni haonaicrep maille let."</note>

Another note is a copy of the verses about Cuchuimne, printed in Todd's edition of the *Book of Hymns*, part II, pp. 139, 144, 155.

"Cuchuimne, <note>An 8th-century Irish monk and scholar known for his work on canon law.</note>
Read learned works halfway;
The other half of his task
He abandoned for hags. <note>original: "Cuicuimni, ro lez ruite co opuimni; a leth all hi anata no lega pop cailecha."</note>

"Happy was it for Cuchuimne,
That he did not stop until he was a sage;
He abandoned his hags;
He read the rest while he lived." <note>original: "An do Coimcuimni rombui, ir ni rualao de conao rui; no leig a cailecha i raill, no lez apall apazmbui."</note></margin>

# The Fifth Law

<margin>Ualgarg took the kingship.</margin>
God ordained the fifth law
When Ualgarg took his turn;
No nephew shall take from him his inheritance,
Because he does not struggle against hardships. <note>original: "ni rettra re hruilib"; likely referring to his undisputed right to rule or his strength in maintaining order.</note>

Seven years and two decades,
And two hundred years, <note>This likely refers to a chronological marker from a significant past event, such as the death of Brian Boru.</note>
From the time Brian was killed—great the field of battle—
Until Ualgarg finished his reign.

Twelve kings of the lineage of the man, <note>original: "do gil in fir"; referring to the ancestral line of the Ua Ruairc family.</note>
Ualgarg Ua Ruairc from Cruachan, <note>Cruachan was the ancient capital of Connacht, signifying high status.</note>
Who took the high-kingship firmly
Over the people of Breifne <term>Breifne: a medieval kingdom in Ireland comprising modern-day Leitrim and Cavan</term> in general.

Though he takes a portion of the land,
A... <note>The text breaks off at the catchword "A".</note>

<summary>This page records the reign of Ualgarg Ua Ruairc over Breifne, noting that he ruled for twenty-seven years and tracing the timeline from the death of Brian Boru.</summary>
<keywords>Ualgarg Ua Ruairc, Breifne, Irish Kingship, Cruachan, Brian Boru, Genealogy</keywords>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the prophetic or historical account of the O'Rourke (O'Ruaire) dynasty and their rule over the kingdom of Breifne.</meta>

Then will be the fifth occasion
On which Ualgarg will assume his rule.
Power after that he will not obtain;
For he could not, on account of his eyes. <note>This likely refers to blinding, a common practice in medieval Ireland to render a claimant ineligible for kingship.</note>
Seven years, and twice ten, <note>original: *ocur oa deich*. The translator notes this calculation may be an error in the original manuscript.</note>
And two hundred years,
From the killing of Brian—great the woe—
Until Ualgarg's reign ceased.

<margin>Cruachan. Not Cruachan, or Rathcroghan, in County Roscommon (the ancient seat of the kings of Connacht); but probably Croghan, in the barony of Tullyhunco, County Cavan, adjoining Leitrim.</margin>
Twelve kings of the seed of the man,
Of Ualgarg O'Rourke from Cruachan,
Will stoutly obtain chief sovereignty
Over the Breifnians <note>The people of the Kingdom of Breifne, modern-day Leitrim and Cavan.</note> in general.
Whatever part of the land each one gets,
I say to you, knowingly,
It is not right firmly to reckon him
In the royal roll of Ireland.

<margin>Cathal Find. "Cathal the Fair." This man seems to have been an O’Reilly, and the same person referred to previously as having slain Aedh, son of Domhnall O’Rourke, on Lough Allen in the year 1226. Annad, the name of Cathal’s father, was a common Christian name in the O’Reilly family, but not in the O’Rourke family.</margin>
After that, Conchobhar, grandson of Domhnall,
Will joyously obtain the kingship;
Until he is subsequently dethroned by
Cathal Find, son of Annad.
Cathal does not obtain the full sovereignty,
(I say that it will be a true story);

<margin>West and East. In other words, on both sides of the kingdom.</margin>
But the power of the Breifnians, West and East,
Shall be his as long as he lives.

<note>The text calculates the end of Ualgarg's reign to the year 1241. However, his death is entered in the *Annals of the Four Masters* under 1231. Probably instead of "and twice ten," we should read "and ten," which would make the calculation correct.</note>

<summary>This page details the succession of the O'Rourke and O'Reilly families over the Kingdom of Breifne, specifically focusing on the reigns of Ualgarg and Conchobhar and the rise of Cathal Find.</summary>
<keywords>Ualgarg O'Rourke, Breifne, Cathal Find O'Reilly, Irish Kingship, Genealogy, Tullyhunco, Lough Allen, Annals of the Four Masters</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the prophetic poem regarding the historical conflicts and successions in the region of Leitrim and Connacht, specifically focusing on the interactions between Irish chieftains and the Anglo-Norman invaders.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>

Connor shall be restored
By that Cathal without error;
And in his time shall come clearly
The visitation of Conall <note>original: "Cuairt Chonaill"; this refers to the traditional tribute or dues paid to the descendants of Conall Gulban, the Cinel-Conaill of Donegal</note> to my church.
During the reign of that Connor,
And of Donnell of the bright Ross <note>original: "in roir reil"; likely referring to Ross in County Leitrim</note>,
That famous Cathal shall give
The kingship to the "Hound" <note>original: "von Choin"; "The Hound" is a common epithet for Irish warriors, here referring to a specific chieftain</note>, who was his brother.
In the time of that "Hound," without contempt,
William <note>original: "Uilliam"; William de Lacy, an Anglo-Norman lord</note> shall come across the sea;
By him, foreigners <note>original: "Goill"; the Anglo-Normans</note> shall be brought into the land,
And a defeat shall be inflicted at Crannachain.
Therefore, I predict here
That the place shall be called Crannachain,
From the "crannagh" <note>original: "erannaiz"; a structure or barricade made of trees/wood</note> that shall be placed there,
From the bog to the river.
<margin>folio 8, a</margin>
<margin>2.</margin>
For "Blue" William <note>original: "Uilliam gorm"; William de Lacy</note>, after that defeat,
For three weeks certainly,
In Meath, from the poison of his wounds,
The strength of the warrior shall be exhausted.
While that "Hound" is building churches,
They shall plunder your lands from you;

<margin>Both sides of the mountains of Slieve-an-Iarainn, County Leitrim.</margin>
<margin>1. See note 4, page 75.</margin>
<margin>2. *Tribute of Conall*; that is, the dues or visitation fees of the Cinel-Conaill, or descendants of Conall Gulban, son of Niall of the Nine Hostages, whose possessions included Tir-Conaill ("the land of Conall"), the present county of Donegal.</margin>
<margin>3. *Ross*. There are two places named Ross in the barony of Dromahaire, County Leitrim; one of which is probably meant here.</margin>
<margin>4. *The Cu*. Literally "the Hound"; a word frequently employed in the composition of Irish proper names, such as Cu-Chonnacht ("Hound of Connacht"), Cu-Ulad ("Hound of Ulster"), etc.</margin>
<margin>5. *William*. The William Gorm, or William de Lacy, mentioned above (page 73) and also in the second next stanza. Professor O’Curry mistook the identity of this William, whom he considered to have been the same as William Ruadh O’Rourke who died in 1430; about which time the Professor thought that this poem (or "very glaring forgery," as he calls it) had been written.</margin>

<summary>This page recounts a prophecy concerning the reigns of Connor and Cathal, the arrival of the Anglo-Norman William de Lacy, and a significant battle at Crannachain. It details the eventual death of William from his wounds in Meath and the subsequent plundering of Irish lands.</summary>

That "Hound" will be a builder of churches.

<summary>This page contains a prophetic poem concerning the political transitions of the O'Reilly dynasty and the defeat of the Anglo-Norman knight William de Lacy at the Battle of Crandchain in 1233. --- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic prophecies and legal claims attributed to Saint Caillin regarding the rights of the church of Fenagh and the territory of the "Cú" (the Hound), referring to Tighearnan O'Rourke.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>

The Hound of the fair spear-heads said,
From the mouth of the River Lainne to the River Lemain. <note>original: "Zebard in Cu celtach cain / O bun Lainne co Lemain."</note>

During the time of this Hound—terrible was the deed—
Many people were in distress;
But there was much wealth in the east, including cattle,
Fruit, milk, and produce.
There was a heavy plague of the houses <note>original: "tenc plaici na tipte"</note>
Which the Hound was prophesying,
Unless he would place three feet <note>meaning three measures of land</note>
Of his ground in the middle of my church.
For the sake of his offering to my holy church,
Let the king give me my tribute;
The King of the Stars <note>God</note> shall give to him
Sovereignty and the fruit of the lands.
I shall proclaim with confidence
That tribute as I am entitled to it;
And if it is given to me for his prosperity,
I shall proclaim that it will not fail.
I shall proclaim for the generous Hound
Prosperity, just as I have prophesied it,
And if I bring that prosperity with me,
He shall be the noble, chosen blood of Ireland.
This is my tribute for my support,
From the race of Aedh <note>Aedh Finn</note> of the feasts:
A *screpall* <note>a silver coin or unit of value</note> from every rock until then,
And an episcopal battle-standard from every assembly.

<margin>1 From Bun-Lainne to Lemain (Leamuin, B.). In a note to his edition of the *Annals of the Four Masters* under the year A.D. 1172, Dr. O’Donovan remarks: "It is stated in the *Book of Fenagh* that Tighearnan acquired dominion over the entire region... from the sea at the borders of Ulster and Connaught to Drogheda." However, there is nothing in the manuscript to support this statement, except the limits prescribed here to the "Hound's" (Tighearnan's) possessions, or those fixed further on (page 87) as the area out of which Saint Caillin's dues were payable. Bun-Lainne seems to be the place called Bunluiny in the Down Survey for the parish of St. John's, County Sligo; and Lemain, otherwise known as Magh-Lemhna or the Closagh, was the name of a district comprising the parish of Clogher in County Tyrone.</margin>

<summary>This page contains a poem attributed to Saint Caillin detailing the territorial extent of Tighearnan O'Rourke's rule and the specific tributes owed to the church of Fenagh in exchange for spiritual protection and prosperity.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Caillin, Tighearnan O'Rourke, Fenagh, Bun-Lainne, Lemain, Irish tribute, Screpall, Aedh Finn, medieval prophecy</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues a prophetic poem regarding the kings of Ireland and the tributes owed to the church.</meta>

<page-num>79</page-num>

The mild, belted Cu <note>original: "Cu"; likely a nickname or shortened form of a name like Cu-Uladh</note> will possess
The land from Bun-Lainne to Lemain.<margin>Bun-Lainne is the name of a river, now called the Owen-Lain, which rises in the mountains of Pomeroy and falls into Lough Neagh, near the town of Stewartstown. Lemain is Lough Lemain, now Lough Leane, near Killarney. The district between these two points comprises part of the parishes of Clogher and Errigal-Keeroge, county Tyrone. See Reeves's *Colton's Visitation*, p. 126.</margin>
In the time of that Cu—woeful is the fact—
Many people will be in trouble;
In the West and East, there will be many demands for payment,
Taken from fruit, milk, and produce.
Lords will be scarce in the lands
Of this Cu, whom I am foretelling,
Unless he performs three fasts
At my shrine in the middle of my church.<margin>*my church*. <gloss>mechal</gloss>, A.</margin>
After his fasting at my holy church,
The king will give me my tribute;
I, and the King of the Stars <note>A poetic title for God</note>, will give him
Lords and produce in his land.
I will cheerfully relate<margin>*I will . . . relate*. <gloss>meram</gloss>, A.</margin>
That same tribute as it is due to me;
And the reason why it was granted to me,
I will tell besides.
I will relate to the generous Cu,
Why I have foretold his coming;
And the reason why I have enumerated<margin>*brought with me*. <gloss>tucur lem</gloss>. This should probably be rendered "enumerated;" the meaning being that the writer had brought the succession of the kings before the attention of his readers.</margin>
The noble high-kings of Ireland.
My tribute<margin>*tribute*. The words <gloss>the tribute of the race of Aedh Find</gloss> <note>original: "cam ríl Aona Find"</note> are written in the margin.</margin> to my house is,
From the race of festive Aedh Find,

<margin>* folio 8, b</margin>
<margin>1.</margin>
A horse for every horse from a friendly chieftain,
Including the head, the body, and the hand; <note>This likely refers to a complete set of horse trappings or a rider's outfit.</note>
A colt from the son of a prosperous king,
To be delivered into the hand of the tax-collector. <note>original: "thabach"</note>
I am entitled to receive from the noble queen
Her horse and her clothing;
I am entitled from the generous chieftainess
To a horse and a steed at my own disposal.
A cow from every <term>Biatagh</term> <note>A Biatagh was a high-ranking farmer or hospitaller responsible for providing food and hospitality to the chief and his retinue.</note> of a single townland,
From Drogheda <note>original: "Ath Droichet"</note> all the way to Sligo;
A captured cow from every plunder,
From every king’s son and chieftain.
The foster-child of the son of a commoner, <note>original: "mac fethan"</note>
I demand of you firmly,
If they wish for their children to be blessed,
Let them bring their tribute-visit to my monastery. <note>original: "catraig"</note>
Whoever will not pay my tribute,
As I say, to my beloved church,
Their children shall not inherit the blessing,
And they themselves shall go to hell.
Whoever will pay my tribute,
As I say, to my beloved church,
Their kindred and children shall be exalted;
They shall not suffer the neglect of their lands.

<margin>¹</margin> It was a common practice among the Irish princes to give ornamental dresses to ecclesiastics, probably in order that they might be converted into vestments. The same practice obtained also in other countries. Harold Harefoot bestowed his coronation mantle on the abbey of Croyland. (Lappenberg's *England*, vol. II., p. 227).
<term>cavalry-dress</term>. *eoach ech*. This would also mean horse-cloths, or horse trappings; but the descriptive references to “head, and leg, and hand,” seems to point to a horseman's dress.

<margin>²</margin> *into my abbot's hand*; that is, into the hand of the abbot of Fenagh, St. Caillin's successor.

<margin>³</margin> *one-bally <term>Biatagh</term>*; that is, a <term>Biatagh</term> possessed of one <term>ballybetagh</term> <note>A large ancient Irish land division, roughly 4,000 acres.</note>. The <term>Biatagh</term> was a farmer who held his land under certain conditions involving the supply of food (*biad*) to the chief and his followers.

The cavalry dress <note>original: "cavalry dress"; likely referring to the formal riding gear or military attire</note> of each noble chief,
From head to leg and hand;

A dress and a steed, at my demand.
A cow from every Biatagh <note>term: Biatagh; a class of ancient Irish landholder responsible for providing food and hospitality to travelers and the local chief</note> holding a townland,

As I command, to my holy church—
His children shall not receive their inheritance;
And they shall go to Hell.
But whosoever provides my tribute,
As I command, to my holy church—
I will save his kindred and his children:
Foreigners shall not possess their land.

<note>The extent of a Ballybetagh <note>term: A large ancient Irish land division</note> was 1,440 acres, according to ancient Irish enumeration. (See Reeves's Paper on the *Townland Distribution of Ireland*; Proceedings of the Royal Irish Academy, vol. vii., p. 474). However, Ware calculates a Ballybetagh as equal to 16 Tates, and a Tate as 60 acres; making 960 acres altogether. See Harris's *Ware*, vol. ii., p. 227.</note>

<meta>This page continues a poetic account of the miracles of Saint Caillin and the origins of the Breifnian nobility.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>

<margin>¹ *Glasraige*; that is, the tribe of Glas. The Glasraige were descended from Cairbre, son of Niall of the Nine Hostages, and were not, therefore, of the same ancestral stock as the Breifnians.</margin>
<margin>² *Aedh Dubh*. "Black Aedh (or Hugh)." He was later known as "Aedh Find" <note>Aedh the Fair</note>. From him was derived the tribe name of a division of the Breifnians, "Sil-Aedha-Find," or the race of Aedh the Fair.</margin>
<margin>³ *Fergna*. The great ancestor of all the Breifnians.</margin>
<margin>⁴ *Rioce*, or Saint Mo-Rioce, of Inis-Mic-Ualaing (or Inis-bo-finde, now Inishboffin) in Lough Ree; whose festival is recorded on August 1st in the *Martyrology of Donegal*.</margin>
<margin>⁵ *whiteness*. The note, "how Caillin made Fair Aedh out of Black Aedh, son of Fergna" <note>original: amail do finde Cailin Aed find von Aed dub mac Fergna</note>, is added in the margin. The power of changing a person's complexion was not limited to Saint Caillin; for in the lives of Saint Maedhoc of Ferns and Saint Finnchu of Brigown, a similar power is re-</margin>

The Glasraige <note>a tribal group</note> were driven out from the lineage,
By Aedh the Black, son of Fergna, the warrior;
It was the form of Rioce, who was of fair complexion,
That was bestowed upon him after his baptism.

Aedh, no longer "Black," the battle-soldier,
Was baptized without fear;
It is I <note>Saint Caillin</note> who caused this change,
Making him "Aedh the Fair-Pure," who was of sufficient whiteness.

I bestowed—it is I who deserved it—
A difference in the appearance of the two;
A tonsure <note>the shaved crown of a monk</note> was on the head of bright Rioce,
But Aedh the Fair had no tonsure at all.

This is the reason it was granted to me,
That great tribute <note>original: cuairtt; a visitation or tax paid to a monastery</note> for my service,
And that his body should be brought to my church,
Regardless of where in Ireland he might die.

Since Aedh of the Victories was baptized,
And since he was buried in my church,
He spent everything he had without theft <note>honestly</note>
For seventy-two years.

Therefore, I name the strong Hound <note>a metaphor for a warrior</note>
The strength of the kings of Ireland.

<summary>This page contains a poem describing how Saint Caillin miraculously transformed the appearance of the warrior Aedh Dubh (Black Aedh) into Aedh Find (Fair Aedh) to match the beauty of Saint Rioce, establishing a spiritual and feudal bond between the saint's church and the Breifnian lords.</summary>

1. Recorded. St. Finnchu so changed the face of Cairbre Crom, a Munster Prince (father of Aedh Caemh, King of Munster, 571–601), that he was afterwards called Cairbre Caemh, or "Cairbre the Handsome." See *Book of Lismore*, folio 72, line 1.

2. Crown. This refers to the coronal tonsure. I am Caillin of Dun-baile <note>original: Duin Daile; the ancient name of Fenagh</note>;
Great is the man who has come to my attention;
The man of the Gaels and of the Foreigners <note>original: na nGaibeal is na nSall</note>,
Who shall take the power of Ireland.
<margin>I will relate <note>original: indebaid; the manuscript notes this refers to the kings of Tirconnell</note></margin>
I will relate now, truly and well,
Of the other man who was revealed to me;
Like a branch at first he grew
From the noble, beautiful senior of Finnros <note>original: Finnros; the ancient name of the Rosses in County Donegal</note>.

The senior of Finnros shall be born, without lie,
A king whose name begins with "E" <note>original: Eicc; referring to Eignechan O'Donnell, Lord of Tirconnell, d. 1207</note>;
Good shall be the reign of the king of hostages,
Until he falls by the hand of the people of Oriel <note>original: Oirghialla; the people of Fermanagh</note>.

His son shall follow after him,
Domnall, a manly senior;
Over the land of Conall <note>Tirconnell</note> his right shall be strong,
For a period of thirty years.

The Battle of Craebh, the Battle of Camlin,
Domnall shall fight them with his people;
I tell you his prophecy,
Harsh will be the blood-shedding during that time.

He shall be wounded in the Battle of Drum,
Domnall, the chosen senior;

<margin>⁴ Finnros: This was the ancient name of the district now called the "Rosses" in the barony of Boylagh, County Donegal. Although the chief of the O'Donnell clan was styled "Lord of Finnross," it appears Finnross was the heritage of O'Furadhran (or O'Farran) in 1370, when O'Dugan wrote his Topographical Poem.</margin>
<margin>⁵ E: Eignechan O'Donnell, Lord of Tirconnell, slain by the people of Fermanagh (who were of the race of the Oirghialla) in 1207. See the Annals of the Four Masters and the Annals of Loch Cé.</margin>

I am Caillin of Dun-baile.<note>Dun-baile: Likely Dundalk, or a fort associated with the saint.</note>
Great is the knowledge that has come to my attention;

How first there will happily be born,

Good will be his career, the king of the hostages,

Domhnall of Finnros, of the spears—

<margin>folio 9, a. 1.</margin>

...he had a bed, and a wise judgment:
Conall, Eoghan, and the Oirghialla. <note>original: "Conall, Eogan, Oirgialla." These represent the major tribal groupings of Ulster: the Cinel Conaill, Cinel Eoghain, and the descendants of the three Collas.</note>
Great is the victory he brings to his cause,

Conall was the first king at Tara,
Of the children of Niall, without dispute;
He was killed at the prosperous Plain of Rein, <note>original: "Moig Rein." Magh Rein is a plain in County Leitrim.</note>
After sixteen years of fair friendship.
The marauders who came before,
After a great raid toward Tara,
Until Conall Gulban came precisely
To the Plain of Rein in pursuit.
It was there that the king was killed,
On that journey, without falsehood.

<margin>1. Conall. See note 7, p. 87.</margin>
<margin>2. Eoghan; that is, the Cinel-Eoghain, or descendants of Eoghan, son of Niall of the Nine Hostages, who have given their name to the present county of Tyrone (Tir-Eoghain).</margin>
<margin>3. Oirghialla. In the time of Domhnall Mór O'Donnell, the territorial name "Oirghialla" was applied to the district comprising the present counties of Armagh, Louth, and Monaghan. In the 5th century, the Oirghialla (who were the descendants of the three Collas) included the greater part of Ulster to the west of the Upper and Lower Bann. In recording the death of Domhnall Mór, the Four Masters <note>The "Annals of the Four Masters" is a major chronicle of medieval Irish history compiled in the 1600s.</note> state (A.D. 1241) that he was "Lord of Tirconnell, Fermanagh, and Lower Connacht as far as the Curlew Mountains, and of Oirghialla from the plain (that is, the level part of County Louth) northwards." On the lower margin of folio 8 b, some rhymes are written which are not worth printing.</note>

<note>The location mentioned in the text is probably Druim-thuama, or Drumhome, in the barony of Tirhugh, County Donegal.</note>

<summary>This page recounts the reign and death of Domhnall Mór O'Donnell, prophesying his death on a pilgrimage, and references the historical death of his ancestor Conall Gulban at Magh Rein.</summary>
<keywords>Domhnall Mór O'Donnell, Conall Gulban, Oirghialla, Cinel-Eoghain, Magh Rein, Irish Kingship, Ulster history</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the prophetic poem regarding the kings of the Northern Uí Néill and their relationship with the church at Fenagh.</meta>

<page-num>89</page-num>

He shall have, and justly have,
The territories of Conall,¹ Eoghan,² and the Oirghialla.³ <note>These represent the major kingdoms of Ulster: Tirconnell, Tirowen, and Oriel.</note>
Many a defeat besides will he inflict—
That King Domhnall of Finnros—
Until he nobly⁴ obtains the sovereignty of the North,⁵
For a full thirteen years.
Though he seeks much, east and west—
This Domhnall—taking hostages as pledges;
I, and the King of Heaven, decree for him,
That he shall die⁶ while on a pilgrimage.⁷
My blessing be upon you, Domhnall;
I prophesy your future without cause for anguish.
Give me my tribute every time,
Just as Conall Gulban once gave it.
Conall was the first king of Tara,⁸
Of the Clan-Neill, without dispute.
Until he was slain in prosperous Magh-Rein,⁹
Sixteen years he spent in happiness.
The Masraighe¹⁰ tribe went to the East
Once, on a great raid against Tara;
Whereupon Conall quickly came
To Magh-Rein in pursuit of them.
A flying spear killed the king,¹¹
On that journey, in truth.

<margin>
¹ <term>Conall</term>: The people of Tirconnell (Donegal).
² <term>Eoghan</term>: The people of Tirowen (Tyrone).
³ <term>Oirghialla</term>: The people of Oriel (parts of Armagh, Monaghan, and Louth).
⁴ Nobly: The original Irish text uses the word *sohan*.
⁵ Thirteen: See the previous note on page 87 regarding the length of his reign.
⁶ Shall die: The original text uses *a echran* for *a ecran*.
⁷ Pilgrimage: See the previous note on page 87 regarding his religious retirement.
⁸ <term>Tara</term>: This is certainly an error by the original author, as Conall Gulban was never the High King at Tara (the monarch of all Ireland).
⁹ <term>Magh-Rein</term>: The ancient name of the district where Fenagh is located. The origin of this name is explained later in the text.
¹⁰ <term>Masraighe</term>: This was a tribe of the Firbolg <note>An ancient race of settlers in Irish mythology</note> located in Magh-Slecht ("The Plain of Adoration"). This plain adjoined <term>Magh-Rein</term> to the east and was famous for containing the great gold-and-silver pagan idol <term>Crom Cruach</term>. According to tradition, St. Patrick caused this idol and its twelve bronze attendant images to sink into the ground. (See Miss Cusack's *Life of St. Patrick*, p. 399.) Dallan Forgaill, who composed the famous poem *Amhra Cholaim Chille*, was from the Masraighe of Magh-Slecht. (*Book of the Dun Cow*, p. 5 *a*).
¹¹ <term>King</term>: The original words are *oireo Conall*.
</margin>

<meta>This page continues the poetic account of the death and miraculous resurrection of Conall Gulban at Fenagh.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>

On Magh Rein <note>The Plain of the Rein, near Fenagh</note> at Dun Baile <note>original: Dun-baile; the ancient name of Fenagh</note>,
The Masraighe <note>An ancient tribe of Breifne</note> boasted of their deed <note>original: Shur chommardret; literally "so that they boasted." They had killed Conall Gulban</note>.
Conall was buried in the earth,
Within the lake <note>original: Loch-Rein or Loch-Saloch at Fenagh</note> at the fort <note>original: Dun</note>;
Five and a half years he remained there,
Until I came to the city <note>original: cathruigh; referring to the monastery at Fenagh</note>.
For a year and a half I had leisure,
Until I brought his children from the north,
And the beautiful saints of Ireland,
To perform his solemn service.
I prayed to the King of the Stars <note>A poetic term for God</note>,
And to the nobles of Ireland;
To bring great Conall back to life,
Which would be an increase to my honor.
He sent to me from heaven
His angels in my company.
I summoned Conall without delay,
In the presence of the men of Ireland.
<margin>folio 9, a 2.</margin> Conall Gulban <note>The ancestor of the Kinel-Connell (O'Donnells)</note> came then from the east,
From the grave even unto the church;
Then I blessed him and his children,
Together with the saints of Ireland.
Then I promised to them,
To the race of Conall, son of Niall,
Until the very end of the world,
That their power would be over Ireland.
Then I promised to them,
To the race of Conall of the battles.

<note>The words "death of Conall Gulban" are added in the margin of the original manuscript.</note>

The cromlech consists of several massive upright stones supporting a very large, flat capstone. The stones are rough and appear ancient, with some vegetation, including a bushy growth, on and around them. In the background, there are rolling fields or hills under a cloudy sky, and a low stone wall is visible in the middle ground.</image-desc>

Along with the glorious Saints of Ireland,
To effect his translation <note>original: tránsláide/translatio; the ceremonial moving of a body or relics to a more sacred location</note>.

To the race of valorous Conall—

Thus did fair Conall give to me
This tribute, every third year.

<meta>This page continues the poetic account of Saint Caillin’s prophecies and the establishment of tributes for his monastery, focusing on the lineage of Conall Gulban.</meta>

After him, a curse fell on each of his children
Both in Scotland <note>original: "Alba"</note> and Ireland.
Conall cursed them, without deceit,
And the noble saints of Ireland cursed them as well—
His children and his race together—
If they did not pay this tribute.
Then I myself foretold
To Conall Gulban, son of Niall,
That a renowned man should be born from his line,
Whose fame would fill Scotland and Ireland.
Thereupon he asked of me—
This Conall Gulban of the mighty deeds—
"What name do you give to the man
Who will be descended from my fair body?"
This is what I said to him:
"Columba <note>original: "Collum Cille"; literally "Dove of the Church"</note> will he be called;
His wonders and his miracles
Would be sufficient for all Ireland.
The other person who shall be descended from you—
Adamnan—his tribute will also be good.
I shall be the tutor of both of them—
I assert it here in your presence."
It was then that the life ended
Of Conall Gulban, without peril,
After he had firmly ordained for me
The tributes of Scotland and Ireland.

<margin>A curse. But it would appear from the context that Conall must have been the agent.
Columba (Colum Cille). First Abbot and founder of Iona <note>original: "Hy"</note>. He was the great-grandson of Conall Gulban (who was killed in 464 AD) and died in the year 597 AD.
Adamnan. Ninth Abbot of Iona and writer of the celebrated *Life of Saint Columba* <note>original: "Vita Sancti Columbæ"</note>, which Dr. Reeves has edited with singular ability for the Irish Archaeological and Celtic Society. He was descended in the seventh generation from Conall Gulban and died in 704 AD. Saint Caillin could not, therefore, have been his tutor, as the poem asserts, if Saints Caillin and Columba were contemporaries.</margin>

Every person of the Cenél nEógain <note>original: "Dpernech" (likely a corruption of Eoghanach)</note>, and every person of the Cenél Conaill <note>original: "Conall"; referring to the descendants of Conall Gulban in Donegal</note>,
