O Ireland, it is for you that the bishops of the end of the world are a sorrow;
Many are their hounds and their servants;
They shall not fulfill the truth.

O Ireland, it is for you that the bishops of the end of the world are a sorrow;
In the churches <note>original: "ceallaib"</note> they shall not do right;
There is discord over the altar.

O Ireland, it is for you that the bishops of the end of the world are a sorrow;
They have secret women <note>original: "mna taide"; a reference to clerical concubinage</note>,
But for the plague of the common chant.

O Ireland, it is for you that the bishops of the end of the world are a sorrow;
They are greedy, small is their hospitality;
Woe to anyone who comes to their houses.

Those bishops—pity the oath—
Unless they cast away their pride;
For themselves, it serves not perfectly,
Neither the right nor the greatness of their service.

<margin>Behold the priests <note>original Latin: "ecce sacerdotes"</note>.</margin> The priests are ruining their holy orders,
Alas, O Christ, how pitiful is the plague;
Following the track of the bishops diligently,
They defile all of Ireland.

The bad kings and the bad bishops,
They chew up the churches and the laity;

E. o.

This word ends the text of folio 9, b, in the lower margin of which are some poetical notes, only one of which is worth reproducing:

"Across the united Ui-Neill <note>The O'Neill dynasty of Ulster</note>, southwards by the side of Echtga <note>Slieve Aughty</note>,
The relics of the son of O'Crimthannan are borne to Inis-Celtra."

Echtga is Slieve Aughty, between Clare and Galway; and Inis-Celtra is in Lough Derg. The son of O'Crimthannan (or, the "son of the descendant of Crimthan")...

<summary>This page contains a Middle Irish poem lamenting the moral decay of bishops and priests in Ireland, accusing them of greed, pride, and breaking their vows. It concludes with a marginal topographical note regarding the transport of relics to Inis-Celtra.</summary>

<page-num>102</page-num>

<margin>Behold the kings.</margin>
<margin>Behold the offspring of rustics, etc.</margin>
<margin>ªFolio 10, a 2. <note>Reference to the original manuscript location.</note></margin>

God, he is the one in the dark, sick place,
A cold, vast flagstone in the bottom of Hell. <note>original: *irpinn*</note>
Since the world’s beginning,
God is restless in every path;
God, many are the lies and the betrayals;
The Harp of the Creator <note>original: *Cairroer Cruit*</note> they do not fulfill.
From Ireland, God, great is the vengeance:
The sons of the rustics <note>original: *bachlach*</note> becoming kings;
The sons of kings and great lords
Driven out across the land of Ireland.
The Judge of the world’s end,
Who comes at the world’s conclusion,
For the evil they have done in their time,
Will cast them into the fire of Hell.ª
The noble and the man of art,
The shoemaker and the stingy embellisher,
Unless they forsake lies without delay,
He will cast them into Hell.
Woe to him who hears it, O King of Heaven:
The lay-overseer of the church,
And the clergy being driven from the church;
God, it is a total vengeance upon Ireland.

<margin>e. o. o. a. <note>Likely scribal abbreviations or a refrain.</note></margin>
<margin>e.</margin>

...taken as the third person plural future of the verb *cagnam*, “I chew or masticate.” It should therefore more properly be rendered “eat,” or “consume;” but it should be understood that the substance, or stock, of the churches and territories was meant.

<margin>¹ *Cold*. *Ruar*. A curious word to apply to Hell, unless the text is corrupt.</margin>

<margin>² *Rustics*. *Bachlach*, nominative singular and genitive plural. The marginal note is valuable as fixing the actual meaning of *bachlach*, which</margin> O’Donovan translates as “shepherds” (*Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 565), and Dr. O’Conor as “keeper of cattle” <note>original: *custos boum*</note> in his edition of the same Annals in the same passage; although in his edition of Tighernach (A.D. 566), O’Conor renders the words *bachlachab bairrne* (literally, “by the rustics of Bairrin”) as “by the falling beams of the roof.” <note>original: *trabibus tecti ruentibus*</note>

<margin>³ *Stingy embellishers*. This is a conjectural translation. The word translated as ‘stingy,’ *zeanna*, should apparently be *zanna*, to rhyme with the last word of the...</margin>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues a prophetic or lamenting poem regarding the social and spiritual decay of Ireland toward the end of the world.</meta>

Their dark, fast abode shall be
The cold flagged floor of lowermost hell. <note>original: "h—l"</note>
The bad kings of the end of the world
Will be unquiet in every way.
Many will be their lies and treacheries;
The friendship of Christ they will not retain.
<margin>Behold the kings!</margin> To Ireland great the retribution will be, for
The crowning of the sons of peasants, <note>original: "rustics"</note>
<margin>Behold the spawn of peasants, etc.!</margin> And the banishment of the sons of kings,
And of stout chieftains, throughout Ireland.
Judges, and Artisans, and Smiths
Who will come towards the end of time—
For the evils they do in their day,
Shall be put into hell fire.
The Musician, and the Poet,
The tanner, and the stingy decorator— <note>original: "embellisher." The note suggests this refers to a maker of ornaments or beds.</note>
Unless they honestly forsake falsehood,
They shall be sent to hell.
Woe to him that hears, O King of Heaven,
Of a lay <term>Herenach</term> <note>A church official or steward; see the detailed note below.</note> of a church.
And the expulsion of the clerics from the church
Will be utter woe to Ireland.

<note>Regarding the preceding line: the word <term>ringeirine</term> seems to be a derivative from <term>ring</term>, which means both a bed (see Stokes's edition of *Cormac's Glossary*, p. 37, note 6), and an ornament (*Leabar Breac*, 6, a).</note>
<note>¹ The cold flagged floor of lowermost hell.</note>
<note>² The crowning of the sons of peasants.</note>
<note>³ The tanner, and the stingy decorator.</note>
<note>⁴ <term>Herenach</term>. This word, in Irish <term>airchinnech</term> <note>original: "an-chinncch, or oirchinncch"</note>, anciently signified "leader" <note>original Latin: "princeps"</note> or "provost" <note>original Latin: "præpositus"</note>; but in later times it seems to have been used for "archdeacon." (See Stokes's *Three Irish Glosses*, p. 75.) Before the complete establishment in Ireland of the diocesan system in the 12th century, the <term>Herenachs</term>, who were the stewards or farmers of the church lands, were frequently laymen. But for some time after the 12th century, these officers were generally ecclesiastics. The foregoing lament would indicate that the lay element was again intruding itself at the date of the composition of the present poem, which may be dated to about the year 1300. See Todd’s *St. Patrick*, pp. 160–162, on the duties of <term>Herenachs</term>.</note>

<meta>This page continues the poetic narrative of Caillín’s travels and his education in Rome during the era of Cormac mac Airt.</meta>

In that time—it is a pity to us—
The noble families mingled with the base-born;
In that mixed harbor from today onward,
<margin>That is, from now on.</margin> Your prosperity has vanished, O Ireland. <note>The poet laments the social upheaval and the loss of noble status through intermarriage with lower classes.</note>

Since I have related here
The news of the end of the world, <note>original: "desio in domuin"</note>
I myself will now tell from the east
The noble tidings of the Church.

It was Sinotan, my righteous tutor,
Who sent me then to Rome;
The provision my tutor gave to me
Was three hundred ounces of red gold.

The reason I was sent there
Was to learn wisdom and piety;
And I brought back with me firmly
The general truths for the seers of Ireland.

The time I had completed there
Was exactly ten years,
When Sinotan sent me away
<margin>That is, out of.</margin> From angelic Rome into exile.

I did not come back from the east
For the space of two hundred years,
Until the time of sweet-voiced Patrick,
<margin>That is, from now on.</margin> Twelve years after he arrived in Ireland. <note>This would be about A.D. 444.</note>

When I received the tonsure <note>original: "coroin" or corona; the clerical hairstyle signifying religious devotion.</note> there,
At the altar of Peter in Rome,
It was in the time of Cormac, grandson of Conn,
A courageous, rightful son of Ireland.

<margin>How Caillín received his holy orders in Rome of Letha is now explained.</margin>

In that time—it is grievous to us—  
Freemen shall wed with slaves;  
And that mixed brood from this day forth,

I myself shall now relate

It was Finntan, my upright tutor,  
That sent me eastwards to Rome;  
The provision my tutor gave me  
Was 300 ounces of red gold.  
The object for which I was sent to the East  
Was to learn wisdom and devotion;  
And that I might firmly bring back  
Universal knowledge to the men of Ireland.  
The time I had completed then  
Was ninety years and ten <note>90 years old</note>,  
When pleasant Finntan sent me  
To angelic Rome from Ireland.  
I did not come back from the East  
During a period of two hundred years,  
Until sweet Patrick had lived  
Twelve years in Ireland.  
There I received the proper tonsure <note>original: *coronam coim*. The tonsure is the practice of cutting or shaving some or all of the hair on the scalp as a sign of religious devotion.</note>,  
At the altar of Peter in Rome,  
In the time of Cormac, grandson of Conn—  
An efficient, active son of Ireland.

<margin>Regarding the name "Letha," see note 1, page 8, above. The words "proper tonsure" <note>original: *coronam coim*</note> would appear to indicate that this portion of Caillin's alleged prophecy must have been composed after the period of the dispute between the Irish and English ecclesiastics respecting the "anterior" <note>shaving the front of the head</note> and the "coronal" <note>shaving the top of the head in a circle</note> tonsure; the latter form having only been adopted by the Irish Church about A.D. 718. See Reeves's *Adamnan*, p. 350.</margin>
<margin>Cormac died, A.D. 266.</margin>
<margin>Grandson: *ua* in Manuscript A. The genitive singular form is *ui*.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the account of Saint Caillin’s travels and the establishment of his religious foundation at Fenagh.</meta>

<page-num>112</page-num>

...learning, wisdom, and piety, after it was assigned to him by Rinntann son of Dochrau, his predecessor. However, the guiding angel did not allow the prayers to be offered in any other place until he reached Magh Rein <note>The Plain of Rein, in County Leitrim</note> in the pass of Connacht, specifically to Dun Baile <note>Baile’s Fort</note>, which is the place called Fenagh today. For it was there that it was destined for him to inhabit his church and his establishment, according to the word of God.

It was a very long time ago indeed since the druid Cathbad <note>original: Cathbad. A famous druid in the time of King Conor Mac Nessa, who died circa A.D. 50.</note> had prophesied that Caillin would come to those people during the time that Eochaidh Feidllech <note>original: Eochaidh Feidllech. King of Ireland, and father of the famous Queen Medbh of Connacht. He died 18 years before the Incarnation.</note> was in the kingship of Ireland. However that may be, Caillin eventually arrived at Dun Baile in Magh Rein.

Why was this place first called Dun Baile? That is not difficult to answer. A noble and illustrious king once ruled Ireland named Conaing son of Congal <note>original: Conaing Bec-ecla, or “Conangus Impavidus” (Conaing the Fearless). He reigned several centuries before the Christian era.</note>. It was there on Magh Rein that this king had his residence and fortress at that time. A stronghold and a <term>stone enclosure</term> <note>original: cairril cloċ. The remains of a stone enclosure, or cashel (Latin: maceria), are still visible at Fenagh.</note> were built by him near Lake Salach. Salach son of Samal of the Trojans was the druid to that Conaing son of Congal, and it is from him that Lake Salach is named.

Baile of the Sweet Speech, son of Buan (the son of the King of Ulster in the north), was the foster-son of Conaing son of Congal, the King of Ireland. It is from him, therefore, that Dun Baile <note>Baile’s Fort</note> was named. Furthermore, the kings, princes, and warriors of Ireland, along with their horses, used to come to that fort to settle their legal cases, their treaties, and their alliances, and to pay their tributes, taxes, and dues.

The name Dun Baile remained attached to that place from the time of Conaing son of Congal and his foster-son Baile son of Buan, until the time of Patrick son of Calpurnius <note>Saint Patrick</note>, and until the time of Feargna, son of Fergus, son of Muireadhach, son of Eoghan Sreim, son of Duach Galach, son of Brian, son of Eochaidh Muighmheadhon.

<margin>Baile. See a curious legend regarding this individual in O'Curry's *Manuscript Materials*.</margin>

<summary>This page describes Saint Caillin's arrival at Fenagh (Dun Baile) in Magh Rein, fulfilling an ancient prophecy by the druid Cathbad. It explains the site's history as a royal seat and its naming after Baile, the foster-son of King Conaing.</summary>

<keywords>Saint Caillin, Fenagh, Magh Rein, Dun Baile, King Conaing, Cathbad the Druid, Irish genealogy, Lake Salach, stone enclosure, cashel</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Saint Caillin’s travels and the sacred history of the site of Fenagh (Fidhnnacha).</meta>

<page-num>113</page-num>

Saint Caillin, a man of great wisdom and piety, was sent to the East by Finntan son of Bochra, his own teacher. Furthermore, the Angel did not permit him to rest or remain in any other place until he came to Magh-Rein, in the region of Breifne in Connacht—specifically to Dun-Baile, which is the place where Fenagh <note>original: Fidhnnacha</note> stands today. It was prophesied that the site of his church and home should be there, according to the instructions of God.

Truly, it was a long time ago that the fair <term>druid</term> <note>A high-ranking professional, priest, or magician in ancient Celtic culture.</note> Cathbad <gloss>¹</gloss> foretold that Caillin would come to that place; this prophecy was made during the time that Eochaidh Feidlech <gloss>²</gloss> held the sovereignty of Ireland. Regardless, the place Caillin arrived at was Dun-Baile in Magh-Rein.

Why, first of all, is that place called Dun-Baile? Truly, it is not difficult to explain. A noble and illustrious king once ruled Ireland: Conaing <gloss>³</gloss> son of Congal. The place where that king’s home and residence were situated was precisely in Magh-Rein. He also built a stronghold and a stone enclosure <gloss>⁴</gloss> there, near Loch-Salach. Salach, son of Samal, of the Trojans, was the <term>druid</term> to King Conaing; it is from him that Loch-Salach is named.

Baile <gloss>⁵</gloss> the sweet-speaking, son of Buan (the son of the king of Ulster <note>original: Ulidia</note> from the North), was the foster-son of Conaing son of Congal, King of Ireland. Therefore, Dun-Baile was named after him.

The kings and lords of Ireland, its warriors, and all others besides, were accustomed to go to that *fortress* <note>original: dun</note> to make their <term>compacts</term>, their <term>covenants</term>, and their <term>treaties</term>, and to pay their <term>tributes</term>, their <term>rents</term>, and their <term>customs</term>. The name Dun-Baile remained attached to that place from the time of Conaing and his foster-son Baile, until the time of Saint Patrick son of Calpurnius, and of Fergna, son of Fergus, son of Muiredach Mal, son of Eoghan Sremh, son of Duach Galach, son of Brian, son of Eochaidh Muidhmedhon. <gloss>⁶</gloss>

***

<margin>(Dublin, 1861), pp. 465–7, and 472–8.</margin>
<gloss>¹</gloss> <note>Cathbad was a famous legendary druid of the Ulster Cycle.</note>
<gloss>²</gloss> <note>A legendary High King of Ireland, father of Queen Medb.</note>
<gloss>³</gloss> <note>As Baile was the sixth in descent from Rudhraide, <term>monarch</term> of Ireland, who died around 212 B.C., he could not have been a contemporary of Conaing.</note>
<gloss>⁴</gloss> <note>Likely referring to a cashel or stone fort.</note>
<gloss>⁵</gloss> <note>Baile is the subject of the famous tragic romance "Baile and Aillinn."</note>
<gloss>⁶</gloss> Eochaidh Muidhmedhon, or "Eochaidh of the moist-middle," as the name is explained in the *Annals of Clonmacnois*, was King of Ireland and died in A.D. 371, according to the *Chronicon Scotorum*. Fergna, the sixth in descent from him, must therefore have flourished about the year 550.

<summary>This page explains the divine and legendary origins of Fenagh (Dun-Baile), tracing its history from ancient druidic prophecies and Trojan-descended druids to its role as a central site for Irish political treaties and tributes.</summary>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Saint Caillin’s mission to convert the local kings of Ireland, specifically focusing on his encounter with King Fergna and his son Aedh Dubh.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>
<header>114</header>

That Fergna was the king of the district <note>original: "indurrn"</note> at the time Caillin arrived at Dun Baile <note>The Fort of the Town</note>. He went to the place where Fergna was to offer the faith to him. The king did not accept it from Caillin or from the other saints. However, Caillin and his holy companions offered excellent rewards to King Fergna: namely, a long life in good health and the kingdom of heaven forever, if he would only believe in them. Fergna rejected those rewards, for he had no desire to believe at all. It is from those rewards <note>original: "comada"</note> that the place previously mentioned is named **Fan Choba**¹ <note>The Slope of Conditions</note>.

After that, Fergna commanded his son—namely, **Aedh the Black**—to go and drive away Caillin and the other clerics from that residence. Then Aedh the Black, son of Fergna, the champion and brave battle-soldier, arrived with his hosts to expel Caillin and his clerics. When Aedh saw Caillin with his psalm-singers and his clerics praying and prostrating themselves, Aedh and his people were immediately inspired with love for God and for Caillin. They knelt before him, and Aedh the Black was baptized afterward. He then granted Dun Baile to Caillin, as had been prophesied for him.

It was after this that the name **Fenagh** <note>original: "Fionacha"</note> was given to Dun Baile: that is, from the "teams," the "yokes," and the "traction" <note>original: "rednach," "imredain," and "tarruib"</note> that Caillin brought to the town; from which Fenagh is named⁴.

When Fergna saw that his son and his people had turned to God and to Caillin, he became greatly enraged. He commanded his druids to go and banish the clerics. The druids rose up immediately and with great speed. They cast their bodies and the openings of their bellies upward toward the air. They opened their mouths and their gullets, and they shouted, shrieked, and cursed 
<margin>in an impious and <note>original: "ezcoin"</note> lawless manner against Caillin and his holy clerics.</margin>

---

¹ **Fan-choba**: "The slope (or declivity) of conditions," from *fan*, "a slope," and *coba* (correctly *coma*), "a condition" or "consideration." The name of the place is more correctly written Fan-Chomha (see page 117).
² **In**: The Irish is *do*, equivalent to the preposition "to" and "for."
³ **To him**: That is, to Caillin.
⁴ **Teams/Traction**: *Reonach* and *imreroain*, which are variously explained as "labour," a "team," a "yoke," or "drawing." The derivation of "Fenagh" (Fidnachá) is traditionally linked to these terms for the labor of establishing the monastery.

<summary>Saint Caillin attempts to convert King Fergna, who refuses; however, the King's son, Aedh Dubh, is converted and grants land to the saint, leading to the naming of Fenagh. In retaliation, Fergna sends druids to perform a ritual curse against the Christians.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Caillin, King Fergna, Aedh Dubh, Fenagh (Fionacha), Dun Baile, conversion, druids, Irish hagiography, place-name origins (dindsenchas)</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Saint Caillin’s arrival at Dun-Baile and his confrontation with King Fergna.</meta>

It is this Fergna who was king there when Caillin came to Dun-Baile. Caillin went to the place where Fergna was to persuade him to receive the Christian faith. However, Fergna did not accept it from Caillin or his fellow saints. Caillin and his saints even offered favorable conditions to Fergna—specifically, a long life in a prosperous state and the kingdom of Heaven for all eternity—if he would believe because of their testimony. Fergna refused these conditions, for it did not please him to believe at all. It is from these conditions that Fan-Choba¹ is always <note>original: "semper"</note> named.

Immediately afterward, Fergna commanded his son, Aedh Dubh <note>Aedh the Black</note>, to go and expel Caillin and the other clerics from the place. Thereupon Aedh Dubh, son of Fergna—the mighty hero and warrior—came with his hosts to expel Caillin and his clerics. But when Aedh subsequently saw Caillin with his singers of psalms and clerics engaged in prayer and prostrations, Aedh and his people immediately believed in God and in Caillin; they knelt to him. Aedh Dubh was afterward baptized. He also presented Dun-Baile to Caillin, as it had been foretold to him.³

It was after this that the name "Fidhnacha" <note>modern Fenagh</note> was given to Dun-Baile; specifically, it was named from the teams⁴ and from the pulling⁴ and from the hauling that brought Caillin to the place. From this, Fidhnacha is named <note>original: "Unde Fidhnacha nominatur"</note>.

When Fergna saw that his son and his people believed in God and Caillin, he was greatly enraged. He told his druids to go and banish the clerics. The druids immediately arose with great activity. They turned up their buttocks <note>original: "podices"</note> and the openings⁵ of their bellies⁵ against the air. They opened their jaws and gluttonous mouths, shouting and uttering provocations⁶ and reproaches, unlawfully and unjustly,⁷ against Caillin and his holy clerics.

***

¹ <margin>A silly guess by the original scribe. It is more probable that the name (by which other places in Ireland were formerly known) was derived from *fioh*, "a wood"; for it appears that as late as the year 1688, Fidhnacha was beautifully wooded. See O’Donovan’s *Breifne Letters*, Ordnance Survey Correspondence, Royal Irish Academy, p. 185.</margin>
⁵ <margin>*faoscal*—*bronnann*. These words would hardly bear translation <note>The editor implies these are obscene anatomical terms</note>.</margin>
⁶ <margin>Uttered provocation. The verb *spuratan*, thus translated, is really an active verb signifying "they incited"; but the context, which implies that the action was "at" or "against" Caillin, necessitated the liberty taken in rendering it by the words in question.</margin>
³ <margin>The text of Manuscript A recommences with this word after a gap caused by the loss of folio 11.</margin>
⁴ <margin>The text of Manuscript A recommences with this word after a gap caused by the loss of folio 11.</margin>
⁷ <margin>Unjustly. The text of Manuscript A recommences with this word after a gap caused by the loss of folio 11.</margin>

<summary>Saint Caillin attempts to convert King Fergna, who refuses; however, the King's son, Aedh Dubh, is converted by the sight of the clerics praying and grants them the site of Dun-Baile (Fenagh). Enraged, Fergna commands his druids to perform a ritual of mockery and provocation against the saints.</summary>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the hagiography of Saint Caillin of Fenagh, focusing on his spiritual power and the divine judgment against those who opposed him.</meta>

<page-num>116</page-num>

Truly, Saint Caillin, son of Niata, was a protector and a father to all. For he was the most chaste and most devout of the saints of his time, and he was the most pure and most worthy. He was the blazing fire for cutting down and burning the persecutors of God and the Church; and he was the judgment over their thresholds, a fierce vengeance against every person who would offend him. He was the wide-wounding lion whom it was impossible to withstand in battle. He was the sea without ebbing in gifts and in miracles, and in the singing of psalms in the constant praise of the Lord.

<margin>Folio 12, a 2.</margin>
Now, when Black Rod <note>original: Crod dub</note> heard the druids and the satirists mocking and reviling the clerics, he recognized the danger and commanded his hosts to attack and assault the druids to stop them from that practice <note>original: abaint; literally meaning "game" or "amusement," here referring to their ritual mockery</note>. "No," said Caillin, "we shall not exercise human power against them; but nevertheless, it is my will <note>original: is cet liumra; literally "it is permission with me"</note>, if it be <note>original: mara</note> the will of the God of heaven and earth, that the druids be turned into frozen stones." Thereupon, the druids were immediately turned into the forms of stones by the word of the person Caillin <note>original: la brethir in ti Caillin</note> in the presence of the hosts. Truly, the name of God and Caillin was magnified through that event and through that great miracle.

However, when Feargna saw these things—his son with his host and his followers believing in God and in Caillin, and his druids turned into the shapes of stone pillars—he was filled with wrath, fear, and unbelief. This is what he did because of it: he led away his hosts without any of them noticing, in order to avoid (that is, to shun) believing in Caillin. This was the path he took until he reached his home. There, the earth swallowed him up instantly, and his mind was not changed. When God saw that Feargna had no desire to believe forever, He separated his soul from his body.

<note>The text from page 110 to this point has been supplied from Manuscript B.</note>
<note>The townland of Longstones, in the parish of Oughteragh, northeast of Fenagh, contains several standing stones which are undoubtedly the ones referred to here. Maurice O’Mulconry, who lived in the area, identified them as such in historical records.</note>

<summary>Saint Caillin demonstrates his divine power by transforming mocking druids into stone pillars and witnessing the earth swallow the defiant King Feargna. These miracles lead many to convert while illustrating the fatal consequences of resisting the saint's mission.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Caillin, Feargna, druids, miracles, petrifaction, Fenagh, Oughteragh, conversion, divine judgment</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Saint Caillin’s spiritual authority and his conflict with the pagan forces opposing the spread of Christianity in Ireland.</meta>

<page-num>117</page-num>

<margin>At Fenagh, it is stated in his prose preface to this poem (that is, the poem beginning at page 124, below), that the stones into which the druids were turned stood to the Northeast of Fenagh.</margin>

<margin>The word "with" (so translated here, without altering the sense of the narrative) is omitted in Manuscript B.</margin>

It would be hard, truly, to cast reproach and contempt on Saint Caillin, son of Niata, for he was the best reputed and the most devout of the saints of his time—the purest and most worthy. He was the blazing fire to destroy and burn the persecutors of God and the Church. He was the doom over his enemies, enacting stern vengeance on everyone who deserved it. He was the fierce-wounding lion, against whom no conflict could be maintained. He was the unebbing sea in prodigies and miracles, and in psalm-singing in perpetual praise of the Lord.

<margin>⁵ Marvel. The original Irish word is *mirbail*, from the Latin *mirabilia* <note>original: "mirabilia" meaning "wonderful things"</note>. The manuscripts have *mirbaili*, the plural form; but the compound preposition, *triasan*, is singular.</margin>

When, therefore, Aedh Dubh <note>Aedh the Black</note> heard the druids and satirists reviling and continually disparaging the clerics, he ordered and commanded his hosts to attack and encounter the druids to restrain them from that practice. “No,” said Caillin; “we will not exercise human power upon them; but it is my will, if it be the will of my God of Heaven and Earth, that the druids may be changed into stones forthwith.” Thereupon the druids were immediately turned into forms of stone at Caillin’s word, in the presence of the multitude. The name of God and of Caillin was magnified, verily, through that miracle and great marvel.

<margin>⁶ With. The preposition *o* (which properly signifies “from,” but could not be used here) is used in the original.</margin>
<margin>⁷ *Fan-Chomha*. See note 1, page 114, previously.</margin>
<margin>⁸ There. *Imourn* in Manuscript A.</margin>
<margin>⁹ His mind changed not. *Ni ro roao aigneó do* in Manuscripts A and B. Manuscript B reads *ni ro roao an aig-neó do*, meaning “it changed not in mind to him.”</margin>
<margin>¹⁰ Inclined. *Nap bo menmarc la Fergna*. Literally, “that it was not desired by Fergna.”</margin>

When Fergna, however, observed these things—namely, that his son, with his host and army, believed in God and Caillin, and that his druids were transformed into the shape of stone columns—he was filled with fury, fear, and unbelief. What he did was this: he quitted his armies, unknown to and unobserved by any one of them, to avoid believing in Caillin. The road he took was to Fan-Chomha. Then it was that the ground sundered under him there; but his mind changed not. When God Himself saw that Fergna was not inclined to believe throughout his life, He separated his soul from his body.

<summary>Saint Caillin miraculously transforms a group of mocking druids into stone pillars, leading to the conversion of Aedh Dubh's army, while the stubborn King Fergna is swallowed by the earth for his refusal to believe.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Caillin, Aedh Dubh, Fergna, druids, Fenagh, miracles, petrifaction, Irish hagiography, conversion</keywords>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Saint Caillin’s interactions with the kings of Ireland, specifically following the miraculous punishment of Feargna.</meta>

<page-num>118</page-num>

<margin>See folio 12, b 1.</margin>
Afterward, the angel came to Caillin and explained fully and very well those miracles he had performed against Feargna—namely, his being swallowed by the earth. The names of God and Caillin were magnified through that event.

The angel then commanded Caillin to assemble the hosts around Aed Dubh <note>Aed the Black</note>, son of Feargna, and to give the kingship to him; for God had granted it to him because of his humility and his reverence toward Saint Caillin. Caillin did as he was told.

The hosts were gathered around Aed, and the kingship was given to him afterward. Then Aed said to the angel: "I beseech you in the name of God, obtain for me from Caillin and from God a change of my form; for the appearance I currently have is not fitting for me to rule over kings and other nobles, lest my blackness and ugliness be held against me." The angel said to Caillin: "Grant to Aed Dubh what he asks—namely, his choice of form and excellent appearance." The form of Rioce of Inis-bo-finde <note>Saint Rioce of Inishbofin, a contemporary saint</note> was the one Aed chose then.

Caillin and his clerics then prayed that night regarding that matter. When the hosts rose the next morning, there was no difference or distinction in appearance between Aed Dubh and Rioce of Inis-bo-finde, except only that there was a tonsure <note>original: coroin, referring to the clerical crown or tonsure</note> on Rioce’s head, and there was none on Aed’s head.

<margin>See folio 12, b 2.</margin>
Just as Eogan son of Niall <note>Eogan mac Néill, ancestor of the Cenél nEógain</note> received his form from Patrick, so too was the appearance of Aed Dubh son of Feargna and Eogan son of Niall identical, for the appearance of Rioce was the foundation for both of them.

Thereupon the Angel came to Caillin and told him plainly and truly the miracles that had been wrought on Fergna—namely, that he had been swallowed under the ground. The name of God and the name of Caillin were magnified through that miracle.

<margin>¹ Why are these two lines here?</margin>
<margin>² Rioce is mentioned in the *Tripartite Life of St. Patrick*. The note "Black Aedh changed into Fair Aedh" is added in the margin.</margin>
The Angel afterwards commanded Caillin to assemble the armies around Aedh Dubh <note>Aedh the Black</note>, son of Fergna, and to give him the kingship, because it was to him God Himself had granted it on account of his humility and reverence towards Saint Caillin.

Caillin did so. The armies were assembled around Aedh, and the kingship was afterwards given to him.

Aedh then said to the Angel: “I beseech you, in the name of God, to obtain from Caillin and from God the transformation of my appearance; for I do not consider the kingly form that I have fit to rule over other kings and princes, lest my blackness and ugliness should be used against me.” The Angel said to Caillin: “Grant to Aedh Dubh that which he requests—namely, that he may have his choice of features and of form.” The form of Rioce of Inis-bo-finde <note>modern: Inishbofin, or "Island of the White Cow"</note> was the appearance that Aedh chose for himself.

<margin>⁶ No difference. This phrase was omitted in some versions.</margin>
<margin>⁷ A crown; specifically the coronal tonsure <note>The practice of shaving the top of the head used by monks and clerics.</note>.</margin>
<margin>⁸ Eoghan Mac Neill. See the curious account of the manner in which St. Patrick gratified the wish of Eoghan Mac Neill to be made as handsome as a saint.</margin>

Caillin, along with his clerics, fasted that night regarding the matter; and after the hosts had risen the next morning, there was neither distinction nor difference of appearance between Aedh Dubh and Rioce of Inis-bo-finde, except only that there was a tonsure on the head of Rioce and none on Aedh’s head. Just as Eoghan Mac Neill had obtained a similar request from Patrick, the appearance of Aedh Dubh, son of Fergna, was therefore identical with that of Eoghan Mac Neill, for it was the likeness of Rioce that each of them possessed.

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Aedh Find (Aedh the Fair) and his relationship with Saint Caillin of Fenagh.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>

Then Aedh Find <note>Aedh the Fair</note> received his name from that hour. It is from him that the *Cath* <note>original: "cath"; literally "battle," but here meaning a tribe or battalion</note> of Aedh Find descended—namely, the three royal lines of Connacht—forever.

After that, Aedh Find arrived and placed the pillar-stone <note>original: "capchi"; a large stone or flagstone often found in ancient forts</note> in the main doorway of the city and the monastery. He granted land to his soul-friend <note>spiritual advisor</note>, namely to Caillin, and to his successors forever, to enlarge his city and to honor his church until the end of the world.

Then Aedh Find ordained a tribute and tax from his kindred forever to Caillin and to his successors. Caillin said, "Heaven shall be yours and your kindred's after you, provided that they choose <note>original: "co tozatt"</note> my church and my monastery <note>original: "im chongbail"</note>." "You have my choice indeed," said Aedh, "for whoever of my kindred does not pay this tribute shall not receive luck or kingship." For seventy-two years after that, Aedh Find remained in a state of penitence.

Aedh was afterwards brought, after his death, by Caillin to Fenagh of Magh Rein. He was buried there in the cemetery of the kings, which the angels had abandoned at that time—
<margin>*folio 13. That is, on the full cemetery of Dun Baile, which was Fenagh at that time.</margin>
This is the tribute which Aedh Find, son of Feargna, ordained for Caillin, son of Niathach, from all the descendants of Aedh Find forever:

The riding horse of every king of the race of Aedh Find, and his assembly-raiment <note>ceremonial dress</note>, including his headgear, his belt, and his mantle. In the same way, from every chieftain of the race of Aedh Find to Caillin and his successors forever:

The horse of every queen and her clothing, just as it is received from the king. In the same way from every chieftain's wife, as it is received from the chieftains.

<meta>This page continues the account of the relationship between Aedh Find (Aedh the Fair), King of Connacht, and Saint Caillin of Fenagh, establishing the spiritual and financial obligations of the king's lineage to the church.</meta>

<page-num>121</page-num>

<margin>¹ "Of Aedh Find and his two kingdoms, etc."; "Selection of Fenagh by Aedh Find and his race, etc.," is added in the margin.</margin>
Aedh Find <note>Aedh the Fair</note> was his name from that hour. From him descended the clan¹ of Aedh Find—that is, the third royal family of Connacht forever.

<margin>² Flag of the Angels. The precise position of this flag, in the cemetery of Fenagh, is not at present known.</margin>
Aedh Find arrived immediately afterwards at the pillar-stone² in the principal door of the city and dwelling; and he granted land to his spiritual advisor <note>original: "soul-friend" or *anam-chara*</note>, namely to Caillin, and to his successors forever, to enlarge his city and to honor his church until the end of the world.

It was then that Aedh Find ordered the dues and³ tribute-rents of his kindred to be paid forever to Caillin and his successors. Caillin said, "Heaven for you, and for your race after you, provided that they choose⁴ to be buried in my church and in my dwelling."⁵ "I make my choice with you,"⁶ said Aedh. "Whosoever of my race shall not pay your tribute, may he not obtain grace or sovereignty."⁷

<margin>⁷ Sovereignty. *rígi* or *ríga*. A marginal note has "Caillin’s tribute upon the race (*cath*) of Aedh Find."</margin>
Aedh Find remained in the sovereignty for seventy-two years after that. After his death, Aedh was taken to Fenagh <note>original: *Fidh-nacha*</note> of Magh-Rein by Caillin, who subsequently buried him at the "yew of the kings,"⁸ exactly under the "flag of the Angels,"⁹ that is, in the middle of the cemetery of Dun-baile, now called Fenagh.

<margin>⁸ "Yew of the kings." *ibar na rígh*. There is no trace or tradition of this celebrated tree at present.</margin>
<margin>⁹ Flag of the Angels. The precise position of this flag, in the cemetery of Fenagh, is not at present known.</margin>
This is the tribute which Aedh Find, son of Fergna, ordained for Caillin, son of Niata, from¹⁰ the race of Aedh Find forever.

<margin>¹⁰ From. *for*, literally "upon."</margin>
<margin>¹¹ Riding steed. *ech gabail*. In the poem printed above, this is represented by *seisrech* or *seisrech maith*, "a good team" or "colt."</margin>
<margin>¹² Body raiment. *eirred cuirp*. The poem referred to has *cath eirred*, "battle dress."</margin>
The riding steed¹¹ of every king of the lineage of Aedh Find, and his personal clothing¹² including everything for head, foot, and hand [shall be given to the church]. The same shall be given from every chieftain of the lineage of Aedh Find to Caillin and his successor forever.
The steed and dress of every queen shall be given in the same manner as from the king. In the same way, the same shall be given from each chieftain’s wife as from the chieftains.

<summary>King Aedh Find establishes Fenagh as his family's burial place and decrees a perpetual tribute of horses and royal clothing to Saint Caillin and his successors. The text records Aedh's burial at the "yew of the kings" and the spiritual pact between the monarch and the saint.</summary>
<keywords>Aedh Find, Saint Caillin, Fenagh (Fidh-nacha), Connacht, royal tribute, burial rites, Magh-Rein, Dun-baile, Irish kingship</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the record of the tributes and spiritual privileges granted to Saint Caillin and his successors by the local kings and chieftains of Ireland.</meta>

<page-num>122</page-num>

From every <term>biatagh</term><note>original: biataig; a provider of food or a public hospitaller</note> and every head of a townland from <term>Ath-droichit</term><note>original: Ath-broidit; likely Drogheda</note> to Sligo:
A <term>screpall</term><note>original: screapall; a small silver coin or unit of value</note> for every sheep-fold. A portion of every spoil from every king’s son and chieftain.
Three visits are due from every foster-son and every son of a king to the King of Cleda <note>Aed Rino</note>.
Whosoever of them does not give that tribute shall not possess his patrimony, and he shall not obtain prosperity or kingship. Every third year that tribute is due to be paid.

<margin>Folio 13, a 2.</margin> This is the reward for that tribute from Caillin to the battalion of Aed Rino: Foreigners shall not take their land by consent or by force as long as they pay this tribute. Prosperity and wealth shall be theirs also.
Whosoever shall not give this tribute, the curse of Caillin and the saints of Ireland be upon him; and his sons shall not possess his land after him, and hell shall be his final dwelling.

It is for the <term>dinnsenchus</term><note>original: dinnrenchur; the history of notable places or fortified hills</note> of Dun-Baile, and Fronacha, and Loch Salach, and Fain-cobha; and concerning the druids who were turned into stone shapes by God and by Caillin to the northeast of Fronacha—namely, the <term>corrguinecht</term><note>original: corrġuineċt; a form of druidical sorcery or incantation performed while standing on one foot</note> which those druids practiced; and concerning the death of Ferghna; and the reason why Aed Rino gave Fronacha to Caillin; and the cause for which he imposed that tribute upon his kindred forever to Caillin and his successors and his sanctuary, that the scholar of history, Flann son of Flann, composed this poem:

->“Dun-Baile, a royal town of battles, etc.”<-

***

**Notes on the text:**
¹ **Biatagh**: A public hospitaller or land-holder responsible for providing food and hospitality.
² **Ath-droichit**: See note 4, p. 81 <note>Drogheda</note>.
³ **Screpall**: An ancient Irish silver coin, often valued at the price of a sheep.
⁴ **Whosoever**: Referring to the "three" or "any" who fail the obligation.
⁵ **In addition**: A similar tribute over the <term>Cinel-Conaill</term>, payable also every third year, was granted to St. Caillin by Conall Gulban.
⁶ **Dinnsenchus**: This word is usually translated as the “history of celebrated places.” But it really signifies the “history of fortresses,” being composed of *dinn* (a hill or fortified residence) and *senchus* (history).
⁷ **Corrguinecht**: Some kind of druidical incantation, as O’Davoran explains in his Glossary.

It introduces a poem by Flann son of Flann regarding the history of these lands and the defeat of druidic sorcery.</summary>

A cow is due from every <term>Biatagh</term> <note>original: Biatagh; a provider of food or a public hospitaller</note>, and from every chief of a <term>bally</term> <note>original: bally; a townland or territorial division</note>, from <term>Athdroichit</term> <note>Drogheda</note> to <term>Sligo</term>.
A <term>screpall</term> <note>original: screpall; an ancient Irish silver coin or unit of value</note> is due from every sheep-owner. A fat cow is due out of every spoil of war from every son of a king and every chieftain.
This tribute is also required from every foster-son and from every sister’s son belonging to the race of Aedh Find.
Whosoever among them does not furnish this tribute shall not obtain his inheritance, nor shall he obtain grace or kingship.
This tribute is required to be paid every third year.
The reward for this tribute, granted by Saint Caillin to the descendants of Aedh Find, was that foreigners should not obtain their land by consent or by force, so long as they paid this tribute. Prosperity and luck should be theirs in addition.
Whoever will not give this tribute, may he have the curse of Caillin and the saints of Ireland; may his sons not possess his land after him; and may hell be his final inheritance also.

Regarding the <term>dinnsenchus</term> <note>original: dinnsenchus; the traditional lore of place-names</note> of <term>Dun-baile</term> and <term>Fenagh</term> <note>original: Fidhnnacha</note>, and of <term>Loch-Salach</term> and <term>Fan-cobha</term>; and regarding the turning of the druids into stone forms by God and Caillin to the north-east of Fenagh (which druids used to perform <term>corrguinecht</term> <note>original: corrguinecht; a form of sorcery or ritual cursing</note>); and regarding the death of Fergna, and the reason why Aedh Find gave Fenagh to Caillin, and the cause why he placed that tribute upon his race forever to Caillin, his successor, and his community—the sage of history, Flann son of Flann, sang this poem, namely: “Dun-Baile, holy, regal place,” etc.

<margin>63), in uttering which the person rested on one foot and one hand, and had one eye closed. The formula used was a *<term>Glaim dichind</term>* <note>original: Glaim dichind; a severe ritual satire or "ending shout"</note>, or an extempore lampoon. It is further explained in the same authority (*Old Irish Glosses*, p. 66) as a trick done by sleight of hand. The manner of performance described in the following poem is rather coarse.</margin>

<margin>⁸ *Flann son of Flann*. Better known as Flann Mainistrech, or Flann of the Monastery, Lector of <term>Monasterboice</term>. He died in 1056. The present poem is not attributed to him by any other authority than the writer of the foregoing introduction to the poem, and the composer of the poem, as far as the Editor is aware.</margin>

<margin>⁹ The words from “namely,” to the end of the sentence, are added from Manuscript B, from which the poem itself is missing.</margin>

1.</margin>Dun Baile <note>original: Dun mbali</note>, the royal town of all,
A port to which the clans of Ireland <note>original: Fail; a bardic name for Ireland</note> come,

It was he who strongly possessed the fortress,

Daile, son of Duan—royal was his foundation—
From whom the beautiful fort is named.
Dun Baile is the name of the city,
And it was not a short period of time
From the era of Conaing of great heat,
Until the time of Fergna, son of Fergus.
Son of Muiredach, son of Eoghan of fame,
Son of Duach, son of Brian, son of Eochaidh.

<meta>This page continues a poetic history of Dun-Baile, tracing its origins from the reign of Conaing Beg-eglach to later descendants.</meta>

# Dun-Baile

Dun-Baile, a holy and royal place;
A mansion where the lords of Ireland <note>original: "Fal," a poetic name for Ireland</note> used to gather,
When the vigorous Conaing Beg-eglach <note>original: "Beg-eglach" means "the Little-fearing" or "Fearless"</note>
Was a brave and raiding king.
Conaing, the son of mighty Congal,
Held power over all of Ireland <note>original: "Banba," an ancient name for Ireland</note>.
He built a beautiful stone fortress <term>cashel</term> <note>a circular stone fort or castle</note>
On the Plain of Rein <note>original: "Magh-Rein"</note>, by the Willow Lake <note>original: "Loch-Salach"</note>.
Salach, the son of the famous Samal,
And grandson of the druid of the Trojans,
Was a grand and powerful druid
Serving Conaing, the King of Ireland.
This is why the Plain of Rein is so named:
From the tracks left by the sons of the brave Eochaidh;
And from the clear leveling of the land
That the foreigners <note>likely referring to Viking or Anglo-Norman invaders</note> achieved through their fighting.
The generous Conaing had a foster-son <term>dalta</term> <note>a foster-child, often a high-ranking student or ward</note>,
Who was the son of the King of Ulster <note>original: "Ulad"</note>, a man of many hostages;
This was Baile, son of Buan, royal in his mind,
After whom this beautiful fortress <note>original: "Dun"</note> is named.
Dun-Baile was the name of the stone city <term>cahir</term> <note>a dry-stone fort or enclosure</note>
(And it held that name for no short time)
From the era of the powerful Conaing,
Down to the time of Fergna, son of Fergus,
Son of Muiredach, son of the famous Eoghan,
Son of Duach, son of Brian, son of Eochaidh.

<summary>This page provides a poetic genealogy and etymology for Dun-Baile, attributing its construction to King Conaing Beg-eglach and its name to his foster-son, Baile mac Buain.</summary>

<margin>folio 13, b 2.</margin>
Son of Muiredach, son of Fiacha Sraiptine <note>original: *Sraip*; the suffix *tene* is omitted here to maintain the poetic meter</note>,
Son of Cairpre, son of Cormac of the grey locks <note>original: *Culghlas*</note>.
Son of Art and son of Conn also;
This is the genealogy of Fergna;
From the time of the fair, perfect Fergna
The name of the city <note>original: *cathraigh*; referring to a stone fort or settlement</note> was changed.
The greatness of the name that existed

Was given to the city of Fergna,
Fenagh <note>original: *Fidhnacha*</note> from the many alder trees
Which Caillin brought to its beautiful foundation.
Then Caillin and his saints came
Against Fergna of the smooth hair,
And he offered him a friendly treaty:
A long life in good health.
It was then that Fergna said

"Arise and drive them from the land;
Do not let the clerics stay here unmolested."
Then Aedh the jet-black arose quickly
With his great cavalry,
To drive the clerics back,
And to expel them from that beautiful place.
When the fair Aedh the jet-black saw
Caillin and his saints in prayer,
He and his host believed in them fervently,
And he surrendered the beautiful place to them.
When Fergna saw truly
That he was betrayed by his own sons,
He spoke to his brothers with bitterness:
"Arise, and drive out the clerics!"

<meta>This page continues the genealogical and hagiographical poem regarding the lineage of Fergna and the founding of the monastery at Fenagh by Saint Caillin.</meta>

Son of Muiredach, son of Fiacha Sraiptene,¹
Son of Cairpre, son of Cormac Culglas,²
Son of Art, who was son of Conn, to whom
I have traced the pedigree of Fergna.
From that famous Fergna’s time
The name of the *stone fortress* <note>original: "cahir"; a stone fort or monastic enclosure</note> was changed.
Here is the explanation for you of the change of name
That was given to Fergna’s *fortress*:
“Fidhnacha” <note>Fenagh</note>; named from the noble *ox-teams* <note>original: "fednach"; the author suggests the name Fenagh comes from the teams of oxen Caillin used</note>
Which Caillin brought to his fair fort.
Then Caillin and his saints came
Against Fergna of the fair hair,
And offered him a noble consideration:
A long life in a state of grace <note>original: "in noegbethaid"; literally "in holy life" or "in good existence"</note>.
It was then that Fergna said
To Aedh Dubh <note>Aedh the Black</note>, his mighty son:
“Arise, and fiercely expel
Those clerics from out the land.”
Thereupon quickly arose
The jet-black Aedh, with his cavalry,
To send the clerics back again,
And to expel them from the fair fort.
When the mild, jet-black Aedh
Saw Caillin and his saints kneeling,
He and his host bravely believed in them;
And he presented the fair fort to them.
As soon as Fergna plainly saw
That he had been betrayed by his own sons,
He venomously <note>original: "co nim"; literally "with poison"</note> called out to his druids,
“Go, and repel the clerics.”

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the conflict between Saint Caillin and the druids of King Fergna, following the king's refusal to grant land for the monastery at Fenagh.</meta>

<page-num>128</page-num>

The druids rise up early,
And they turn their backs <note>original: "tona"; literally "buttocks" or "posteriors." This refers to a ritual of magical insult or "glamour" performed by druids.</note> upward,
And they chant their prayers with loathing;
The saints curse them with indignation.
When Aed, son of Fergna, saw
The druids coming with such fury,
He said to his noble people,
"Rise up and repel this heavy company."
"I will not go against Caillin," [said Aed],
"To kill the druids cruelly,
Lest the miracles of God from heaven
Turn the druids into stones." <note>A common motif in Irish hagiography where the saint petrifies his magical opponents.</note>
When Fergna of the steeds <note>original: "in sguir"; referring to his royal stud or cavalry.</note> saw
The druids being turned into stones,
He incited his host fiercely,
So that he committed a crime through ill-will.
Though the strong earth swallowed
Fergna while he was all alone,
He did not believe in beloved God any the more
Until his body parted from his soul.
Then the holy angel comes
In honor of Caillin and his saints;
He relates with great light then
That Fergna had gone beneath the earth.
Then the angel said,
In honor of the faithful Caillin,
"Give the kingdom to Aed the Black,
And let his hosts be summoned to him."

The druids arise quickly,
And turn up their backs;¹
And their jaws move angrily,
As they unjustly revile the clerics.
When Aedh, son of Fergna, saw
The druids advancing boldly,
He said to his renowned people,
“Arise; and repel the great company.”
“They shall not go from us,” said Caillin,
“To slay the druids rigorously;
But the miracles of God from Heaven
Shall change the druids into stones.”²
When Fergna of the steeds³ <note>original: *scuir*. This is the genitive form, signifying a stud of horses, and also a tent.</note> beheld
The druids changed into stones,
He furiously left his army,
[And went] to Fan-Cobha, through an evil mind.
And although the mighty earth sank
Under Fergna, and he was all alone,
He did not believe any more in the loving God,
Until his soul departed from his body.⁴ <note>original: *body*. The literal translation of this line is “until his body separated from his soul,” rather a peculiar manner of expressing the act of dying.</note>
After that came the mild Angel,
In honor of Caillin and his saints,
And then plainly⁵ <note>original: *plainly*. *co rolur*, literally “with light.” It is probably an error for *co rollur*, meaning plainly.</note> related
That Fergna⁶ <note>Fergna, Version A.</note> had gone under the ground.
Then the Angel said,
In honor of devout Caillin:
“Give the kingship to Aedh Dubh;
Let his army be summoned to him.”

"Listen to what Black Aedh <note>original: Aedh dubh</note> has understood,"
Said the beautiful angel of the sweet voice:
"Speak to the heavenly Caillin
Concerning the black warrior and lord.
Pray to the heavenly Christ,
That He might grant me my choice of form,
If it be the will of the King that I should be counted
Among the princes and lords."
Then the angel spoke there
To the noble and very great Caillin:
"Grant to Aedh his desire <note>original: breth; literally 'judgment' or 'award'</note> as a gift,
As a man who will not be refused."
Caillin, the man of iron holiness,
Fasted to God with great endurance,
So that Black Aedh became Fair-White Aedh <note>original: Aedh findgel</note>
After he had risen from his sleep.
Aedh came out to the pillar-stone <note>original: cairti</note>,
In the very front of the stone fortress <note>original: cathrach</note>,
And there he gave a response indeed
To Caillin, his spiritual adviser <note>original: anmchara; literally 'soul-friend'</note>.
Then the prosperous Caillin said
To the wise Aedh, son of Fergna:
"Heaven is for you and your family without deceit,
Provided you uphold my fair festival.
Seven Breifne men shall choose my church,"
Said Caillin to Aedh of the cloaks,
"They shall obtain heaven in every way,
And their children shall be kings."

<margin>Heavenly <note>original: nemda</note>; literally "heavenly," used here for the sake of the meter.</margin>
<margin>Fair Aedh <note>original: Aedh Find</note> is the name by which the former "Black Aedh" is called from this point forward in this work.</margin>
<margin>Desire <note>original: breth</note>; the original word properly means "award" or "judgment."</margin>
<margin>Pillar-stone <note>original: cairti</note>; a standing stone or monument.</margin>
<margin>Black Aedh <note>original: Aedh dubh</note>.</margin>
<margin>Stone fortress <note>original: cathrach</note>; a stone-built fort or "cahir."</margin>
<margin>Fair-white Aedh <note>original: Aedh findgel</note>. The last syllable (gel) is added for the sake of the rhyme.</margin>
<margin>Spiritual adviser <note>original: anmchara</note>; from *anim* (soul) and *cara* (friend).</margin>

<summary>This page recounts the miraculous transformation of the chieftain Aedh Dubh into Aedh Find (Fair Aedh) following Saint Caillin's fasting and prayer. In gratitude, Aedh pledges his loyalty to the saint, who in turn promises heavenly rewards and kingship to Aedh's descendants.</summary>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the interaction between Aedh Dubh (Black Hugh) and Saint Caillin, focusing on the miraculous transformation of the king's physical appearance and his subsequent donation of land to the church.</meta>

Aedh Dubh <note>original: "Black Hugh"</note> after that besought
The pure Angel of sweetest voice:
“Say to holy Caillin,
That a black warrior should not be Lord.”
“Say, moreover, to Heavenly Christ,
That He grant me my choice of features,
If He wishes that I should be here,
In sovereignty over princes.”
Hereupon spoke the Angel,
To pure, illustrious Caillin:
“Grant his wish to Aedh,
As he has sought, without refusal.”
Caillin and his saints, afterwards,
Fasted before the Lord until morning,
So that black Aedh was transformed into fair-white Aedh
When he rose from his slumber.
Aedh came out to the pillar-stone
In the principal door of the *stone fortress* <note>original: "cahir"</note>;
And he then, moreover, presented land
To Caillin, his soul’s friend <note>original: "anam-chara"; a spiritual adviser or confessor</note>.
Then Caillin himself said
To the wise Aedh son of Fergna:
“Heaven for you, and for your guileless race,
Provided they select my fair church as their burial place.”
“May every man of Breifne that selects my church,”
Said Caillin to Aedh of the spears,
“Obtain Heaven in every way <note>original: "ar gach mug" (omnimodo); in every manner</note>,
And may his children succeed him in his place.”

<margin>(Correctly *anam*) = soul <note>original Latin: "anima"</note>, and *cara*, “a friend” <note>original Latin: "carus"</note>. The word is generally used in the sense of “confessor,” or “spiritual adviser.” See Reeves *On the Culdees*, Transactions of the Royal Irish Academy, volume 24, page 88.</margin>
<margin>Select; that is, as a place of burial <note>original: "sepulture"</note>.</margin>
<margin>In every way. *Ar gach mug*. The word *mug* is used by mistake for *mod*, meaning "method" or "way" <note>original Latin: "modus"</note>. The expression is equivalent to the Latin *omnimodo* (in every way).</margin>

<summary>King Aedh Dubh requests a physical transformation from Saint Caillin to better suit his royal status; after a night of fasting, he is made "fair," leading him to grant land to the Saint and establish his church as a dynastic burial site.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the poetic and prose accounts of St. Caillin’s interactions with the descendants of Aed mac Fergna and the establishment of his rights at Fenagh.</meta>

<page-num>132</page-num>
<margin>Folio 14, column 2.</margin>
"I choose this, O Caillin,"
Said Aed mac Fergna of the shouts;
"Heaven <note>original: Nem</note> in the field for your family without falsehood,
Provided they choose the fortress of the town <note>original: Dun Baile; referring to Fenagh</note> for me."ᵃ

From that hour when Aed was baptized,
I say to you—it was no short span—
The time that Aed of the battles <note>original: na n-aeg</note> spent
Was eighty-two years.

At the Yew of the Kings thereafter,
Aed was certainly buried,
Under the Stone of the Angels without falsehood,
In the middle of the cemetery of Dun Gaile <note>another name for Fenagh</note>.

I am Flann, son of Flann, who sang
This noble ancient history <note>original: senchurra</note>,
For Caillin from the fresh ancient plain,
For the race of Aed in their fair fortress.
The fortress of the town.

It is regarding that visit that Caillin is entitled to dues from the race of Aed, as Caillin himself declared in the preceding poem, namely: "Ireland, great island of angels"; and let us remember a few of those verses here, for it makes them easier to keep in mind.

Caillin said:

"This is my tribute for my house,
From the festive race of Aed:
A scruple <note>a small silver coin or unit of weight</note> for every fighting man,
A horse and its trappings from every high-king."

Aed was a noble battle-soldier,
He was brought to me without fear;
It is I who did this for him,
Aed the slender, who is sufficient for us.
I placed him—and it was no mistake—
Appropriate to the form of the end;

Because of that, there was granted to me

And for his body to be brought to my church,
No matter where he might die in Ireland.
Since famous Aed is being brought,
Let him be buried in my doorway;
He spent his life without hardship,
Eighty-two years <note>original: Da bliadain ar rechtmogait; the text notes a correction from seventy to eighty-two</note> in total.

<margin>From folio 14, column b 2.</margin> That is enough <note>original: Ní beag sin</note> to have written in this place, for there is more than that in the "Great Poem" which begins: *He is the noble island of angels.*

If one wishes to investigate further how Saint Caillín, son of Niataic, sought the tribute due from the descendants of Conall, son of Niall—namely, from the seven illustrious sons he had, and from their seed until Judgment Day—it was to confirm that family and their names that this verse was spoken:

Fergna, Angus, Cochaid, Enna,
Nasi, Ruaman, Dluigoir of mirth;
Those are they, with running fame and great strength,
The seven sons of Conall Gulban of the hosts.

Though black was the warrior Aedh,
I baptized him without fear.
It was I who made of him
Pure-fair Aedh of ample whiteness.<note>original: "Aedh Find." "Find" or "Finn" means fair or white, suggesting a spiritual transformation through baptism.</note>
It was I who made—and it was no harm—

A crown on the head of fair Rioce;
Aedh Find without a crown at all.
This is the reason why to me was given

And for bringing his body to my church,
Wherever in Ireland he might die.
From the hour I baptized Aedh the Famous,

If it is desired to know how St. Caillin, son of Niata, obtained the tribute to which he is entitled from the children of Conall son of Niall—namely, from the seven illustrious sons he had and from their descendants forever—be it known that it was in proof of those sons, and of their names, this verse <note>original: "rann"</note> was spoken:

<summary>This page records a poem attributed to St. Caillin regarding the baptism and long life of King Aedh Find, and begins an explanation of the tributes owed to the saint by the descendants of Conall Gulban.</summary>
