--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues the genealogy and historical accounts of the descendants of Conall Gulban and their obligations to Saint Caillin.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>

These are the descendants of that son <note>Conall Gulban</note> from whom Caillin is entitled to his tribute: namely, the **Clann-Dalaigh** <note>original: *clann Dalais*; the O'Donnells and their kin</note> in the first place, and the **Cinel-Boghaine**, the **Cinel-Luigdech**, the **Cinel-Maeldoraidh**, the **O'Canannans**, the **sons of Gilla-Findein**, and the rest of the **descendants of Conall**.

It was Conall who was the first King of Tara among the sons of Niall, until he yielded the kingship to Laeghaire; and that was a "stone in the place of an egg" <note>an idiom meaning a poor exchange or a heavy burden</note>. For it was Conall who won fifty battles in defense of Ireland, and he was never defeated in battle or single combat. No wrong or enmity was ever committed against any of his brothers that Conall did not avenge; and Conall never accepted cattle or compensation for his losses, but only the head of a hero or a battle-soldier in payment.
<margin>Folio 15, column a 1.</margin>
Furthermore, the strength and heroism of Conn of the Hundred Battles were present in his father, Niall of the Nine Hostages, and the strength of Niall was embodied in this Conall.

It was he who divided the spoils among his brothers, and he took half of Ulster for himself alone, giving the other half to his brothers, because it was he who had defended that province for them.

Once, when the Masraighe of Magh Slecht <note>a tribe from the "Plain of Adoration" in County Cavan</note> went on a raid to Tara, they carried off horses from the east. Conall pursued them upon hearing the outcries, accompanied by the small force he had with him at the time. He did not stop until he reached Dun Conaing on that plain—also called Fronacha—where the people of the district wounded him. He was unarmed at the time, and they would never have dared to lay a hand on him had fate not willed it; or perhaps it was from the exhaustion of running that he died. However it happened, it was the Masraighe who boasted of the deed.

***

¹ **Clann-Dalaigh**, or “descendants of Dalach.” This was the tribe name of the great sept from which the O’Donnells of Tirconnell and their relatives have sprung. Dalach died in 868. From his grandson, Domhnall, the O’Donnells derive their hereditary surname.

² **Cinel-Boghaine**. The descendants of Enna Boghaine, son of Conall Gulban, who gave their name to the barony of Bannagh, County Donegal.

³ **Cinel-Luigdech**. This seems to have been the original tribe name of the O’Donnell sept before the time of Dalach (died 868), who was the first of the O’Donnell race to obtain entire rule over the territory of Tirconnell, or Donegal. Dalach was fifth in descent from Lughaid (from whom the “Cinel-Luigdech” are named), the great-grandson of Conall Gulban.

⁴ **Cinel-Maeldoraidh**. The family of Muldory, or O’Muldory, descended from Maeldoraidh, who was fourth in descent from Flaithbertach, King of Ireland (727–734). They were chiefs of Tirconnell, ruling alternately with the [O'Canannans].

<summary>This page lists the noble families of Tirconnell descended from Conall Gulban who owe tribute to Saint Caillin and recounts the military prowess and eventual death of Conall Gulban at the hands of the Masraighe.</summary>
<keywords>Conall Gulban, Clann-Dalaigh, O'Donnells, Tirconnell, Niall of the Nine Hostages, Masraighe, Magh Slecht, Irish genealogy, Saint Caillin</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the genealogical and historical account of the descendants of Conall Gulban and their relationship to Saint Caillin of Fenagh.</meta>

These are the descendants of those sons from whom Caillin is entitled to receive his tribute: namely, the Clann-Dalaigh <note>The O'Donnells of Donegal</note>, the kingly clan, first of all; and the Cinel-Boghaine, and Cinel-Luigdech, and Cinel-Maeldoraidh, and the Ui-Canannain, and the Mac Gilla-Finnein <note>This name is now generally Anglicized as Leonard</note>, and the rest of the race of Conall besides.

It was Conall who was the first king of Tara <note>original: Temhair</note> among the sons of Niall, until he himself granted the kingship as a loan to Laeghaire. <margin>Tara. This statement is not found in any other authority; it is a flourish by the writer to magnify the character of Conall Gulban, whose descendants paid dues to the Abbots of Fenagh.</margin> And that was "a stone in the place of an egg" <note>An Irish proverb for a poor exchange or a hard bargain</note>; for it was Conall who won fifty battles contending for Ireland, and he was never defeated in battle or combat. No injury or injustice was done to any of his brothers that would not be complained of to Conall; and Conall would not accept cattle or payments as compensation, but required the head of a knight or warrior to pay for it. Moreover, the strength and valor of Conn of the Hundred Battles was in his father, Niall of the Nine Hostages; and the strength of Niall was in Conall himself.

It was he who distributed land to his brothers. He took half of Ulster <note>original: Uladh</note> for himself alone and gave the other half to his brothers, for it was he himself who defended that province for them.

One time the Masraidhe <note>An ancient tribe of Cavan and Leitrim</note> of Magh-Slecht went on a plundering expedition to Tara, where they took a prize of horses from the east. Conall, hearing the shouts, pursued them with the small number of men who were near him at the time. He did not stop following them until he came to Dun-Conaing on Magh-Rein—which is Fenagh <note>original: Fidhnocha</note> at this day. And the old tribes of Magh-Slecht <note>original: Tuatha-Slecht</note> killed him because he was unarmed; that would not have been a "hand of slaughter" <note>original: lam an anrgei; a proverbial expression for a successful killing or a fatal opportunity</note> for them if luck had not willed it. Or, perhaps it was a flying spear that killed him. But whichever of these was the manner of his death, it was the Masraidhe who committed the deed.

<margin>The O'Canannans held power before the Clann-Dalaigh rose to prominence.</margin>

<summary>This page details the noble lineages of the Cinel Conaill who owe tribute to Saint Caillin and extols the military prowess of their ancestor, Conall Gulban, before describing his death at the hands of the Masraidhe at Fenagh.</summary>
<keywords>Conall Gulban, Clann-Dalaigh, Tara, Niall of the Nine Hostages, Ulster, Magh-Slecht, Fenagh, Masraidhe, Irish genealogy, medieval tribute</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the hagiographical account of Saint Caillin and his interactions with the descendants of Conall Gulban, specifically focusing on the resurrection and baptism of Conall.</meta>

<page-num>140</page-num>

In short, the stone and the grave <note>original: *lia ocur a lecht*</note> of Conall were placed on Magh Rein at Dun Baile. <note>The place where Conall Gulban was first interred is not now known to tradition, unless the remarkable Dolmen <term>a prehistoric stone tomb</term> at Fenagh, represented in the illustration to this volume, may mark the site.</note>

Five and a half years after the death of Conall, Caillin arrived at that place. He was making a circuit of that beautiful district <margin>Folio 15, a 2.</margin> until he found the grave of Conall, the battle-valiant. It was revealed to him how Conall had died and that his soul was in punishment <note>original: *i pen*; likely referring to Purgatory or a state of being unbaptized</note> afterward.

Caillin was greatly grieved and sorrowful because of this. After that, Caillin gathered all the chief saints of Ireland and the clans of Conall from the north. Caillin and the saints of Ireland fasted so that Conall might be resurrected. Thereupon, Conall was raised from death and he related his experiences. They then proceeded to the church. The name of God and of Caillin were magnified through this miracle. He was baptized afterward in the **Bell of the Kings** <note>original: *cluz na riṫ*</note>. This was the only name he had from then on. Caillin and the saints of Ireland then confirmed Conall, and Conall was also anointed afterward.

<margin>The reason for the tribute on the descendants of Conall Gulban.</margin>

Thereupon, Conall ordained a fair tribute <note>original: *cain*</note> from his descendants to Caillin, to his successors, and to his church forever. This is the tribute that was promised there in the presence of the saints of Ireland: namely, the riding horse and the robes of every king, and his colored garment; and the tribute of the people of Tirconnell <note>original: *Conallach*</note> forever. A horse from every female ruler. an ounce of gold or its value from every ruler. Also three handfuls from every townland that supports a king. A *screpall* <note>a small silver coin or unit of value</note> from every sheep-owner. These are to be paid every third year forever.

The blessing of the saints of Ireland and of Conall, and the special blessing of Caillin, <margin>Folio 15, b 1.</margin> be upon the children of Dalach and upon the race of Conall in general for fulfilling the...

<note>The **Bell of the Kings** (*Clog-na-Righ*) was so called from the number of kings baptized out of it. This bell still exists and is preserved in the Roman Catholic Church at Foxfield, near Fenagh, where it is regarded as a sacred relic. It is stated further on that St. Patrick gave this bell to St. Caillin. It is described as circular in form, resembling an inverted globe, being very unlike other Irish bells in shape.</note>

<summary>Saint Caillin discovers the grave of the unbaptized Conall Gulban and, through fasting with the saints of Ireland, resurrects him. Conall is baptized in the "Bell of the Kings" and establishes a perpetual tribute from his descendants to Caillin’s monastery at Fenagh.</summary>

<keywords>Saint Caillin, Conall Gulban, Fenagh, Magh Rein, Clog-na-Righ, Bell of the Kings, resurrection, baptism, tribute, Irish saints</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Saint Caillin and his interactions with the royal house of Conall Gulban.</meta>

Regardless, the stone and grave of Conall were placed on Magh-Rein <note>The Plain of the Portion, in County Leitrim</note>, at Dun-Baile.

Five and a half years after Conall's death, Caillin came to that place. He was making a circuit of that fair land until he found the grave of battle-strong Conall. It was then revealed to him how Conall had died and that he was suffering in torment <note>likely referring to Purgatory</note> afterward. This was sad and grievous to Caillin.

Caillin afterwards assembled the majority of the saints of all Ireland, as well as the children of Conall from the north. Caillin and the saints of Ireland fasted to petition for the resuscitation of Conall. Thereupon, Conall was raised from death and pain by him; he came along with them as far as the church. The name of God and the name of Caillin were magnified by this miracle.

He [Conall] was afterwards baptized out of the *Clog-na-Righ* <note>The Bell of the Kings</note>; and he was one of those from whom the bell's name was derived. Caillin and the saints of Ireland blessed Conall after that, and Conall was subsequently anointed as well.

After these things, Conall ordained a tribute from his descendants to Caillin, and to his *comarb* <term>successor in an ecclesiastical office</term> and congregation forever. <margin>The tribute on the race of Conall Gulban, etc.</margin> This is the tribute that was promised there, in the presence of the saints of Ireland: namely, the yoke and riding horse of every king, his colored mantle, and the *cendaige* <term>likely meaning "customs" or "donations"</term> of the Conallachs <note>the people of the race of Conall</note> forever. A steed was required from every chieftainess. An ounce of gold, or its value, from every chieftain. An *agh tri nglac* <term>literally a "cow of three hands," referring to a cow with fat three hands thick</term> from every *bally* <term>a townland or ancient land division</term> that supplies food-tribute to a king. A *screpall* <term>an ancient Irish silver coin or unit of weight</term> from every sheepfold. All of this was to be paid every third year forever.

The blessing of the saints of Ireland and of Conall, and the everlasting blessing of Caillin, be upon the Clann-Dalaigh <note>the O'Donnells and their kin</note> and the rest of the race of Conall.

<summary>Saint Caillin miraculously resuscitates Conall from the dead to save him from spiritual torment, leading Conall to establish a perpetual tribute from his descendants to the Saint's monastery.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Caillin, Conall Gulban, Magh-Rein, Clog-na-Righ, resurrection, Irish saints, ecclesiastical tribute, Clann-Dalaigh, medieval taxation</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the spiritual laws and prophecies concerning the Cenél Conaill (the descendants of Conall Gulban).</meta>

<page-num>142</page-num>

These are the laws. The curse of the three and the "evil prayer" <note>original: *anopart*; a malediction or negative prayer</note> of the saints of Ireland be upon them if they do not fulfill them.

These are the conditions of those laws: Patrick, the Apostle of Ireland, with his saints, and Michael with the angels of heaven established them. Adamnan confirmed them later when he arrived.

These are the blessings <note>original: *buada*; virtues or victories</note> that Caillin promised them as rewards for fulfilling them: victory in battle before them; success in raiding, in wisdom, and in eloquence; success in inheritance and in teaching; the blessing of beauty and the blessing of craftsmanship. The blessing of nephews <note>original: *gormac*; specifically a sister's son</note>, of youths, and of foster-sons; the blessing of kings and of royal heirs; the blessing of soldiers, of high warriors, and of royal champions. The blessing of children and the blessing of counsel; the blessing of churches and of clerics; the blessing of hospitality; the blessing of smiths. The blessing of fortune and of great wealth. Long life and great prosperity forever to the race of Conall for fulfilling these laws. The strength of a battalion <note>original: *spain chatha*</note> shall be granted to every nine of them, and the strength of nine men to every single individual among them against <note>original: *ag*; literally "with"</note> their enemies.

Furthermore, Caillin prophesied to Conall that a glorious child would be born from him, and that Ireland and Scotland <note>original: *Eri* and *Alban*</note> would be full of his fame—namely, Columba <note>original: *Colum Cille*</note>. He also prophesied concerning Adamnan afterward, and said that he would be the guardian of that later generation.

<margin>Folio 15, b 2.</margin> After this, Conall died. Caillin then buried Conall Gulban with great honor and reverence, accompanied by an assembly of clerics and saints, in that church of Caillin's. Afterward, Columba confirmed that law upon the descendants of Conall.

It was concerning the death and the spiritual revival of Conall, and the prophecy of the kings who would descend from him, that this poem was spoken:

->Caillin sang:<-

->Here is the grave of hardy Conall;<-
->A great power who won victory on every side;<-

***

<summary>This page details the spiritual rewards promised to the Cenél Conaill for adhering to the laws of Saints Patrick, Michael, and Caillin, and records the burial of Conall Gulban and the prophecy of his descendant, Saint Columba.</summary>
<keywords>Cenél Conaill, Saint Caillin, Saint Patrick, Saint Columba (Colum Cille), Conall Gulban, Adamnan, Irish hagiography, prophecies, spiritual laws</keywords>

--- Page 173 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the spiritual and material relationship between Saint Caillin and the Cenel-Conaill (the descendants of Conall Gulban).</meta>

If they observe this tribute, they shall be blessed. However, the perpetual malediction, curse, and rejection <note>original: "reprobation"</note> of the saints shall be upon them unless they observe it. The guarantors of that tribute are Patrick, the apostle of Ireland, along with his saints; Michael with the Angels of Heaven; and Adamnan, later, when he arrived.

The virtues which Caillin left to them as gifts for observing the tribute are: victory in battle; the excellence <note>original: "palm"</note> of learning, wisdom, and eloquence; the excellence of ending and beginning; and the excellence of physical features and form. They shall have the luck of nephews, of servants <note>original: "gillas"</note>, and of foster-children. They shall have the luck of kings and queens; the luck of soldiers, noble heroes, and true warriors; the luck of children and of wise counsel; the luck of churches and clerics; the luck of musicians <note>original: "minstrels"</note>; and the luck of smiths. They shall have the excellence of prosperity and great wealth, length of life, and an eternal reward—all granted to the Cenel-Conaill for observing this tribute. They shall have success in battle for every group of nine <note>original: "ennead"</note> among them, and the power <note>original: "puissance"</note> of nine men shall be in each single man against their enemies.

Caillin also prophesied to Conall afterward that an illustrious descendant should be born from him, and that Scotland <note>original: "Alba"</note> and Ireland would be full of his renown—namely, Colum Cille <note>Saint Columba</note>. He further prophesied the coming of Adamnan after that. He also said that he himself would be the spiritual guardian <note>original: "tutor"</note> of that pair.

It was after this that Conall died. Thereupon, Caillin buried Conall Gulban with great honor and reverence, attended by clerics and saints, in Caillin's own oratory. Colum Cille later confirmed that tribute upon Conall's children.

Regarding the death and resurrection <note>original: "resuscitation"</note> of Conall, and to foretell the kings who would descend from him, this poem was composed <note>original: "said"</note>.

<margin>It is "a chlaen, no comao delióin" <note>A technical linguistic note regarding the Irish meter or phrasing</note>.</margin>
<margin>The text here refers to "Ogma son of Elathan" <note>A figure from Irish mythology associated with speech and writing</note>; the original notes discuss the arrangement of letters and the inversion of "g" for "ag."</margin>

->CAILLIN sang:<-
->Behold here the grave of hardy Conall,<-
->Who often achieved victory on every side;<-

<meta>This page continues a poetic lament or eulogy concerning Conall, son of Niall of the Nine Hostages, and his burial at Dun Conaing.</meta>

Many he put to death,
Many bodies, many hundreds, many spoils.
Two charges of a king who possessed strength,
The fierce lion with the power of a champion;
There was no nation he did not defeat,
With spear, with sword, with shield.
His battle-career was like Tuathal Techtmar <note>original: "Tuathal techt"; a legendary High King of Ireland known for his conquests.</note>,
The king who fell upon the grave where you are;
His heroism was like Cuchulainn <note>original: "Choin Cuailnge"; the Hound of Ulster, the central hero of the Ulster Cycle of Irish mythology.</note>,
Winning victory in every place.
The son of Niall of the Nine Hostages <note>original: "Nell naiziallax"; Niall Noígíallach, the ancestor of the Uí Néill dynasties.</note> of the powers,
Heroic is his grave upon the sea-plain;
Under the protection of the King <note>Referring to God.</note> I place him,
That He may bring his soul out of pain.
Dun Conaing was built today,
By the career of sixty <note>original: "rercat"; sixty.</note> famous kings,
Until Conall son of Niall fell
By the children of the one who struck the mountain peak.
The peak of the mountain until this day,
From the mountain of Conall, head of the harbor;
The protection of Caillin son of Niata;
His soul is without lie until the Day of Judgment.
After death he came strongly
From the east, from Tara <note>original: "Themraig"; the ancient seat of the High Kings of Ireland.</note> of the hosts;
The noble youth, he was a foolish wave,
As he fell by the spear of the people of Magh Slecht <note>original: "Tuathaib Slecht"; a plain in County Cavan associated with the idol Crom Cruach.</note>.

<margin>Folio 16, column a 1.</margin>
<margin>A marginal note states that "Colum Cille composed this song," but it appears from the poem that Caillin should be regarded as the author. A marginal note, nevertheless, has "a conjecture, truly" <note>original: "tomtiron".</note></margin>

<note>1</note> *Tuathal Techtmar*. Written as *Tuath techt* in Manuscript A; (the word *techt* being used for *Techtmar* to avoid a metrical error). Manuscript B has *tuathaib techt*, which is wrong, as the intention of the poet plainly was to compare Conall with Tuathal Techtmar, monarch of Ireland in the first century, from whom Conall was descended in the tenth generation.

<note>2</note> *Cuchulainn of Cuailnge*; that is, the hero Cuchulainn.

<note>3</note> *Sixty*. *Sercat*. Notated as "lx." in Manuscripts A and B.

It discusses his death, his burial at Dun Conaing, and the spiritual protection invoked for his soul.</summary>

He sent multitudes to their deaths—
Great bands, great hundreds, great armies.
Regal was his onslaught, subduing the strong—
The furious lion of heroic might;
There was no clan <note>original: "sept"</note> over which he did not triumph,
With spear, with sword, and with shield.
Like the battle-career of Tuathal Techtmar <note>A legendary High King of Ireland credited with establishing the province of Meath</note>
Was the career of this man over whose grave I stand;
His valor was like that of the Hound of Cuailnge <note>original: "Cu of Cuailnge"; referring to Cú Chulainn, the central hero of the Ulster Cycle</note>,
Triumphing over every land.
The son of mighty Niall of the Nine Hostages—
Heroic is his grave on the Plain of Rein <note>original: "Magh-Rein"</note>.
May the King whom I love, in His mercy,
Deliver his soul from pain.
This place was called Dun-Conaing until today,
During the reigns of sixty prosperous kings,
Until Conall, son of Niall, fell
By the sons of the Liath <margin>⁴ The Liath (genitive: Leth). This person has not been identified.</margin>, across the gap of treachery.
"The Gap of Treachery" <margin>⁵ Berna-in-braith; literally, “gap of treachery.”</margin> was its name <margin>⁶ its name; that is, another name for Fenagh.</margin> until this day,
From the betrayal of Conall, the leader of the host;
The Wood of the Islands <note>original: "Fidhnacha"</note> of Caillin, son of Niata <margin>⁷ Niata. The genitive singular is written *net* here to maintain the poetic meter, though it is usually *niatach* in the rest of the text.</margin>,
Shall be its name, truly, until the Day of Judgment <margin>⁸ Day of Judgment. *Lá m luain*; literally, “the day of the Monday.” Another manuscript provides an alternative reading, *lá in luain*, meaning “the day of the reward.” *La in Luain* is an expression still used for the Day of Judgment. In pursuit of horses he came stoutly,
From the east, from Tara of the flocks,
With only a small company; it was a foolish journey,

A nut-tree has blossomed for the lord,
Being <note>original: "bech"; likely meaning "existing" or "being"</note> without a shield against the thrusts of spears;
Many stars and many weapons
They found in the path of the stars.
I am praying to the King of the Stars,
And chanting psalms over his gravestone,
Together with my noble, beloved city of stars,
Because I love the way he believed.
Under my protection he has gone,
Coming from the north across the grave-mound to me;
He promised that my path would be maintained
According to my desire <note>original: "mian"</note>, whether the time be long or short.
Every victorious child until the Judgment
Is a noble star from him in the northern land;
Every one of them who does not distress my eye,
To them all I shall grant every victory.
I bless Fergus <margin>¹ Fergus, surnamed Cenn-fota, or “long-head,” son of Conall Gulban.</margin> truly,
May he be a co-god with my King in his footsteps;
I bless Setna <margin>² Setna, son of Fergus called Cenn-fota.</margin> of the chariots,
May he be a co-god forever in his right.
Ainmire <margin>³ Ainmire, grandson of Fergus Cenn-fota and monarch of Ireland, A.D. 568-571.</margin> and Lugaid <margin>⁴ Lugaid, brother of Ainmire.</margin> of the ships;
I bless the two heroes of the battles;
From them the sovereignty shall truly descend,
A multitude of kings upon whom there shall be grace.
From him shall be served, as is seen,
Ireland—and the judgment was no lie;
It is four who have followed the Lord,
They shall rule as far as the bare sea beyond.

If they break through every gap <note>original: "briit sach bern," meaning to overcome all opposition</note>;

Of the race of Setna <note>Setna was the son of Fergus Ceannfada and ancestor of the principal families of Tirconnell</note> who followed the path;

Flaithbertach the prosperous, and Mael—
Who were obedient to the saints at all times.

Nine men shall come of the race of Sarb,
Great shall be their fame in the northern land;
Since they do not oppress my church,
They shall be obedient to me, though they are far from me.

When they are not ruling over noble Ireland,
They will not obtain a province, except by battle.
They will not be without a chief of their own;
Nor will they annoy my church—a happy thing.

They are bound yonder to obey me readily.
Contention here shall be theirs, if they practice deceit;
Ruin yonder, unless they practice good.
The colored mantle of each manifest king;
His own yoke-steed, it is known;

If they give it, they will break through every gap; <note>Meaning they will be victorious in breaking through enemy defenses.</note>
And pestilence shall not come into their land.
Five high-kings will not deceive me,
Of the seed of Setna, a proud race.
The five who will not obey my commands,
Shall suffer pain—true is the miracle.
Ainmire, Domhnall of the bands,
The tormentless Aedh, shall be my friends;
Flaithbhertach the faithful, and Mael—
Two who lived after the manner of the saints at all times.
Nine shall come of the seed of Garbh,
Whose fame will be great in the northern land;
Heroes who will not annoy my church,
Who will be submissive to me, though they live a long time after me.

He shall seek me out at the meeting of the paths,
The son of the prince who strikes between two peaks;

***

**Notes on the Text:**

1. **Enechan:** An early O'Donnell chief.
2. **Waterfall of the Kings:** <note>original: Es-na-righ</note> Now known as Assaroe, near Ballyshannon, County Donegal.
3. **Grey-chest:** <note>original: Cliabh-glas</note> Literally "grey-chest," a descriptive epithet.
4. **Domnall:** Domhnall Mór O’Donnell, a significant ruler of Tirconnell.
5. **The Warrior:** The warrior referred to here was evidently Melaghlin O’Donnell, successor to Domhnall Mór. He was the son of Lughaidh, from whom the *Cenel-Luig-Senach* <note>The kindred of Lughaidh Senach</note> descend. He was killed by an army under Maurice FitzGerald in A.D. 1247 at Ath-Senaigh.
6. **The Ford:** Ath-Senaigh, the "Ford of Senach," now the ford at Ballyshannon, County Donegal.
7. **The host from the East:** This refers to the English army.
8. **Red Fort:** <note>original: Rath-ruadh</note> Literally "Red-rath." Possibly the place now called Rarooy, in the parish of Donegal, barony of Tirhugh, County Donegal.
9. **Red-Colored:** <note>original: Derg-daith</note> A nickname. The person alluded to was likely Godfrey O’Donnell, chief of Tirconnell, who died in 1258 from wounds received in battle.

<summary>This page contains a modernized Irish poem prophesying or recording the succession and deaths of the O'Donnell chiefs, specifically mentioning Domhnall Mór and Godfrey O'Donnell. It highlights the conflict with the English ("the host from the East") at Ballyshannon.</summary>
<keywords>O'Donnell dynasty, Tirconnell, Ballyshannon, Assaroe, Irish prophecy, Medieval Ireland, Maurice FitzGerald, Godfrey O'Donnell</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a prophetic poem concerning the future rulers of Tirconnell (Donegal), specifically focusing on the O'Donnell dynasty and their conflicts.</meta>

<page-num>151</page-num>

Enechan <note>original: Enechan; likely Enechan O'Donnell</note> of Es-na-righ <note>original: Es-na-righ; "The Waterfall of the Kings," likely Assaroe at Ballyshannon</note>.
To me, it is known, he will be a true friend.
After him, my church yonder will be abandoned,
Until the prince with the *gray-chest* <note>original: cliabh-glas; a nickname referring to a gray breastplate or coat of mail</note> arrives,
The gray-chested one, the cleaver of bosoms—
Domhnall <note>Domhnall Og O'Donnell, inaugurated in 1258</note>, not violent, is the man's name.
It is not a short, but a long while from my time,
The period during which he obeys me afterward.
After that will come the generous warrior,

His grave shall be at the Ford in the North <note>Referring to the battle at Credran-Cille</note>,
Prepared by the host from the East <note>The English forces</note>.
From Rath-ruadh <note>The Red Fort</note> will come the "Red-Color" <note>original: Derg-daith; a nickname for a fierce warrior</note>,
Who will be lord until the battle of smooth Ros <note>Rosses in County Sligo</note>.

Will be a stout king, this Domhnall who is not mean;
Until he is summoned unto Maelmara,
Few are the friends over whom he does not prevail.
He will obey me in all things—
The hero's son from two-peaked Bernas <note>Barnismore Gap, a famous mountain pass in Donegal</note>;

---

<margin>1. He died at Credran-Cille, in Ross-Cede, in the barony of Carbury, County Sligo, fighting against an English army commanded by Maurice FitzGerald, the Justiciary of Ireland. The *Annals of the Four Masters* (A.D. 1258) relate that when he was almost about to expire, he had himself carried on his bier before his followers during a conflict in which the Cenel-Eoghain (the O'Neills) engaged them.</margin>

<margin>2. Mor O'Donnell, whose son, Domhnall Og, was inaugurated as chief of Tirconnell in 1258, succeeding Godfrey O'Donnell.</margin>

<margin>3. *Domhnall*. Called Domhnall Og, or young Donnell.</margin>

<margin>4. *Ros*. Ros-Cede (pronounced Roskedy). Now the Rosses, in the parish of Drumcliff, barony of Carbury, County Sligo.</margin>

<margin>5. *Donn*. A nickname for Domhnall.</margin>

<margin>6. *Maelmara*. The meaning of this line is unclear.</margin>

<margin>7. *prevails not*. At the end of this line in the manuscript, the scribe adds the note: "I am weary, without food today" <note>original: am triam tain seise hoie</note>.</margin>

**Caillin:**
Believe what I say to you,
O noble, beautiful Angel,
For the meeting is certain.
Believe the true word
To me, do not neglect it,
From the first utterance of the clans.

<margin>folio 17, a 1.</margin>
The seed of Conall, brave in battle,
Since I magnify them,
Let them do my will.
It is well that he is revived,
The brave, heroic one,
Conall, their father;
His soul I have brought,
Truly, out of his pain. <note>original: "fír for a pén." This refers to Caillin's miraculous intervention to save the soul of Conall Gulban from torment.</note>

**The Angel said:** <note>original: "Aingel dixit."</note>
Believe what they have promised you,
O fair, ancient man,
O descendant of curly-haired Caireda, <note>original: "Chainedai." Caireda was an ancestor of Caillin; see the note below regarding his genealogy.</note>
Great is your power.
Tell me the truth,
Which shall never be spoken
Except as a righteous voice.

**Caillin performed [this]:** <note>original: "Caillin fecit."</note>
A *screpall* <term>screpall: a small silver coin or unit of value used in ancient Ireland</term> from every single person,
That is what I am entitled to from them,
Because it is a true law.
Every third righteous year,
As often as I milk them, <note>A metaphor for collecting dues or tributes.</note>
The good horse of the King.

**Caillin**

<meta>This page continues the prophetic dialogue between the Angel and Saint Caillín regarding the spiritual and temporal obligations of the Conmaicne people.</meta>

**The Angel:**
If they do not pay the tribute <note>original: "chain"; a customary ecclesiastical tax or due</note>,
I will bring vengeance upon them;
Of this I am certain.
Their churches shall be destroyed,
Their sanctuaries shall be divided,

If they should violate my rights,
Death shall come upon them,
For it is a true story.
Though they be brave in the heat of battle,
And though their numbers be great,
The prosperity of their king depends on their goodness.

<margin>Folio 17: Concerning the fulfillment of these conditions, Caillín himself sang this in the prophecy.</margin>

He spoke further to the Conmaicne <note>The Conmaicne were a significant tribal group in medieval Ireland, particularly in the area of Leitrim and Longford</note> after the Angel had questioned him in that same dialogue. However, let us pass over that for now until we speak of those Conmaicne in another place hereafter.

It is regarding these matters that Caillín revealed elsewhere—namely, when he sang for the children of Conall <note>The Cenél Conaill</note>, and concerning the raising of that same Conall from death, and the victories they would achieve for fulfilling that tribute, and the misfortunes they would face if they did not pay it, and the guarantees and pledges given to Caillín for its fulfillment—Caillín sang this in the prophecy:

->Caillín sang this:<-

The parched earth shall shake with trembling,
Every land shall be debased after the judgment of deeds;
It is very certain that the Doom <note>original: "brath"; the Last Judgment</note> has come;
The waves of the sea shall rise over everyone.
My Caillín, who tells the truth;
I shall subdue the strength of every king;
Let everyone say, for I am certain of it,
Just as I have restored Conall to life.

<meta>This page continues the poetic record of ecclesiastical tributes and the miraculous deeds of Irish saints, specifically focusing on the resurrection of Conall and the resulting obligations of his descendants.</meta>

After a year and five years,
As the time of my visitation comes,
When I arrived at my church,
Conall was there without life.
I restored to life <note>original: Ṫaṫḃeoġur; revived</note> Conall of the hundreds,
A man who had long been in death;
It was not easy to revive him then,
Since it was long until I had arrived. <note>original: nı hoır; he obtained not/it was not found easy</note>
Conall promised me my tribute;
He promised he would lie by my hand.
His children owe it after him,
A vast tribute to me for my maintenance.
Seven <note>original: Ṡeċt</note> sons had gentle Conall,
They shall pay all my tributes;
This is the tribute they shall promise,
Between house and field.
A *screpall* <note>original: Ṡcrepall; a silver coin or unit of value</note> from every sheep-owner, <note>original: ċaırċıġ; from caera, "a sheep"</note>
I am entitled to from you every productive year;
The horse of the house shall be mine,
If it be given during my visitation.
<margin>folio 17, b 1.</margin> Patrick, the pure servant of Armagh,
Binds all of you by oaths,
To give the tribute to us,
As it was brought to me in ancient times.
The noble, high-born Adamnan,
Wishes all good to you in your lives;
It is he and his bell that I bring,
To bind you in your settlements.

<note>1. Revived: *ṫaṫḃeoġuḋ*, for *ḋo-aṫ-ḃeoġuḋ*.</note>
<note>2. He obtained not: *nı hoır*, a very incorrect form of *nı ḟuaır*, in which the infected (or aspirated) *ḟ* is not sounded in pronunciation.</note>

For five and a half years,
As the rules of nature accord,
Until I arrived at my church,
Conall was without life.
I revived <note>original: "athbeochaig" (revived/reanimated)</note> Conall of the hundreds,
Though he had lain in death for a long time;
He did not obtain his revival, moreover,
No matter how much time passed until I came.

Conall promised me my tribute;
He promised he would swear by my hand.
His children after him owe me
A tribute so prodigious it is difficult to tell.
The mild Conall had seven sons,
Who all promised my dues.
This is the tribute they promised,
Both the kings and the chieftains among them.

A *screpall* <note>original: "screpall"; a silver coin or unit of value in ancient Ireland</note> from every sheep-owner <note>original: "caerach"; literally "of the sheep"</note>
Is due to me every third year,
And the horse of the king who rules over them,
If they wish to live according to my rule.

<margin>Macha. Ard-Macha ("Macha's height"), or Armagh.</margin>
Great, pure Patrick of great Macha,
<margin>Of both worlds. The original Irish *oib bathaib* is likely a misspelling of *bethaib* (the dative and ablative plural of *bith*, meaning "world"), used here to rhyme with the final word of the stanza. In the prose version of this account, the Archangel Michael and the saints of Heaven are also named as sureties.</margin>
And all the Angels of both worlds,
Were given to me as sureties <note>original: "tucao" (given)</note>
To guarantee the payment of that tribute.

The exalted, perfect Adamnan,
And all the nobles of both worlds;
Adamnan himself and his bell, which I gave him,
Were given to me as sureties.

--- Page 192 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic and prose declarations regarding the tributes owed to Saint Caillin and the spiritual consequences of paying or withholding them.</meta>

<page-num>162</page-num>

Three times, with the Black Abbey <note>original: "Dubh-Regles"; likely referring to the oratory at Fenagh or a related foundation in Derry</note>,
Unless they bring my visitation dues;
If those three times pass by,
Until Judgment Day their sorrows will be sufficient.
If they pay my tribute,
They shall have the victory of beloved, host-filled prosperity;
But I will proclaim a sudden madness upon them
Until the victorious Day of Judgment arrives.

The victory of battle, a noble triumph,
The victory of slaughter and the victory of wisdom;
The victory of inheritance, the victory of conquest,
The victory of form and the victory of deed;
The victory of noble sons, the victory of pure youths,
The victory of kings and the victory of royal heirs;
The victory of soldiers, the victory of perfect abbots,
The victory of fierce, swift royal leadership.

I have a choice for them,
Whether they be refusals or payments;
For these shall be their failures <note>original: "dimbuada"</note>,
The failure of commerce and of battle;
The failure of kings and of royal people;
The failure of a king, the failure of a lord;
The failure of kingship over kinsmen;
Along with the failure of battle. *So it is.*

There is even more of that lore which speaks concerning the Conmaicne <note>The descendants of Conmac, a major tribal grouping in ancient Ireland</note>, but I will not touch upon that at this time, until we have explored other matters further.

Furthermore, Columba <note>original: "Colam cilli"</note>—son of Fedlimid, son of Fergus Cennfada, son of Conall Gulban, the primary saint of our island—praised, confirmed, and established these rights.

<margin>The benefits <note>original: "buada"</note> of the Cenel-Conaill if they pay the tribute, and their misfortunes if they do not pay it.</margin>

***

1. **Black Abbey** <note>original: "Dubh-Regles"</note>. "Black Regles" (or Church). Possibly another name for Saint Caillin’s *Duirtech*, or Oratory, at Fenagh. One of Saint Columba’s churches at Derry was also called Dubh-Regles.
2. **Virtues** <note>original: "buada"</note>. In the margin of Manuscript A is the note: "The profits of the Cenel-Conaill if they pay the tribute, and their misfortunes if they do not pay it."
3. **Battle** <note>original: "catha"</note>.

<summary>This page contains a prophetic poem attributed to Saint Caillin detailing the blessings of victory granted to those who pay their tributes and the total social and military failure promised to those who refuse. It also notes the historical endorsement of these rights by Saint Columba.</summary>

<keywords>Saint Caillin, Saint Columba, Fenagh, tributes, Cenel-Conaill, Conmaicne, Irish hagiography, blessings and curses</keywords>

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page continues the prophetic or poetic pronouncements attributed to Saint Colum Cille regarding the consequences of paying or withholding tributes to his monastery.</meta>

<page-num>163</page-num>

Three fasts shall be performed at the *Dubh-Regles* <note>original: Dubh-Regles; the "Black Abbey" or "Black Church" founded by St. Columba in Derry</note>,
Unless they give my tributes.
If they omit these fasts,
Their woes shall be, forever, many.
If they furnish my tributes,
Their profits shall be numerous.
I will leave unto them,
Until the Day of Judgment comes, these virtues:
Success in battle before them;
The prize of knowledge and of wisdom;
The prize of ending, and of beginning;
The prize of figure, and of form;
Luck for nephews; luck for fair servants;
Luck for kings, and luck for queens;
Luck for soldiers, luck for arch-heroes;
Luck for true, fierce warriors.
A choice I give to them;
They may have ill luck, or graces;
For their misfortunes shall be these:
Ill luck in foray and battle;
Ill luck for kings and royal heirs;
Ill luck for men and princes;
Ill luck in holding kingship over the *Gaidhel* <note>original: Gaidhel; the Gaelic people of Ireland and Scotland</note>,
Along with ill luck in battle. <margin>I see that the tribute was not paid; for the ill-luck and misfortunes have come. The saints do not speak falsely. <note>original: "vo cim nan hiocao an cáin or vo čainig na diombuada ocur na miorata. H. deanamn na naomh breag." — A marginal note by the 17th-century scholar Thady O'Roddy.</note></margin>

There is also more in that composition which speaks of the *Conmaicni* <note>An ancient tribal group of Ireland</note>; but I will not touch upon it now, until we attempt something further regarding them.

Colum Cille, moreover—the son of Fedlimidh, son of Fergus Cennfoda, son of Conall Gulban (that is, the chief prophet of Heaven and Earth)—awarded, and...

<summary>This page concludes a poetic prophecy attributed to Saint Colum Cille (Columba), detailing the divine blessings or curses that will befall the people based on their payment of tributes to the church. It also includes a genealogical identification of the Saint and a marginal commentary by Thady O'Roddy noting that the predicted misfortunes have indeed come to pass.</summary>

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the spiritual and legal relationship between Saint Caillín and the descendants of Conall, as well as his connection to Saint Columba (Colum Cille).</meta>

<page-num>164</page-num>

<margin>Folio 17, b 2.</margin>
...of heaven and earth, that tribute shall remain upon the descendants of Conall <note>The Cenél Conaill, the kin of Saint Columba</note> forever, payable to Caillín and to his successor.

This is how that matter came to pass. On one occasion, Columba came to Caillín after he had fought his battles; for Columba fought three battles in Ireland—namely, the battles of the three "Culs" <note>original: "na tri cul"; "Cul" means a back, corner, or remote place</note>—as the poet said:

> The battle of Cul Dremne <note>A place in County Sligo; the battle took place in A.D. 561</note> of the crowds,
> The men of Ireland heard of it;
> The battle of Cul Feada <note>Likely in Meath</note> of the great slaughter,
> And the battle of Cul Rathain <note>Coleraine</note>.

Now, Columba came after fighting one of those battles to the place where the precious stone and the chosen holy man was—the lamp shining with feasts and miracles, and the ever-living fire of perfect wisdom—namely, Caillín, son of Niata. He came to Fenagh of Moy Rein to seek forgiveness for his soul. It was with Caillín that he most desired to find release from his transgressions and sins. This was fitting and appropriate for him, for Caillín was the <term>chief elder</term> <note>original: "sruithe"; a term for a senior venerable cleric</note> of Ireland at that time; and he was the tutor and the soul-friend <note>original: "anmchara"; a spiritual director or confessor</note> to Columba. Columba then confessed his transgressions to Caillín, until Caillín gave him forgiveness and pardon.

<margin>Folio 18, b 1.</margin>
After that, Columba gave to Caillín the right of visitation and the maintenance of his monks and his attendants throughout Scotland <note>original: "Albain"</note> and Ireland, and a third of Columba's own visitation rights throughout Ireland and Scotland. Furthermore, Columba ordained the first-fruits of his churches for Caillín; and Columba ordained and left to Caillín those good gifts and the tributes which we have mentioned before.

<note>1. Poet: "porta" in manuscript A.</note>
<note>2. Cul-Dremne: The name (now obsolete) of some place in the barony of Carbury, Co. Sligo—between Sligo and Drumcliff. The battle was fought A.D. 561.</note>
<note>3. Cul-Feada: Dr. Reeves identifies this place with Bealagh-Dathi, where a battle was fought in 587 between the Northern and Southern Hy-Neill. Tuath-Dathi was the name of a district in Bregia, or Meath, with which the name of Bealagh-Dathi may be connected. But as St. Columba was not in Ireland in 587, he could hardly have taken part in the battle of Bealagh-Dathi. In the authority which Dr. Reeves quotes, namely the Preface to the Altus Prosator in the Lebar Brecc, Cul-Feadha (or Bealagh-Feadha, as it is there called) is mentioned.</note>

<summary>Saint Columba (Colum Cille) visits Saint Caillín at Fenagh to seek penance for the blood shed in his three great battles. In gratitude for spiritual absolution, Columba grants Caillín significant ecclesiastical tributes and rights across Ireland and Scotland.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Columba, Saint Caillín, Fenagh, Cul Dremne, penance, soul-friend, Cenél Conaill, ecclesiastical tribute</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the spiritual and legal relationship between Saint Caillin and Saint Colum Cille, specifically regarding the tributes owed by the clans of Conall.</meta>

<page-num>165</page-num>

...confirmed, and left that tribute on Conall's clans forever, to Caillin and his successor.

This is the way it happened. One time Colum Cille <note>Saint Columba</note> came to Caillin, specifically after he had gained three battles; for Colum gained three battles in Ireland, namely, the battles of the three *Culs*, as the poet said <note>original: "ut poeta dixit"</note>:

->The battle of Cul-Dremne <note>A site near Sligo where a famous battle occurred in 561 AD</note> of the conflicts<-
->The men of Ireland have heard;<-
->The battle of Cul-Feada, this is true,<-
->And the battle of Cul-Rathain <note>Coleraine</note>.<-

Colum came, therefore, after fighting one of these battles, to the place where the precious stone was—the true, manifest saint, the lamp shining with wonders and miracles, and the perpetual fire with life-giving heat—namely, Caillin son of Niata, at Fenagh <note>original: "Fídhnacha"</note> of Magh-Rein, to implore forgiveness for his soul. It was with Caillin, he thought, that his absolution from his transgressions and pride lay. This was right and becoming for him, for Caillin was the senior of the saints of Ireland; he was also the chief legate and archbishop of Ireland, and it was he who was the tutor and soul-friend <note>original: "anmchara"; a spiritual director or confessor</note> to Colum himself. He afterwards confessed his transgressions to Caillin, who gave him pardon and forgiveness.

It was after this that Colum Cille gave Caillin the <term>visitation fees</term> <note>original: "cuairt"; dues paid to a church leader during an official tour</note> and <term>provisions</term> <note>original: "caithem"; the right to be fed and housed</note> of his monks and youths both in Scotland <note>original: "Alba"</note> and Ireland, along with a third of Colum Cille's own fees in both Ireland and Scotland. And he ordered land for every church to be given to Caillin from Colum Cille. And Colum Cille ordained, and left to Caillin, the good conditions and tributes we...

<margin>The battle of Cul-Feada (as it is called) is said to be near Clonard <note>original: "Cluain-Iraird"</note> in Meath. The battle of Cul-Feada was evidently fought before 563 AD, the date of Saint Colum Cille's departure from Ireland, since it is assigned as one of the causes of his leaving.</margin>

<margin>Cul-Rathain: Coleraine, County Londonderry.</margin>

<margin>Caillin: Other authorities say that Saint Molaise of Inishmurry, off the coast of Sligo (or Saint Molaise of Devenish, as O'Donnell states in his Irish life of the patron saint of his clan), was the person to whom Saint Colum Cille applied for counsel.</margin>

<summary>This page describes Saint Colum Cille seeking spiritual absolution from Saint Caillin of Fenagh following three major battles, resulting in Colum Cille granting Caillin extensive ecclesiastical rights and tributes across Ireland and Scotland.</summary>

<keywords>Saint Caillin, Saint Colum Cille, Fenagh, Cul-Dremne, soul-friend, ecclesiastical tribute, Irish saints, absolution</keywords>

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the spiritual and legal alliance between Saint Columba (Colum Cille) and Saint Caillín, specifically regarding the tributes owed by the descendants of Conall Gulban.</meta>

<page-num>166</page-num>

among the descendants of Conall Gulban <note>original: *clannaib Conaill Gulbain*; the O'Donnells and related families of Tír Chonaill</note>, and half a portion from every guest house, and a share for every person before every feast and every assembly.

Afterward, Columba gave to Caillín the four books and the *Cathach* <term>The "Battler"; a famous 6th-century psalter and reliquary</term> which he had written with his own hand. He promised many victorious virtues and triumphs to the monks and the people of Caillín forever, through those relics—namely, the *Cathach* and the four books. Columba also declared that neither the people nor the lineage of any of the descendants of Conall who should oppose that tribute would ever prosper.

Columba bestowed excellent blessings upon Caillín, his successors, his community, and his church: namely, the gift of wise abbots, the gift of capable deputies <note>original: *tanairi*; tanists or successors-in-waiting</note>, the gift of protection and sanctuary, the gift of devotion and faith, the gift of hospitality, generosity, and providing food, and the gift of prosperity and...

<summary>Saint Columba grants his hand-written books and the sacred Cathach relic to Saint Caillín, establishing a system of tribute and spiritual blessings for Caillín's monastery at Fenagh.</summary>

--- Page 197 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the spiritual and legal relationship between Saint Colum Cille (Columba) and Saint Caillin of Fenagh, specifically regarding the tributes owed by the descendants of Conall Gulban and the miraculous relics bestowed upon the monastery.</meta>

<page-num>167</page-num>
I have mentioned before, on the children of Conall Gulban <note>Conall Gulban (died c. 464) was an Irish prince and founder of the kingdom of Tír Chonaill; he was the ancestor of the O'Donnell dynasty</note>, and the door-post of every drinking house, and the portion of any five to be placed before every couch.

<margin>This arose from a dispute regarding it. It was always regarded with veneration by the clans of Tirconnell, particularly the O'Donnells; and was usually carried three times, clockwise <note>original: "right-wise"</note>, around their armies when going to battle. See the several virtues ascribed to this relic (the name of which signifies "The Battler" <note>original: "preliator"</note>), in Reeves's *Adamnan*, pages 233, 249, 320, etc. The statement in the text, that it was given by Saint Colum Cille to Caillin, is doubtless an invention, inasmuch as it seems to have been always preserved in County Donegal (ibid. 284).</margin>
Colum Cille afterwards gave to Caillin the *Cethir-lebor* <note>The "Four Books," likely referring to the Four Gospels</note>, and the *Cathach* <note>The "Battler," a famous 6th-century Irish psalter traditionally attributed to St. Columba</note> which he wrote with his own hand. He promised him that those relics would be ensigns of victory and triumph to the monks and people of Caillin until the Day of Judgment <note>original: "doom"</note>—namely, the *Cathach* and the *Cethir-lebor*. And Colum Cille declared that whosoever of Conall's children should oppose that tribute would obtain neither territory nor tribe.

Colum Cille bequeathed good gifts <note>A marginal note in Manuscript A reads: "Colum Cille's 'graces' (victories) to the Successors <note>original: "Comarbs"</note> of Fenagh, etc."</note> to Caillin, and to his successors, and to his community and city: namely, the excellence <note>original: "palm"</note> of abbots; the excellence of tanists <note>Heirs-apparent or abbots-elect</note>; the excellence of power and protection; the excellence of devotion and faith; the excellence of generosity, guest-ship, and hospitality; the excellence of happiness and prosperity; the excellence of covenant and counsel, and of every affair besides. Colum Cille promised Heaven to everyone who would do Caillin's bidding. He promised hell and shortness of life to every man who would profane Fenagh-Caillin <note>original: "Fidnacha-Chaillin"</note> forever.

Colum Cille foretold that an abbot would come in Fenagh who would levy this tribute. He sang this poem <note>original: "lay"</note>:

->After the battle I come<-
->To you, Caillin of miracles.<-
->Your protection I implore<-
->Against the demons of the angry world.<-

<summary>Saint Colum Cille bestows the miraculous Cathach and other relics upon Saint Caillin, establishing the spiritual privileges and the dire consequences for those who refuse to pay tributes to the monastery of Fenagh.</summary>
<keywords>Colum Cille, Saint Caillin, Cathach, Fenagh (Fidnacha), Conall Gulban, O'Donnells, Irish relics, monastic tributes</keywords>

--- Page 198 ---
<meta>This page continues a poetic dialogue between Saint Caillín and Saint Colum Cille (Columba) regarding their spiritual bond and the exchange of ecclesiastical privileges.</meta>

<page-num>168</page-num>

<margin>Caillín</margin>
You are my confessor <note>original: "anmchara" - literally "soul-friend"</note> and my teacher,
You are the purest person on earth;
I lift my red hand <note>original: "lam derg" - likely a reference to being blood-stained from battle</note> to you,
To ask for your peace <note>original: "t'oruio" - for your protection or blessing</note> for my soul.
My blessing upon your sweet mouth,
Great is the good you speak to us.
I will ward off the judgment of battle <note>original: "breth chatha" - a reference to the penance or sentence imposed on Colum Cille after the Battle of Cúl Dreimne</note> from you,
O Colum, of fair grace.

<margin>Colum Cille</margin>
I give to you in return for that,
O high legate of Ireland,
The tributes <note>original: "cuarta" - visitation dues or monastic taxes</note> of my monks at their homes
Both in Ireland and in Scotland.
And a full third of my tribute,
Between Scotland and Ireland,
I give to you with clear tidings,
O noble, sweet-voiced Caillín.
O Caillín, O high legate,
O high prophet of my heart,
I give to you in truth,
Great land for every church.
Yours shall be the gold of every drinking house,
And a share of food on Wednesdays;
The horn of every temple as well,
For your monks forever.
And I have granted you the *Cathach* <note>The "Battler" - a famous 6th-century Irish psalter associated with Colum Cille</note>;
And the four books likewise;

<note>The original notes explain that "red hand" refers to being blood-stained; "peace" (t'oruio) refers to spiritual protection; and "judgment of battle" refers to the sentence pronounced on St. Colum Cille following his participation in battles (such as Cúl Dreimne), which led to his departure from Ireland to Iona. See Reeves's *Adamnan*, Int. lxxiv.</note>

<summary>This page features a poetic exchange where Saint Caillín offers spiritual intercession for Saint Colum Cille, who in turn grants Caillín significant ecclesiastical tributes, lands, and the custody of the sacred Cathach manuscript.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a poetic dialogue between Saint Caillin and Saint Colum Cille (Columba) regarding their spiritual bond and the mutual exchange of ecclesiastical rights and tributes.</meta>

<page-num>169</page-num>

You are my tutor and my soul-friend;
You are the purest person on the earth.
I bring my red hand <note>original: "red hand"; likely referring to a hand stained by sin or violence, seeking absolution</note> to you,
Seeking your peace for my soul.

**Caillin.** My blessing on your sweet mouth!
You say much good to us.
I will protect you from the judgment of battle,
O, Colum of the mild graces.

**Colum Cille.** I will give to you in return,
You high-legate <note>term: arch-legate; a high-ranking representative of the Pope or a supreme ecclesiastical authority</note> of Ireland,
The tributes of my monks at home,
Both in Ireland and Alba <note>term: Alba; the Gaelic name for Scotland</note>.

And a full third of my own tribute,
Both in Alba and Ireland,
I give to you, by the Gospel <note>The original manuscript has *gim* or *ginn*, the meaning of which is uncertain, but it likely refers to a formal oath on the scriptures</note>,
You true, illustrious Caillin.

O, Caillin; O, high-legate;
You archbishop of my heart,
To you I give, with truthfulness,
The broad land of every church.

One door-post <note>original: *let opra*; literally "half door-post," an idiom meaning one of a pair</note> of every tavern,
The rations of five people immediately,
A drinking-horn for every pillow <note>note: or "couch"</note> also,
Your monks shall bravely have.

And the *Cathach* <note>term: Cathach; "The Battler," a famous 6th-century Irish psalter traditionally attributed to St. Columba</note> which I wrote;
The "Four Books" <note>note: The four Gospels</note> besides;
